Written somewhat just for myself, as I make a slightly more serious (than in the past) reading around this subject.

\tableofcontents

\section{\texorpdfstring{$C^*$}{CStar}-Categories}

There are lots of nice sources, so I won't make detailed notes here.  The original paper seems to be Ghez, Lima, and Roberts, \cite{GLR_Wstar_categories}.  Mitchener's paper \cite{mitchener_cstar_cats} is also very readable.  A much more category-theory treatment is given in \cite{Bunke_NonUnitalCstarCats}; a more readable category-theory take is \cite{DellAmbrogio_unitary_monoidal}.


\subsection{Definitions}

As categories always have unit morphisms, this means all $C^*$-algebras have units, which is not very natural.  There are no major complications with just looking at ``categories'' where we don't suppose identity morphisms, and functors which don't need to preserve units.  See Ferrier's thesis \cite{ferrier_thesis} and Mitchener's paper \cite{mitchener_cstar_cats}.

We follow the axioms in \cite{GLR_Wstar_categories}.  A $C^*$-Category is a category $\mc A$ such that:
\begin{enumerate}[(1)]
  \item\label{cstar-cat-axioms:1} each morphism space $\mc A(A,B) = \hom(A,B)$ is a complex vector space where composition of morphisms is bilinear;
  \item\label{cstar-cat-axioms:2} there is an involutive antilinear contravariant endofunctor $*$ of $\mc A$; denote the image of $x\in\mc A(A,B)$ by $x^*\in\mc A(B,A)$;
  \item\label{cstar-cat-axioms:3} for each $x\in\mc A(A,B)$ there is $a\in\mc A(A,A)$ with $x^*x = a^*a$, that is, $x^*x$ is positive; and $x^*x=0\implies x=0$;
  \item\label{cstar-cat-axioms:4} each $\mc A(A,B)$ is a Banach space, and composition of morphisms is contractive: $\|xy\| \leq \|x\| \|y\|$;
  \item\label{cstar-cat-axioms:5} for each $x\in\mc A(A,B)$ we have $\|x^*x\| = \|x\|^2$;
\end{enumerate}

\ref{cstar-cat-axioms:1} means that $\mc A(A,A) = \hom(A)$ is an algebra.
In \ref{cstar-cat-axioms:2}, an ``endofunctor'' just means a functor from $\mc A$ to itself; contravariant means we reverse the directions of arrows, as expected.
So $\hom(A)$ is a $*$-algebra, and by \ref{cstar-cat-axioms:4} and \ref{cstar-cat-axioms:5} is a $C^*$-algebra.  (Note then that the final part of \ref{cstar-cat-axioms:3} is automatic: if $x^*x=0$ then $\|x\|=0$ so $x=0$, by axionm \ref{cstar-cat-axioms:5}.  Also, implicit in axiom \ref{cstar-cat-axioms:4} is that $\mc A$ is locally small.)

The axioms corresponding to the $C^*$-condition are more complicated in \cite[Definition 1.2.10]{ferrier_thesis}, but I think this is because Ferrier wishes to consider real-$C^*$-algebras, whereas we work only with the complex numbers; compare \cite[Definition~2.8]{mitchener_cstar_cats}.
Axiom \ref{cstar-cat-axioms:3} appears surprising, but is necessary, see \cite[Example~2.10]{mitchener_cstar_cats}.
The concept of a (possibly non-unital) \emph{algebraoid} is revelant here, \cite{ferrier_thesis,mitchener_cstar_cats}.

\begin{example}
Let $\catHilb$ be the category where objects are Hilbert spaces, and $\hom(H,K)$ is the space of all bounded linear maps from $H$ to $K$, equipped with the usual involution.  This is readily seen to be a $C^*$-Category.  More generally, we can consider any sub-category $\mc A$ with $\mc A(H,K)$ closed for each $H,K$ in $\mc A$.  A natural (from the point of view of functional analysis) subcategory is where we restrict to compact operators: this is then a non-unital $C^*$-Category.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:to_modules}
Every morphism space $\mc A(A,B)$ is a Hilbert $C^*$-module over the $C^*$-algebra $\mc A(A)$.  See \cite{Lance_HilbModsBook} or \cite{RaeburnWilliams_MoritaEquivBook} for good textbooks on Hilbert $C^*$-modules (the later has an unlikely title, but the initial chapters form a very nice introduction to aspects of the theory which Lance's book does not much touch upon).

However, here we have more symmetry, and so naturally also $\mc A(A,B)$ is a \emph{left} Hilbert $C^*$-module over $\mc A(B)$.  Indeed, we end up with a structure reminiscent of an \emph{imprimitivity bimodule}, \cite[Definition~3.1]{RaeburnWilliams_MoritaEquivBook}, excepting that $\mc A(A,B)$ need not be a \emph{full} Hilbert module (it could easily be $\{0\}$ for example!)  See Appendix~\ref{sec:bimod} for more on this viewpoint.
\end{remark}

\begin{example}\label{eg:HilbMods}
Hilbert $C^*$-modules yield another example.  Fixing a $C^*$-algebra $A$, we can consider $\catHCStar{A}$ the category of all right Hilbert $C^*$-modules over $A$, with morphisms as adjointable maps.  Again, there is a non-unital subcategory formed by consider just the compact operators.  One subtle point here: given $t \in \mc L(E,F)$, it is not obvious to use that $t^*t$ is positive in the $C^*$-algebra $\mc L(E)$.  This can be shown directly, see \cite[Lemma~4.1]{Lance_HilbModsBook}, or by using various representation results for Hilbert modules (ultimately reducing the problem to Hilbert space operators: we have not checked that this does not lead to a circular argument, however).
\end{example}

\begin{example}\label{eg:RepA}
Let $A$ be a $C^*$-algebra, and let $\Rep(A)$ be the representation category of $A$.  So objects of $\Rep(A)$ and pairs $(\pi,H_\pi)$ where $H_\pi$ is a Hilbert space, and $\pi\colon A\to\mc B(H_\pi)$ is a $*$-homomorphism.  It is natural to require that $\pi$ is non-degenerate, $\lin\{ \pi(a)\xi : a\in A, \xi\in H_\pi \}$ is dense in $H_\pi$.  Morphisms $x \colon (\pi_1,H_{\pi_1}) \to (\pi_2,H_{\pi_2})$ are intertwiners, bounded linear maps $x\colon H_{\pi_1}\to H_{\pi_2}$ with $x \pi_1(a) = \pi_2(a) x$ for each $a\in A$.  So $\hom(\pi) = \pi(A)'$ the commutant.  Then $\Rep(A)$ is a subcategory of $\catHilb$, and it is easy to check that $\Rep(A)$ is a $C^*$-Category.

The same remarks apply to unitary representations of a group $G$; or use $A = C^*(G)$.
\end{example}

One can define ideals and quotients, see \cite[Definition~1.6]{GLR_Wstar_categories}, \cite[Section~4]{mitchener_cstar_cats}.  There is a sensible notion of unitisation, \cite[Section~3]{mitchener_cstar_cats}.

\begin{remark}\label{rem:mults}
When dealing with the non-unital case, perhaps the theory of \emph{multipliers} is more general and useful than simply using unitisations.  There is a summary of a suitable multiplier construction in \cite[Section~2]{AV_Bicolimits_Cstar_Cats}, but see that paper for citations to some of the original literature.  At present I have not explored this for myself.
\end{remark}

% The following appears non-standard, but we find it useful.

% \begin{definition}
% Given a $C^*$-category $\mc A$, for any object $A\in\mc A$ as $\mc A(A,A)$ is a $C^*$-algebra, we shall identify $A$ with $\mc A(A,A)$ and simply write that $A$ is a $C^*$-algebra.  We continue to write $\mc A(A,B)$ for the morphism space between two (possibly equal, possibly different) objects.
% \end{definition}


\subsection{Functors}

A functor between $C^*$-categories is linear and $*$-preserving on morphism spaces.  As for $C^*$-algebras, a functor is automatically contractive, \cite[Proposition~2.14]{mitchener_cstar_cats}.

A \emph{representation} of a $C^*$-Category $\mc A$ is a functor $F\colon\mc A \to \catHilb$.

\begin{lemma}
Let $F\colon\mc A \to \catHilb$ be a representation.  For each object $A$ let $H_A$ be the closed linear span of $\{ F(a)\xi : a\in A, \xi\in F(A) \}$ inside the Hilbert space $F(A)$.  For every $A,B$ and $x\in\mc A(A,B)$ we have that $F(x)$ restricts to a map $H_A \to H_B$, and so replacing $F(A)$ by $H_A$ for all $A$, we may suppose that $F$ is non-degenerate.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $x\in\mc A(A,B)$ and consider $\xi_1 = F(a)\xi \in H_A$.  We wish to show that $F(x)\xi_1 \in H_B$, or equivalently, if $\eta\in H_B^\perp \subseteq F(B)$, then $0 = (\eta|F(x)\xi_1) = (F(x^*)\eta | F(a)\xi)$.  There is $b\in B$ with $b^*b = xx^*$, and then as $H_B^\perp = \{ \eta : F(c)\eta=0 \ (c\in B)\}$, we see that $F(b^*b)\eta=0$ so $F(xx^*)\eta=0$ so $F(x^*)\eta=0$ because $\ker F(x^*) = \ker F(x)^* = \ker F(x) F(x)^* = \ker F(xx^*)$.  This shows the claim.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:exists_faithful_rep}
For any $\mc A$ there is a faithful (and hence isometric) representation.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
See \cite[Propositions~1.9, 1.14]{GLR_Wstar_categories} or \cite[Section~6]{mitchener_cstar_cats}.  The idea is to mirror the GNS construction: for any state $\mu$ on $A$, where $A$ is for now a fixed object in $\mc A$, we find a cyclic representation which recovers $\mu$.  Then take a direct sum over all $\mu$, and then over all $A$.  This assumes that $\mc A$ is small, in the category theory sense.

We sketch an alternative argument using the interior tensor product, \cite[Chapter~4]{Lance_HilbModsBook}.  Choose a faithful (non-degenerate) representation $\pi\colon\mc A(A,A) \to \mc B(H)$, so for any $B$ we can form $\mc A(A,B) \otimes H$, a Hilbert space, this being the seperation--completion of the algebraic tensor product for the pre-inner-product
\[ (x_1\otimes\xi_1|x_2\otimes\xi_2) = (\xi_1|\pi(x_1^*x_2)\xi_2), \]
which makes sense as $x_1^*x_2\in\mc A(A,A)$.  We need to show that this is positive.  Given $(x_i)_{i=1}^n \subseteq \mc A(A,B)$ we claim that the matrix $(x_i^*x_j)\in M_n(\mc A(A,A))$ is positive.  By \cite[Lemma~IV.3.2]{TakesakiI} this is if and only if $\sum_{i,j} a_i^* x_i^* x_j a_j \in \mc A(A,A)$ is positive, for any $(a_i)_{i=1}^n \subseteq \mc A(A,A)$.  However, setting $y = \sum_j x_j a_j \in \mc A(A,B)$, we see that this sum is $y^*y$ which by the axiom is positive in $\mc A(A,A)$.  Compare also \cite[Lemma~4.2]{Lance_HilbModsBook}.  Then $(\pi(x_i^*x_j)) \in M_n(\mc B(H)) \cong \mc B(H^n)$ is positive, and it now follows that
\[ \sum_{i,j} ( x_i\otimes\xi_i | x_j\otimes\xi_j ) = \sum_{i,j} ( \xi_i | \pi(x_i^*x_j) \xi_j ) \geq 0, \]
as needed.

Given any $C$ and $y\in\mc A(B,C)$, with $(x_i)$ as above, consider $(yx_i) \subseteq \mc A(A,C)$.  Exactly the same argument shows that $(x_i^*y^*yx_j) \leq \|y\|^2 (x_i^*x_j)$ in $M_n(\mc A(A,A))$, from which it follows that we have a contractive map $F(y) : \mc A(A,B) \otimes H \to \mc A(A,C) \otimes H; x\otimes \xi\mapsto yx\otimes\xi$.  In this way we obtain a functor $F \colon \mc A \to \catHilb$ given by $F(B) = \mc A(A,B)\otimes H$, and $F$ acts on morphisms as just described.  It is easy to see that this is a $*$-functor.  As $F(A) = \mc A(A,A)\otimes H \cong H$ for the map $x\otimes\xi \cong \pi(x)\xi$, so that $F(x) \cong \pi(x)$ for $x\in\mc A(A,A)$, we see that $F$ is isometric on $\mc A(A,A)$.  We now take a direct sum over all $A$.
\end{proof}

Recall if needed the notion of a natural transformation from Definition~\ref{defn:natural_transformation}.  Notice that if $\alpha\colon F\Rightarrow G$ then $\alpha^*$, which has components $(\alpha^*)_A = (\alpha_A)^*$, is a natural transformation $G\Rightarrow F$.  We often suppose that $\alpha \colon F \Rightarrow G$ is \emph{bounded}, meaning that $\sup_A \|\alpha_A\| < \infty$.  Define $\alpha$ to be \emph{unitary} if each component $\alpha_A$ is a unitary.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:functor_cat}
Let $\mc A, \mc B$ be $C^*$-categories, and let $(\mc A, \mc B)$ be the functor category: objects are functors $F\colon \mc A \to \mc B$ and morphisms are bounded natural transformations.  Then $(\mc A, \mc B)$ is a $C^*$-category.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For $\alpha \colon F \Rightarrow G$, we of course define $\|\alpha\| = \sup_A \|\alpha_A\|$; then composition is contractive.  Consider $\alpha^*\alpha$ which has components $(\alpha^*\alpha)_A = \alpha_A^* \alpha_A \colon F(A) \to F(A)$.  Then $\|\alpha\|^2 = \sup_A \|\alpha_A\|^2 = \sup_A \| (\alpha^*\alpha)_A \| = \|\alpha^*\alpha\|$.  All of the axioms are now clear, except to show that $\alpha^*\alpha$ is positive: this entails finding $\beta \colon F \Rightarrow F$ with $\beta_A^*\beta_A = \alpha_A^*\alpha_A$ for each $A$.

I don't see that this is immediate%
\footnote{My problem is the following.  A natural transformation $\beta \colon F \Rightarrow F$ is a bounded family $(\beta_A)$ with $\beta_B \circ F(x) = F(x) \circ \beta_A$ for every $x\colon A\to B$.  So it is not enough to choose each $\beta_A$ independently, which, axiom of choice issues aside, can always be done by the $C^*$-category axioms: there is some $\beta_A$ with $\beta_A^*\beta_A = \alpha_A^*\alpha_A$.  Instead, we need such a choice to be ``coherent''.  The second argument in the proof now seems to solve this.}%
, and I have not found a reference which considers this.  Here is one argument, which works if $\mc A$ is small.  Consider the $C^*$-algebra
\[ A_0 = \ell^\infty - \bigoplus_{A\in\mc A} \mc B(F(A), F(A)). \]
The map sending $\beta\colon F \Rightarrow F$ to $(\beta_A)_{A\in\mc A} \in A_0$ is an injective $*$-homomorphism.  By the positivity axiom for $\mc B$, we see that $\alpha^*\alpha$ is positive in $A_0$; that is, for each $A$ we can find $\gamma_A \in \mc B(F(A), F(A))$ with $\gamma_A^*\gamma_A = \alpha_A^*\alpha_A$.  As positivity passes to subalgebras, we conclude that $\alpha^*\alpha$ is positive in the smaller algebra formed by natural transformations $F\Rightarrow F$, as required.

Here is another argument, which gives a concrete receipe for finding $\beta$.  Given $\alpha\colon F\Rightarrow G$, for each $A\in\mc A$ by the $C^*$-category axioms, $\alpha_A^*\alpha_A$ is positive in $\mc B(F(A))$ and so $\beta_A = |\alpha_A| = (\alpha_A^*\alpha_A)^{1/2}$ is well-defined.  Now let $A,B\in\mc A$.  We jump ahead and use the algebra $M(F(A),F(B))$ considered below in Section~\ref{sec:alg_of_cat}.  For each $x\in\mc A(A,B)$, by naturality, $F(x) \alpha_A^*\alpha_A = \alpha_B^*\alpha_B F(x)$ by naturality, so in $M(F(A), F(B))$ we have
\[ \smattwo{0}{0}{F(x)}{0} \smattwo{\alpha_A^*\alpha_A}{0}{0}{\alpha_B^*\alpha_B} = 
\smattwo{\alpha_A^*\alpha_A}{0}{0}{\alpha_B^*\alpha_B} \smattwo{0}{0}{F(x)}{0} \qquad(x\in\mc A(A,B)). \]
By uniqueness of square-roots, $\smattwo{\alpha_A^*\alpha_A}{0}{0}{\alpha_B^*\alpha_B}^{1/2} = \smattwo{\beta_A}{0}{0}{\beta_B}$ so by the continuous functional calculus, also 
\[ \smattwo{0}{0}{F(x)}{0} \smattwo{\beta_A}{0}{0}{\beta_B} = 
\smattwo{\beta_A}{0}{0}{\beta_B} \smattwo{0}{0}{F(x)}{0} \qquad(x\in\mc A(A,B)). \]
Thus $F(x) \beta_A = \beta_B F(x)$ for each $x$, which shows that $\beta \colon F \Rightarrow F$, as we want.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
Let $A$ be a $C^*$-algebra, identified with a one-object $C^*$-category.  Then $(A, \catHilb)$ is isomorphic to $\Rep(A)$, Example~\ref{eg:RepA}.  Indeed, a functor $F\colon A\to\catHilb$ is just a $*$-homomorphism $\pi\colon A\to\mc B(H)$ for some Hilbert space $H$, and we see that $\alpha\colon F\Rightarrow G$ is just an intertwiner between the associated representations.
\end{example}

\begin{example}
Let $A$ be a $C^*$-algebra and let $H\colon \Rep(A) \to \catHilb$ be the evaluation functor: so $H$ sends a representation $\pi\colon A\to\mc B(H_\pi)$ to the Hilbert space $H_\pi$, and is the identity on morphisms.  Consider $\hom(H,H)$ the algebra of bounded natural transformations $\alpha \colon H \Rightarrow H$.  The components of such an $\alpha$ satisfy that $x \alpha_{\pi_1} = \alpha_{\pi_2} x$ for each intertwiner $x\in\hom(\pi_1,\pi_2)$, where of course $\alpha_{\pi} \colon H_\pi \to H_\pi$ is a bounded linear map.

For $x\in\hom(\pi) = \pi(A)'$ we hence have $x \alpha_\pi = \alpha_\pi x$, that is, $\alpha_\pi \in \pi(A)''$.  By the universal property of the enveloping von Neumann algebra $A^{**}$, \cite[Lemma~III.2.2]{TakesakiI} for example, there is a unique normal $*$-homomorphism $\lambda_\pi \colon A^{**} \to \pi(A)''$, for each $\pi\in\Rep(A)$.  Indeed, $\lambda_\pi$ is the normal extension of $\pi$, so $\lambda_\pi(a) = \pi(a)$ for each $a\in A$.  As any member of $A^{**}$ can be weak$^*$-approximated by a bounded net in $A$, it follows that if $x\in\hom(\pi_1,\pi_2)$ then also $x \in \hom(\lambda_{\pi_1}, \lambda_{\pi_2})$.

Let $\pi_0\in\Rep(A)$ be a representation with $\lambda_{\pi_0}$ faithful: that is, $\pi_0$ is a universal representation, which exists by \cite[Theorem~III.2.4]{TakesakiI} for example.  We may suppose that $H_{\pi_0}$ is sufficiently large that every normal state on $A^{**}$ arises from a vector functional.  There is a unique $a_0 \in A^{**}$ with $\lambda_{\pi_0}(a_0) = \alpha_{\pi_0}$.  Now let $\pi\in\Rep(A)$, and let $\xi\in H_\pi$ be a unit vector, so as $\pi$ is non-degenerate, $A \to \mathbb C; a \mapsto (\xi|\pi(a)\xi)$ is a state on $A$, hence a normal state on $A^{**}$, and so there is $\xi_0 \in H_{\pi_0}$ with $(\xi|\pi(a)\xi) = (\xi_0 | \pi_0(a)\xi_0)$ for every $a\in A$.  Thus the map $u \colon \pi_0(a) \xi_0 \mapsto \pi(a)\xi$ extends to a partial isometry with initial space $\overline{\pi_0(A)\xi_0}$ and final space $\overline{\pi(A)\xi}$.  Then $u \in \hom(\pi_0, \pi)$, and so $\alpha_\pi u = u \alpha_{\pi_0} = u \pi_0(a_0)$.  Approximating $a_0$ be a bounded net in $A$, for example, we may check that $u \pi_0(a_0) = \lambda_\pi(a_0) u$.  In particular, $\alpha_\pi \xi = \lambda_\pi(a_0) \xi$.  As $\xi$ was arbitrary, this shows that $\alpha_\pi = \lambda_\pi(a_0)$, for any $\pi$.

Conversely, given any $a\in A^{**}$, we have already argued that the family $(\lambda_\pi(a))$ defines a natural transformation.  So conclude that $\hom(H,H) \cong A^{**}$.
\end{example}


\subsection{The \texorpdfstring{$C^*$}{CStar}-algebra of a \texorpdfstring{$C^*$}{CStar}-catgeory}\label{sec:alg_of_cat}

Consider two objects $A,B$ in $\mc A$.  We can turn the space
\[ M(A,B) = \begin{pmatrix} \mc A(A,A) & \mc A(B,A) \\ \mc A(A,B) & \mc A(B,B) \end{pmatrix}
= \Big\{ \begin{pmatrix} a & x \\ y & b \end{pmatrix} : \begin{matrix} a\in\mc A(A,A), x\in\mc A(B,A) \\
  y \in\mc A(A, B), b\in\mc A(B,B) \end{matrix} 
  \Big\} \]  
into a $*$-algebra for the obvious matrix adjoint and multiplication.  Any functor $F\colon\mc A\to\catHilb$ induces a $*$-representation $M(A,B) \to \mc B(F(A)\oplus F(B))$.  Suppose this functor is faithful, hence isometric, on $\mc A(A,A)$ and $\mc A(B,B)$ (such a functor exists by Theorem~\ref{thm:exists_faithful_rep}).  For $x\in\mc A(B,A)$ we see that
\[ \left\| \left(\begin{smallmatrix} 0 & x \\ 0 & 0 \end{smallmatrix}\right) \right\|^2
= \left\| \left(\begin{smallmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ x^* & 0 \end{smallmatrix}\right) \left(\begin{smallmatrix} 0 & x \\ 0 & 0 \end{smallmatrix}\right) \right\|
= \left\| \left(\begin{smallmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & x^*x \end{smallmatrix}\right) \right\|
= \| x^*x \|_{\mc A(B,B)} = \|x\|_{\mc A(B,A)}^2 \]
and similarly for $\mc A(A,B)$.  Thus we obtain a $C^*$-norm on $M(A,B)$ which restricts to the given norms on each component.  We remark that we only need the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:exists_faithful_rep} here, so $\mc A$ doesn't need to be small.

\begin{remark}
I do not immediately see how to construct a $C^*$-norm on $M(A,B)$ directly.  However, once we have a norm which agrees on the given norms on $\mc A(A,A)$ and $\mc A(B,B)$, as above, also the norm agrees with the given norms on the off-diagonal corner entries.  Of course, once we have established such a norm exists, it is unique, \cite[Corollary~I.5.4]{TakesakiI}.
\end{remark}

More generally, given any set $I$ of objects of $\mc A$, we can form $M_0(I)$ as ``matrices'' $(x_{i,j})$ where $x_{i,j} \in \mc A(i,j)$ for $i,j\in I$, and all but finitely many of the $x_{i,j}$ are zero.  Again, we can find a faithful representation of this $*$-algebra, and so form a $C^*$-completion $M(I)$ where the inclusion $\mc A(i,j) \to M(I)$ is isometric for each $i,j\in I$.  In particular, when $I$ is finite, $M_0(I) = M(I)$ has a unique $C^*$-norm.

When $\mc A$ is small, we turn the finite subsets of the objects of $\mc A$ into a direct set, ordered by inclusion.  When $I\subseteq J$ there is an obvious isometric inclusion $M(I) \to M(J)$, and so we obtain an inductive system (the inclusions are coherent in the required way).  We can hence form the inductive limit $C^*$-algebra $M(\mc A) = \underrightarrow{\lim}\, M(I)$.  For each $I$ there is an isometric inclusion $M(I) \to M(\mc A)$, respecting the inclusions $M(I)\to M(J)$, and such that the union of these images is dense in $M(\mc A)$.  Given $x\in \mc A(A,B)$, given any $I \supseteq \{A,B\}$, we consider $x\in M(I) \subseteq M(\mc A)$, and coherence ensures this isometric map $\mc A(A,B) \to M(\mc A)$ is well-defined.  We then obtain a functor $\mc A \to M(\mc A)$ (the latter considered as a one-object $C^*$-Category).  Passing if necessary to the unitisation, for each $A$ we find a projection $1_A \in M(\mc A)$.  Obviously $x\in\mc A(A,B)$ implies that $1_B x 1_A = x$ in $M(\mc A)$.

\begin{lemma}
The image of $\mc A(A,B)$ in $M(\mc A)$ is the set $\{ x\in M(\mc A) : 1_B x 1_A = x \}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
We need to show that if $1_B x 1_A = x$ then $x$ arises from some $y\in \mc A(A,B)$.  Given some finite $I$ and $z\in M(I)$, clearly $1_B z 1_A = z$ if and only if $z\in\mc A(A,B)$.  By density, we can find a sequence of such $(z_n)$ (where $I$ varies with $n$) with $z_n \to x$ in $M(\mc A)$.  Then $x = 1_B x 1_A = \lim_n 1_B z_n 1_A$ and for each $n$ we have that $1_B z_n 1_A$ is identified with some $y_n \in \mc A(A,B)$.  So $(y_n)$ is Cauchy in $\mc A(A,B)$ and so converges, say to $y$.  Then clearly $x = \lim_n y_n = y$, as required.
\end{proof}

Let $F\colon \mc A \to \mc B$ be a functor.  Given a (finite) set of objects $I$ in $\mc A$, let $F(I) = \{ F(A) : A\in I \}$ a set of objects in $B$.  One may check that $F$ induces a $*$-homomorphism $F_I \colon M(I) \to M(F(I))$, and when $I\subseteq J$ we have that $F_J \colon M(J) \to M(F(J))$ and $F_I$ are intertwined by the inclusions.  By coherence, we obtain a $*$-homomorphism $M(I) \to M(\mc B)$, and as $I$ varies, these are coherent, so we obtain a $*$-homomorphism $F \colon M(\mc A) \to M(\mc B)$.  Conversely, suppose we have a $*$-homomorphism $\phi \colon M(\mc A) \to M(\mc B)$ along with a map $\phi_0$ from objects of $\mc A$ to objects of $\mc B$, such that $1_{\phi_0(B)} \phi(1_B x 1_A) 1_{\phi_0(A)} = \phi(1_B x 1_A)$ for all $A,B$ and all $x\in\mc M(\mc A)$.  Then $\phi$ arises from a functor $F$ which on objects is the map $\phi_0$.


\subsection{Equivalence and adjoints}

The following is shown carefully by Dell'Ambrogio.

\begin{proposition}[{\cite[Proposition~2.6]{DellAmbrogio_unitary_monoidal}}]\label{prop:always_unitary_IM}
Let $A,B\in\mc A$ be isomorphic.  There is a unitary $u\in\mc A(A,B)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Let $a\in\mc A(A,B)$ is invertible.  Then $u = a (a^*a)^{-1/2}$ is the required unitary.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:equiv_star_cats}
Let $\mc A,\mc B$ be $C^*$-tensor categories.  A $*$-functor $F\colon \mc A\to\mc B$ is an equivalence of categories if and only if there is a $*$-functor $G \colon \mc B \to \mc A$ and unitary $\epsilon \colon FG \Rightarrow 1_{\mc B}$ and $\eta \colon 1_{\mc A} \Rightarrow GF$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The ``if'' case is a triviality; the ``only if'' case follows by examining the proof of Proposition~\ref{thm:cat_equiv_FFES}, for example, because using Proposition~\ref{prop:always_unitary_IM}, we may choose the components $\epsilon_B$ to be unitaries, and then the constructed $G$ will be a $*$-functor, and $\eta$ will be unitary.  See also \cite[Lemma~4.6]{DellAmbrogio_unitary_monoidal}.
\end{proof}

Mitchener \emph{defines} an equivalence of $C^*$-Categories by requiring unitary natural transformations, \cite[Definition~2.13]{mitchener_cstar_cats}.  The previous result shows that this is not really necessary.

I haven't seen much in my reading of the literature which touches upon adjoints.  I wondered if it is sensible to ask that (co)units be unitary, but nothing of interest immediately came to mind.



\section{\texorpdfstring{$W^*$}{WStar}-Categories}

This is the primary topic of \cite{GLR_Wstar_categories}.

\begin{definition}
Let $\mc A$ be a $C^*$-Category such that every morphism space $\mc A(A,B)$ is a dual Banach space, say with predual $\mc A(A,B)_*$.  In this case, $\mc A$ is a \emph{$W^*$-Category}.
\end{definition}

It is not totally clear in the original, but we mean ``isometric predual'', so to be precise, there is a Banach space $\mc A(A,B)_*$ and a linear isometric bijection between $\mc A(A,B)$ and the dual space of $\mc A(A,B)_*$.  This matters, because it is possible for a $C^*$-algebra to be isomorphic (but not isometric) to a dual space without being a $W^*$-algebra: an example is \cite[Example~6.9.10]{DDLS_CX_spaces_Book} which gives a construction of a compact Hausdorff space $X$ such that $C(X)$ is isomorphic to the dual of a Banach space, but $C(X)$ is not a von Neumann algebra, because $X$ is not a hyper-Stonian space.  As one might expect, it is not easy to sketch the construction of $X$.  We come back to this in the next subsection.

\begin{example}
The $C^*$-Category $\catHilb$ is a $W^*$-Category.  For Hilbert spaces $H,K$ the space of bounded linear maps $\mc B(H,K)$ is dual to $\mc T(K,H)^*$, the dual of the trace-class operators, for trace-duality.  Alternatively, the predual is $H \proten \overline K$, the projective tensor product of $H$ with the conjugate of $K$.
\end{example}

\begin{example}\label{eg:subcat_Hilb_WStar}
Let $\mc A$ be a subcategory of $\catHilb$ such that each morphism space $\mc A(A,B)$ is weak$^*$-closed.  Then $\mc A$ is a $W^*$-Category.
\end{example}

\begin{example}
Let $M$ be a von Neumann algebra, and let $\catHWStar{M}$ be the full subcategory of $\catHCStar{M}$ consisting of \emph{self-dual} Hilbert $C^*$-modules $E$ over $M$, see \cite{paschke_inner_prod_mods}.  By definition, this means that if $t\colon E \to M$ is bounded and an $M$-module map, then there is $x_0\in E$ with $t(x) = (x_0|x)$ for each $x\in E$.  By \cite[Proposition~3.8]{paschke_inner_prod_mods} each self-dual module is (isometrically) a dual space, and by \cite[Proposition~3.10]{paschke_inner_prod_mods}, $\mc L(E) = \hom(E,E)$ is a dual space.  An examination of the proof shows that this also holds for $\mc L(X,Y)$, and so $\catHWStar{M}$ is a $W^*$-Category.  \cite[Proposition~3.4]{paschke_inner_prod_mods} also shows that $\mc L(X,Y)$ coincides with the space of bounded $M$-module maps, when $X,Y$ are self-dual.

Furthermore, in \cite[Section~3]{paschke_inner_prod_mods}, Paschke shows that for any Hilbert $C^*$-module $E$ over $M$, denoting $E'$ the space of all module maps $E\to M$, we have that $E'$ becomes a self-dual Hilbert $C^*$-module, for an inner-product which extends that of $E$.  Any $T\in\mc L(X,Y)$ extends to $T'\in\mc L(X',Y')$, and this preserves the $*$-structure.  We hence obtain a functor $F \colon \catHCStar{M} \to \catHWStar{M}$, with the property that if $\iota \colon \catHWStar{M} \to \catHCStar{M}$ is the inclusion, then $F \circ \iota = \id$.
\end{example}

We shall abuse terminology a little, and use ``von Neuamnn algebra'' interchangably with ``$W^*$-algebra''.  If we wished to be more precise, a von Neumann algebra $M$ should always come with an embedding $M\subseteq\mc B(H)$, while a $W^*$-algebra is an abstract $C^*$-algebra with a (n isometric) predual.  Modern usage, however, tends to elide these differences.

Here $2\times 2$ matrix techniques can be useful, compare Section~\ref{sec:alg_of_cat}.  We give one example: let $H,K$ be Hilbert spaces and let $X\subseteq \mc B(H,K)$ be a linear subspace.  Then consider
\[ A_X = \begin{pmatrix} A & X^* \\ X & B \end{pmatrix} \subseteq \mc B(H \oplus K) \cong
\begin{pmatrix} \mc B(H) & \mc B(K,H)  \\  \mc B(H,K) & \mc B(K) \end{pmatrix}. \]
Here $X^*=\{x^*:x\in X\}$ and $A=\lin\{ x^*y : x,y\in X\}, B=\lin\{xy^* : x,y\in X \}$.  Then $A_X$ is a self-adjoint subspace of $\mc B(H\oplus K)$.  In general, $A_X$ is not an algebra, but when $X$ is a \emph{ternary ring of operators}, meaning that $\{ xy^*z: x,y,z\in X\}\subseteq X$, we see that $A$ and $B$ are $*$-algebras, and that $X$ is a $B$-$A$-bimodule (and $X^*$ an $A$-$B$-bimodule), and hence $A_X$ is a $*$-algebra.  We can then apply various results from the theory of operator algebras to $A_X$: for example, the Kaplansky density theorem.

More prosaically, suppose $X$ is weak operator topology closed, and we define $A$ and $B$ to the weak operator topology closures of the algebras defined before.  Then $A_X$ is wot closed: this can be shown by using that the projection of $A_X$ onto $A$, and the inclusion of $A$ into $A_X$, are wot continuous, and so forth.  It follows that $A_X$ is a von Neumann algebra, so in particular is closed in all of the operator topologies, and so the same is true of $X$.

\begin{example}
Thus, in Example~\ref{eg:subcat_Hilb_WStar}, $\mc A(A,B)$ is closed in one of the operator topologies if and only if it is closed in any of them.
\end{example}

\begin{example}
We give an example of a subspace $X\subseteq\mc B(H)$, with $H$ a separable Hilbert space, with $X$ weak$^*$-closed but not wot closed.  Let $H = \ell^2$ with standard orthonormal basis $(\delta_i)$.  For $\xi,\eta\in H$ denote by $\rankone{\xi}{\eta}$ the rank-one operator $t \mapsto (\eta|t)\xi$.  Let $\tau = \sum_{n\geq 1} 2^{-n} \rankone{\delta_n}{\delta_n}$ a trace-class operator with $\tau(1) = 1$.

Let $I$ be the collection of finite-dimensional subspaces of $H$ partially ordered by inclusion, so $I$ becomes a directed set.  For $H_0\in I$ let $(e_j)_{j=1}^m$ be an orthonormal basis of $H_0$.  We first claim we can find an operator $z_i$ with $z_i(H_0) \subseteq H_0^\perp$ and yet $\tau(z_i) = \Tr(\tau z_i)\not=0$.  Towards a contradiction, suppose not.  Notice that an operator $z$ satisfies $z(H_0) \subseteq H_0^\perp$ if and only if $\tau_{j,k}(z)=0$ for each $1\leq j,k\leq m$, where $\tau_{j,k} = \rankone{e_j}{e_k}$.  So for $z\in\mc B(H)$ we have that $\tau_{j,k}(z)=0$ for all $j,k$ implies $\tau(z)=0$.  By Hahn-Banach, this means that $\tau \in \lin \{ \tau_{j,k} \}$.  In particular, viewing $\tau$ as an operator on $H$, we have that $\im\tau \subseteq H_0$, contradiction, as $\tau(\delta_i) = 2^{-i}\delta_i$ for every $i$.

Let $p_i = \sum_{j=1}^m \rankone{e_j}{e_j}$ the projection onto $H_0$.  By rescaling $z_i$ we find a $y_i$ with $y_i(H_0)\subseteq H_0^\perp$ and $\tau(y_i) = -\tau(p_i)$.  Set $x_i = p_i + y_i$, so for $\xi,\eta\in H_0$ we see that $(\xi|x_i\eta) = (\xi|\eta) + (\xi|y_i\eta) = (\xi|\eta)$ as $y_i\eta\in H_0^\perp$.  Also $\tau(x_i) = \tau(p_i) + \tau(y_i) = 0$.  We hence obtain a net $(x_i)$ with $x_i \to 1$ in the weak operator topology, but $\tau(x_i)=0$ for every $i$, so $x_i \not\to 1$ weak$^*$.

Now let $X$ be the weak$^*$-closure of the linear span of the $x_i$.  As $\tau(x_i)=0$ for every $i$, $\tau$ annihilates $X$ and so $1\not\in X$.  However, as $x_i\to 1$ wot, we see that $X$ is not wot closed.

Of course, $X$ cannot be a TRO, and so cannot arise as a morphism space of a $C^*$-Category.
\end{example}

\begin{example}\label{eg:RepA_is_WStar}
Let $A$ be a $C^*$-algebra and consider $\Rep(A)$ as in Example~\ref{eg:RepA}.  Each morphism space is weak$^*$-closed, and so we obtain a $W^*$-Category.
\end{example}

\subsection{The algebra of a catgeory}

We believe there is a gap in the proof of \cite[Lemma~2.6]{GLR_Wstar_categories}: the explicit use of ``equivalence of norms'' is not valid, as it is possible for a $C^*$-algebra to be \emph{isomorphic} (but not \emph{isometric}) to a dual Banach space and yet fail to be a $W^*$-algebra, as mentioned above.  We fix this problem in \cite{TODO}.  So when $\mc A$ is a $W^*$-category, the algebra $M(A,B)$, see Section~\ref{sec:alg_of_cat}, is always a von Neumann algebra.  We state this formally.

\begin{theorem}[{See my paper \cite{TODO}}]\label{thm:MAB_WStar}
Let $\mc A$ be a $W^*$-category and let $A,B\in\mc A$.  The $C^*$-algebra $M(A,B)$ is a $W^*$-algebra, and the isometric inclusions $\mc A(A) \to M(A,B), \mc A(B) \to M(A,B), \mc A(A,B) \to M(A,B)$ are all weak$^*$-weak$^*$-homeomorphisms onto their ranges.
\end{theorem}

\begin{corollary}
Let $\mc A$ be a $W^*$-category and let $A,B\in\mc A$.  The predual $\mc A(A,B)_*$ is, up to isometric isomorphism, unique.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The $W^*$-algebra $M(A,B)$ has a unique predual, and this determines the weak$^*$-topology on $\mc A(A,B)$.

One might worry that the ``weak$^*$-topology'' could be given by different preduals, so let us give some details, using (for example) \cite{MegginsonBook} which conveniently has all that we need.  Denote by $\iota \colon \mc A(A,B) \to M(A,B)$ the inclusion, which is weak$^*$-weak$^*$-continuous, so by \cite[Theorem~3.1.11]{MegginsonBook} there is a bounded linear map $\iota_* \colon M(A,B)_* \to \mc A(A,B)_*$ with $(\iota_*)^* = \iota$.  %As $\iota$ has norm closed image also $\iota_*$ has norm closed image, \cite[Theorem~3.1.21]{MegginsonBook} (this is perhaps the only result we use which is not easily shown).
As $\iota$ is an isomorphism onto its range, \cite[Theorem~3.1.22]{MegginsonBook} shows that $\iota_*$ is surjective.  Factor $\iota_*$ as the composition of the quotient map $M(A,B)_* \to M(A,B)_* / \ker(\iota_*)$ and the induced map $j \colon M(A,B)_* / \ker(\iota_*) \to \mc A(A,B)_*$.  So $j$ is a bijection, hence invertible by the Open Mapping Theorem.  The adjoints of these maps are
\[ j^* \colon \mc A(A,B) \to \ker(\iota_*)^\circ = \{ x\in M(A,B) : \ip{x}{\omega}=0 \ (\omega\in\ker(\iota_*)) \} \]
an isomorphism, and the adjoint of the quotient map which is the (isometric) inclusion $\ker(\iota_*)^\circ \to M(A,B)$.  As the composition is $\iota$, an isometry, we must have that $j^*$ is an isometric isomorphism, and so $j$ is also an isometric isomorphism.  We conclude that $\iota_*$ is a ``metric surjection'', that is,
\[ \|\mu\| = \inf\bigl\{ \|\omega\| : \omega\in M(A,B)_* \text{ and } \iota_*(\omega)=\mu \bigr\} \qquad (\mu\in\mc A(A,B)_*). \]
It is now clear that $M(A,B)_*$ determines both the space $\mc A(A,B)_*$ and the norm on $\mc A(A,B)_*$.
\end{proof}

It is helpful to use a form of ``module notation''.  Given a $C^*$-category $\mc A$ and $A,B,C\in\mc A$, for $x\in\mc A(B,C)$ and $\mu\in\mc A(A,C)^*$ we define $\mu\cdot x \colon \mc A(A,B) \to \mathbb C; y \mapsto \mu(xy)$, so $\mu\cdot x\in\mc A(A,B)^*$.  Similarly $x\cdot\mu$ can be defined when this makes sense.

Up to isomorphism, $M(A,B)$ is the direct sum of its \emph{components} or \emph{corners} $\mc A(A), \mc A(A,B)$ and so forth.  The components are contractively complemented subspaces, because, for example, the map
\[ M(A,B)\to M(A,B); \quad \begin{smat} a & x^* \\ y & b \end{smat} \mapsto
\begin{smat} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{smat}
\begin{smat} a & x^* \\ y & b \end{smat}
\begin{smat} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{smat}
= \begin{smat} 0 & 0 \\ y & 0 \end{smat}, \]
is a contractive projection with image isometric to $\mc A(A,B)$.  Notice that this map is also weak$^*$-weak$^*$-continuous, and so its Banach space pre-adjoint gives a contractive projection of $M(A,B)_*$ to $\mc A(A,B)_*$.   Thus, up to isomorphism, the predual $M(A,B)_*$ is isomorphic to a direct sum of the preduals of the components.  We find it helpful to write
\[ M(A,B)_* \cong \begin{pmatrix} \mc A(A)_* & \mc A(A,B)_* \\ \mc A(B,A)_* & \mc A(B)_* \end{pmatrix}. \]
Notice the unusual order of the anti-diagonal: this is chosen so that the canonical module actions of $M(A,B)$ on $M(A,B)_*$ are given by matrix multiplication.  For example,
\begin{align*}
& \ip{ \begin{pmatrix} a' & y'^* \\ x' & b' \end{pmatrix} }{ \begin{pmatrix} a & y^* \\ x & b \end{pmatrix} \cdot \begin{pmatrix} \omega_A & \mu_0 \\ \mu_1 & \omega_B \end{pmatrix} }
= \ip{ \begin{pmatrix} a'a + y'^*x & a'y^* + y'^*b \\ x'a + b'x & x'y^* + b'b \end{pmatrix} }{ \begin{pmatrix} \omega_A & \mu_0 \\ \mu_1 & \omega_B \end{pmatrix} }  \\
&\qquad
= \omega_A(a'a + y'^*x) + \mu_0(x'a+b'x) + \mu_1(a'y^*+y'^*b) + \omega_B(x'y^*+b'b)   \\
&\qquad
= \ip{ \begin{pmatrix} a' & y'^* \\ x' & b' \end{pmatrix} }{ \begin{pmatrix} a\cdot\omega_A + y^*\cdot\mu_1 & a\cdot\mu_0 + y^*\cdot\omega_B \\ x\cdot\omega_A + b\cdot\mu_1 & x\cdot\mu_0 + b\cdot\omega_B \end{pmatrix} },
\end{align*}
which is indeed ``matrix multiplication''.

As we find some of \cite[Section~2]{GLR_Wstar_categories} hard to follow, we now give some proofs of further results from there.  Fix a $W^*$-catgeory $\mc A$.  Given $A,B\in\mc A$ we say that $u\in \mc A(A,B)$ is a \emph{partial isometry} when $u u^* u = u$.  The following is of course standard, but we give a proof which does not use Hilbert spaces.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:pi}
For $u\in\mc A(A,B)$ the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[(1)]
  \item\label{lem:pi:1}
    $uu^*u=u$;
  \item\label{lem:pi:2}
    $u^*u \in \mc A(A)$ is a projection;
  \item\label{lem:pi:3}
    $uu^* \in \mc A(B)$ is a projection;
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
That \ref{lem:pi:1} implies \ref{lem:pi:2} is a calculation: $(u^*u)(u^*u) = u^*(uu^*u) = u^*u$ is obviously also self-adjoint.  Conversely, if \ref{lem:pi:2} holds, then set $a = u(1-u^*u) \in \mc A(A,B)$, so $a^*a = u^*u - (u^*u)^2 - (u^*u)^2 + - (u^*u)^3 = 0$ and hence $a=0$ by the axiom.  Hence $u = uu^*u$ showing \ref{lem:pi:1}.

Given \ref{lem:pi:2}, we follow the proof of \cite[Theorem~2.3.2]{Murphy_book}, and observe that $(uu^*)^3 = u (u^*u)^2 u^* = (uu^*)^2$, so the spectrum of $uu^*$ is contained in $\{0,1\}$.  By the functional calculus, the positive element $uu^*$ is a projection, so \ref{lem:pi:3} holds.  By symmetry, also \ref{lem:pi:3} implies \ref{lem:pi:2}.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[{\cite[Corollary~2.7]{GLR_Wstar_categories}}]\label{prop:Wstar_has_PD}
Let $\mc A$ be a $W^*$-category, let $A,B\in\mc A$ and let $x\in \mc A(A,B)$.  Let $|x| = (x^*x)^{1/2} \in \mc A(A)$.  There is a unique partial isometry $u\in\mc A(A,B)$ with $x = u|x|$, $u^*u$ equals the support projection of $|x|$, and $uu^*$ equals the support projection of $|x^*|$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
We consider the element $x_0 = \left(\begin{smallmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ x & 0 \end{smallmatrix}\right)$ in the von Neumann algebra $M(A,B)$.  By the polar decomposition theorem for von Neumann algebras, \cite[Theorem~1.12.1]{Sakai_Book}, there is a unique $u_0 \in M(A,B)$ with $x_0 = u_0 |x_0|$ and $u_0^*u_0 = s(|x_0|), u_0u_0^* = s(|x_0^*|)$.  Notice that $|x_0| = \begin{smat} (x^*x)^{1/2} & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{smat} = \begin{smat} |x| & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{smat}$ and $|x_0^*| = \begin{smat} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & (xx^*)^{1/2} \end{smat} = \begin{smat} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & |x^*| \end{smat}$.  It follows that
\[ s(|x_0|) \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix} = s(|x_0|)
\quad\implies\quad
u_0 = u_0 u_0^* u_0 = u_0 s(|x_0|^*)
= u_0 s(|x_0|^*) \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}
= u_0 \begin{pmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{pmatrix}. \]
Similarly $u_0 = \begin{smat} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{smat} u_0$.  So there is $u\in \mc A(A,B)$ with $u_0 = \begin{smat} 0 & 0 \\ u & 0 \end{smat}$.  Then
\[ \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ u & 0 \end{pmatrix} = u_0
= u_0 u_0^* u_0
= \begin{pmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ u u^* u & 0 \end{pmatrix}, \]
so $u$ is a partial isometry.  Similar calculations show that $u|x| = x$ and $u^*u = s(|x|), uu^* = s(|x^*|)$.

Conversely, given such a $u$,  setting $u_0 = \begin{smat} 0 & 0 \\ u & 0 \end{smat}$ gives a partial isometry in $M(A,B)$ satisfying the properties above.  However, such a $u_0$ is unique, and so also $u$ is unique.
\end{proof}

We recall the notion of the \emph{support} of a positive normal functional $\omega$ on a von Neumann algebra $M$, \cite[Section~III.4]{TakesakiI} or \cite[Section~1.14]{Sakai_Book} for example.  For us, the most useful characterisation is that $s(\omega) = 1 - p$ where $p$ is the greatest projection with $\omega(p) = 0$. We also have that $\omega = s(\omega)\cdot\omega = \omega\cdot s(\omega)$.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:support_corner_pos_func}
Let $\omega \in \mc A(A)_*^+$ and let $\omega_0 = \begin{smat} \omega_0 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{smat} \in M(A,B)$.  Then $s(\omega_0) = \begin{smat} s(\omega) & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{smat}$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $p = \begin{smat} p_{11} & p_{12} \\ p_{12}^* & p_{22} \end{smat}$ be the greatest projection with $\omega_0(p)=0$.  So obviously $p \geq \begin{smat} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{smat}$.  Hence
\[ p \begin{smat} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{smat} = \begin{smat} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{smat} = \begin{smat} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{smat} p
\quad\implies\quad
\begin{smat} 0 & p_{12} \\ 0 & p_{22} \end{smat} = \begin{smat} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{smat}
= \begin{smat} 0 & 0 \\ p_{12}^* & p_{22} \end{smat}
\quad\implies\quad
p = \begin{smat} p_{11} & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{smat}, \]
for some projection $p_{11} \in \mc A(A)$.  Then $p_{11}$ must be the greatest projection with $\omega(p_{11})=0$, that is, $p_{11} = 1-s(\omega)$.  So $s(\omega_0) = 1 - p$ is as claimed.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[{\cite[Corollary~2.8]{GLR_Wstar_categories}}]\label{prop:pd_funcs}
Every $f \in \mc A(A,B)_*$ can be expressed uniquely in the form $f = \omega \cdot u$ where $\omega\in\mc A(A)_*^+$ and $u\in \mc A(B,A)$ is a partial isometry with $uu^* = s(\omega)$.  Furthermore, $f \cdot u^* = \omega$ and $\|\omega\| = \|f\|$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
We find the proof in \cite{GLR_Wstar_categories} to be extremely sketchy, so we give the details.  Define
\[ f_0 \colon M(A,B) \to \mathbb C; \quad \begin{smat} a & y \\ x & b \end{smat} \mapsto \overline{f(y^*)}. \]
As the projection $M(A,B) \to \mc A(B,A)$ is weak$^*$-continuous, $f_0 \in M(A,B)_*$.  By the polar decomposition theorem, \cite[Theorem~III.4.2]{TakesakiI} for example, there is a unique $\omega_0 \in M(A,B)_*^+$ and partial isometry $u_0 \in M(A,B)$ with $f_0 = u_0 \cdot \omega_0$ and $u_0^* u_0 = s(\omega_0)$.  Furthermore, $s(\omega_0) = s_r(f_0)$ where $s_r(f_0)$ is the projection with $M(A,B)_* \cdot s_r(f_0) = \overline{M(A,B)\cdot f_0}$, and $v_0v_0^* = s_l(f_0)$ where $s_l(f_0)$ is the projection with $s_l(f_0) \cdot M(A,B)_* = \overline{f_0 \cdot M(A,B)}$.

We compute $M(A,B) \cdot f_0$.  We have
\begin{align*}
\begin{smat} a & y^* \\ x & b \end{smat} \cdot f_0 \colon 
\begin{smat} a' & y'^* \\ x' & b' \end{smat} \mapsto
  f_0\left( \begin{smat} a' & y'^* \\ x' & b' \end{smat}
  \begin{smat} a & y^* \\ x & b \end{smat} \right)
= \overline{ f( (a'a+y'^*x)^* ) }
= \overline{ f( a^*a'^* + x^*y' ) }
= \ip{\begin{smat} a' & y'^* \\ x' & b' \end{smat}}{\begin{smat} \mu_0 & 0 \\ \mu_1 & 0 \end{smat} },
\end{align*}
say, where, for example, $\mu_1(y'^*) = \overline{f(x^*y')}$.  Hence $(M(A,B)\cdot f_0) \cdot \begin{smat} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{smat} = M(A,B)\cdot f_0$, and so $M(A,B)_* \cdot s_r(f_0) = M(A,B)_* \cdot s_r(f_0) \begin{smat} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{smat}$.  Thus $s_r(f_0) = s_r(f_0) \begin{smat} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{smat}$.  An analogous calculation shows that $\begin{smat} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{smat} s_l(f_0) = s_l(f_0)$.

As $s_r(f_0) = u_0^* u_0$ it follows that $u_0^* u_0 = u_0^* u_0\begin{smat} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{smat}$ and so $u_0 = u_0 \begin{smat} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{smat}$ using that $u_0 u_0^* u_0 = u_0$.  Similarly, $u_0 = \begin{smat} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{smat} u_0$.  Hence there is $u\in \mc A(B,A)$ with $u_0 = \begin{smat} 0 & 0 \\ u^* & 0\end{smat}$ and that $u_0 u_0^* u_0 = u_0$ is equivalent to $u^*uu^*=u^*$, so $u^*$ is a partial isometry.  Then $s(\omega_0) = u_0^*u_0 = \begin{smat} uu^* & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{smat}$ and so as $\omega_0 = s(\omega_0) \cdot \omega_0 \cdot s(\omega_0)$ it follows that $\omega_0 = \begin{smat} \omega & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{smat}$ for some (necessarily positive) $\omega \in \mc A(A)_*$.  By Lemma~\ref{lem:support_corner_pos_func} we see that $s(\omega) = uu^*$.  Finally, we see that for $x\in\mc A(A,B)$,
\[ \overline{f(x)} = f_0\left( \begin{smat} 0 & x^* \\ 0 & 0 \end{smat} \right)
= \omega_0 \left( \begin{smat} 0 & x^* \\ 0 & 0 \end{smat} u_0 \right)
= \omega_0 \left( \begin{smat} x^*u^* & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{smat} \right)
= \omega(x^*u^*)
= \overline{ \omega(ux) } = \overline{ \ip{x}{\omega\cdot u} }, \]
using that $\omega$ is positive.  So $f = \omega\cdot u$ as claimed.

To show uniqueness, suppose also $f = \omega'\cdot u'$ for some other choice of $\omega', u'$.  Set $\omega_0' = \begin{smat} \omega' & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{smat}$ and $u_0' = \begin{smat} 0 & 0 \\ u'^* & 0 \end{smat}$.  Then $\omega'_0 \in M(A,B)_*^+$ and $u_0'$ is a partial isometry.  We have that
\[ (u_0'\cdot\omega_0') \begin{smat} a & y \\ x & b \end{smat}
= \ip{ \begin{smat} a & y \\ x & b \end{smat} \begin{smat} 0 & 0 \\ u'^* & 0 \end{smat} }{ \begin{smat} \omega' & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{smat} }
= \omega'(yu'^*)
= \overline{ \omega'(u'y^*) }
= \overline{ f(y^*) }
= f_0 \begin{smat} a & y \\ x & b \end{smat}. \]
So $u_0' \cdot \omega_0' = f_0$, and by Lemma~\ref{lem:support_corner_pos_func}
\[ s(\omega_0') = \begin{smat} s(\omega') & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{smat}
= \begin{smat} u'u'^* & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{smat}
= u_0'^* u_0', \]
so by the uniqueness of the polar decomposition of $f_0$, we conclude that $u_0' = u_0, \omega_0' = \omega_0$.  Thus also $u' = u$ and $\omega' = \omega$.

We finish by show the norm equality.  Obviously $\|f\| \leq \|\omega\| \|u\| = \|\omega\|$, but also $f \cdot u^* = \omega \cdot (uu^*) = \omega \cdot s(\omega) = \omega$ and so $\|\omega\| \leq \|f\|$ and we have equality.
\end{proof}

We also have an analogous result for $B$.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:pd_funcs_other}
Every $f \in \mc A(A,B)_*$ can be expressed uniquely in the form $f = v \cdot \omega$ where $\omega\in\mc A(B)_*^+$ and $v\in \mc A(B,A)$ is a partial isometry with $v^*v = s(\omega)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
This time, define
\[ f_0 \colon M(A,B) \to \mathbb C; \quad \begin{smat} a & y \\ x & b \end{smat} \mapsto f(x). \]
Now take the polar decomposition of $f_0$, and proceed as in the above proof.
\end{proof}

Omitted from \cite{GLR_Wstar_categories} is any discussion of a converse, perhaps because it is easier.

\begin{proposition}
Let $\omega\in\mc A(A)_*$ and $u\in\mc A(B,A)$.  Then $f = \omega\cdot u \in \mc A(A,B)_*$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Notice that $f$ factors as the composition of the inclusion $\mc A(A,B) \to M(A,B)$ and the functional
\[ f_0 \colon M(A,B) \to \mathbb C; \quad
\begin{smat} a & y^* \\ x & b \end{smat} \mapsto \omega(ux)
= \ip{ \begin{smat} 0 & u \\ 0 & 0 \end{smat} \begin{smat} a & y \\ x & b \end{smat} }{ \begin{smat} \omega & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{smat} }. \]
Both these maps are weak$^*$-continuous, and so $f$ is weak$^*$-continuous, as claimed.
\end{proof}

The following result has already been implicitly used when we have only two objects: it follows from Theorem~\ref{thm:MAB_WStar} and that $M(A,B)_*$ is a $M(A,B)$-bimodule.  We now extend it to three objects (which also follows, eventually, from Corollary~\ref{prop:func_to_Hilb} below.)

\begin{corollary}\label{corr:sep_ws_cty}
Let $\mc A$ be a $W^*$-category.  For any $A,B,C\in\mc A$ the composition $\mc A(B,C) \times \mc A(A,B) \to \mc A(A,C)$ is separately weak$^*$-continuous.  Equivalently, for $\omega \in \mc A(A,C)_*, x\in\mc A(B,C), y\in\mc A(A,B)$ we have $\omega\cdot x \in \mc A(A,B)_*$ and $y\cdot\omega \in \mc A(B,C)_*$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Fix $\omega\in\mc A(A,C)_*$ and $x\in\mc A(B,C)$.  By Proposition~\ref{prop:pd_funcs}, there are $\omega_A \in \mc A(A)_*$ and $u\in\mc A(C, A)$ with $\omega = \omega_A \cdot u$.  Then $\omega\cdot x = \omega_A \cdot (ux)$.  Noting that $ux \in \mc A(B,A)$, we have reduced the problem to working in $M(A,B)$, and so we know that $\omega_A \cdot ux \in \mc A(A,B)_*$, as required.  The other claim follows analogously using Proposition~\ref{prop:pd_funcs_other}.
\end{proof}


Let the \emph{$\sigma$-strong} topology on $\hom(A,B)$ be generated by the seminorms $x \mapsto \omega(x^*x)$ as $\omega\in A_*^+$ varies; this is called the \emph{s-topology} in \cite{GLR_Wstar_categories}.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:inc_coms_sigmastrongly}
The inclusions of $A,B,\hom(A,B),\hom(B,A)$ into $M(A,B)$ are $\sigma$-strong-homeomorphisms onto their ranges.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
We show the case of $\hom(A,B)$, the other components being similar.  Let $(x_i)$ be a net in $\hom(A,B)$ converging $\sigma$-strongly to $0$.  Given $\omega\in M(A,B)_*^+$, in matrix form, we have that $\omega = \smattwo{\omega_A}{*}{*}{*}$ where necessarily $\omega_A \in A_*^+$.  Then
\[ \omega\left( \smattwo{0}{0}{x_i}{0}^* \smattwo{0}{0}{x_i}{0} \right)
= \omega_A(x_i^*x_i) \to 0, \]
showing that $\smattwo{0}{0}{x_i}{0} \to 0$ $\sigma$-strongly in $M(A,B)$.  Conversely, suppose that $\smattwo{0}{0}{x_i}{0} \to 0$ $\sigma$-strongly in $M(A,B)$ and let $\omega_A \in A_*^+$.  Then $\omega = \smattwo{\omega_A}{0}{0}{0}$ is positive, and so the same calculation shows that $\omega_A(x_i^*x_i) \to 0$, hence $x_i\to0$ $\sigma$-strongly in $\hom(A,B)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[{\cite[Corollary~2.9]{GLR_Wstar_categories}}]\label{corr:wscts_strlycts}
A bounded linear functional $f$ on $\hom(A,B)$ is weak$^*$-continuous if and only if it is $\sigma$-strongly continuous.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Suppose that $f\in\hom(A,B)_*$.  Let $(x_i)$ be a $\sigma$-strongly null net in $\hom(A,B)$.  Again define $f_0 \in M(A,B)_*$ by $f_0 \colon \smattwo{a}{y}{x}{b} \mapsto f(x)$.  As the weak$^*$ (aka $\sigma$-weakly) continuous functionals on a $W^*$-algebra are the same as the $\sigma$-strongly continuous functionals, \cite[Theorem~II.2.6(ii)]{TakesakiI} for example, also $f_0$ is $\sigma$-strongly continuous, and so by Lemma~\ref{lem:inc_coms_sigmastrongly}, $f(x_i) = f_0(\smattwo{0}{0}{x_i}{0}) \to 0$.  So $f$ is $\sigma$-strongly continuous.

Conversely, let $f$ be $\sigma$-strongly continuous, and form $f_0$ again, so $f_0 \in M(A,B)^*$.  We will show that $f_0$ is $\sigma$-strongly continuous, hence weak$^*$-continuous, showing that $f$ is weak$^*$-continuous.  To this end, let $(\alpha_i)$ be a $\sigma$-strongly null net in $M(A,B)$ and for each $i$ let $\alpha_i = \smattwo{a_i}{y_i}{x_i}{b_i}$.  Given $\omega_A \in A_*^+$ let $\omega = \smattwo{\omega_A}{0}{0}{0} \in M(A,B)_*^+$, so $\omega_A(a_i^*a_i + x_i^*x_i) = \omega(\alpha_i^*\alpha_i) \to 0$.  However, as $\omega_A(a_i^*a_i)\geq 0$ and $\omega_A(x_i^*x_i)\geq 0$ for each $i$, it must be that $\omega_A(x_i^*x_i) \to 0$.  So $(x_i)$ is $\sigma$-strongly null in $\hom(A,B)$, and so $f_0(\alpha_i) = f(x_i) \to 0$.
\end{proof}

Following \cite[Definition~2.11]{GLR_Wstar_categories} we say that a $*$-functor $F\colon\mc A\to\mc B$ between $W^*$-categories $\mc A$ and $\mc B$ is \emph{normal} when $F\colon A=\hom(A,A) \to F(A) = \hom(F(A), F(A))$ is a normal $*$-homomorphism, for each object $A\in\mc A$.  

\begin{proposition}[{\cite[Proposition~2.12]{GLR_Wstar_categories}}]\label{prop:meaning_of_normal_functor}
Let $\mc A, \mc B$ be $W^*$-categories, and let $F\colon\mc A\to\mc B$ be a $*$-functor.  The following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[(a)]
  \item\label{prop:meaning_of_normal_functor:one}
  $F$ is normal, that is, for each $A\in\mc A$ the $*$-homomorphism $F \colon A = \\mc A(A,A) \to \mc B(F(A), F(A)) = F(A)$ is normal;
  \item\label{prop:meaning_of_normal_functor:two}
  for each $A,B\in\mc A$ the (bounded) linear map $F \colon \mc A(A,B) \to \mc B(F(A), F(B))$ is weak$^*$-continuous.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Clearly \ref{prop:meaning_of_normal_functor:two} implies \ref{prop:meaning_of_normal_functor:one}.  Suppose \ref{prop:meaning_of_normal_functor:one} holds.  For $f \in \hom(F(A), F(B))_*$, we claim that $f \circ F \in \hom(A,B)^*$ is $\sigma$-strongly continuous.  Indeed, for $(x_i)$ a $\sigma$-strongly null net in $\hom(A,B)$, for each $\omega \in A_*^+$ we have that $\omega(x_i^*x_i) \to 0$.  So for $\omega' \in F(A)_*^+$, as $\omega' \circ F \in A_*^+$ because $F\colon A\to F(A)$ is normal, we have that $\omega'(F(x_i)^*F(x_i)) = (\omega'\circ F)(x_i^*x_i) \to 0$, and hence we conclude that $F(x_i) \to 0$ $\sigma$-strongly.  So as claimed, $(f\circ F)(x_i) = f(F(x_i)) \to 0$ as $f$ is $\sigma$-strongly continuous, by Corollary~\ref{corr:wscts_strlycts}.  Then by the lemma, also $f\circ F$ is weak$^*$-continuous, and so the Banach space adjoint $F^*$ restricts to a map $F_* \colon \hom(F(A), F(B))_*$ to $\hom(A,B)_*$, and then $(F_*)^* = F$ so $F$ is weak$^*$-continuous, showing \ref{prop:meaning_of_normal_functor:two}.
\end{proof}

Recall that a normal $*$-homomorphism between von Neumann algebras always has weak$^*$-closed range, \cite[Proposition~1.16.2.]{Sakai_Book} for example.  The following extends this.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:normal_functor_all_wsclosed}
Let $\mc A, \mc B$ be $W^*$-categories, and let $F\colon\mc A\to\mc B$ be a normal $*$-functor.  For each $A,B\in \mc A$ the image of $F \colon \mc A(A,B) \to \mc B(F(A), F(B))$ is weak$^*$-closed.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Consider the $W^*$-algebras $M(A,B)$ and $M(F(A), F(B))$.  That $F$ is a $*$-functor means that $F$ induces a $*$-homomorphism $\pi \colon M(A,B) \to M(F(A), F(B))$, and as these algebras contain weak$^*$-homeomorphic copies of $\mc A(A,B)$ and $\mc A(F(A), F(B))$, respectively, it suffices to show that $\pi(\mc A(A,B))$ is weak$^*$-closed in $M(F(A), F(B))$.  That $F$ is normal means that $\pi$ is weak$^*$-continuous, so $\pi$ has weak$^*$-closed range.  Let $(x_i)$ be a net in $\mc A(A,B)$ with $\pi(x_i) \to x \in M(F(A), F(B))$ weak$^*$, so $x$ is in the image of $\pi$, say $x = \pi(y)$ for some $y\in M(A,B)$.  Set $y' = 1_B y 1_A \in \mc A(A,B)$ so $\pi(y') = 1_{F(B)} \pi(y) 1_{F(A)} = 1_{F(B)} x 1_{F(A)} = \lim_i 1_{F(B)} x_i 1_{F(A)} = \lim_i \pi( 1_B x_i 1_A) = \lim_i \pi(x_i) = x$.  So $x\in\pi(\mc A(A,B))$ as required.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[{\cite[Proposition~2.13]{GLR_Wstar_categories}}]\label{prop:func_to_Hilb}
Given a $W^*$-category $\mc A$ and any set $I$ of objects in $\mc A$, there is a functor $F \colon \mc A \to \catHilb$ which is weak$^*$-continuous on morphism spaces, and isometric on $\hom(A,B)$ for each $A,B\in I$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Consider the construction of a faithful functor $\mc A \to \catHilb$ given by Theorem~\ref{thm:exists_faithful_rep} (or the arguments in \cite{GLR_Wstar_categories,mitchener_cstar_cats} using the GNS construction).  When we choose $\pi \colon A \to \mc B(H)$ to be also normal, the resulting $*$-functor $F\colon\mc A \to \catHilb$ has that $F = \pi$ on $A$.  We now verify that also $F \colon B \to \mc B(F(B)) = \mc B(\mc A(A,B)\otimes H)$ is normal, for each object $B$.

To do this, it suffices to show that if $(b_i)$ is a \emph{bounded} net in $B$ converging weak$^*$ to $0$, then $F(b_i) \to 0$ weak$^*$ in $\mc B(\mc A(A,B)\otimes H)$.  We are not aware of a canonical reference for this claim, but it's shown in \cite[Section~10]{Daws_multipliers}, for example.\footnote{There must be some canonical reference, say in a textbook, for this!}
As then also $(F(b_i))$ is a bounded net, it suffices to check that
\[ 0 = \lim_i \bigl( y\otimes\eta \big| b_ix\otimes\xi \bigr)
= \lim_i (\eta|\pi(y^*b_ix)\xi) \qquad (x,y\in\mc A(A,B), \xi,\eta\in H). \]
As $\pi$ is normal, it hence suffices to show that $y^* b_i x \to 0$ weak$^*$ in $A$, for each $x,y\in\mc A(A,B)$.  This follows immediately by using the $W^*$-algebra $M(A,B)$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{corr:finite_Ai_gives_MA_i_Wstar}
For any finite collection $(A_i)_{i=1}^n$ of objects of $\mc A$, we have that $M(A_1,\cdots,A_n)$ is a $W^*$-algebra.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
As $I = \{ A_i \}$ is finite, $M(A_i)$ is a $C^*$-algebra.  Apply the functor $F\colon \mc A \to \catHilb$ from Proposition~\ref{prop:func_to_Hilb}.  Given $i,j$, as $F\colon\hom(A_i,A_j) \to \hom(F(A_i), F(A_j))$ is weak$^*$-continuous and isometric, it is a weak$^*$-weak$^*$-homeomorphism onto its range.  The $C^*$-algebra $M(\{ F(A_i) \})$ is, by definition, represented on a direct sum of Hilbert spaces, say $H_1\oplus\cdots\oplus H_n$.  As each component of $M(\{ F(A_i) \})$ is weak$^*$-closed, also $M(\{ F(A_i) \})$ is weak$^*$-closed and is hence a $W^*$-algebra (indeed, is unital, so a von Neumann algebra.)  As $F$ is isometric and a weak$^*$-weak$^*$-homeomorphism on each component, it induces a weak$^*$-continuous $*$-isomorphism $M(A_i) \to M(\{ F(A_i) \})$, and so $M(A_i)$ is a $W^*$-algebra.
\end{proof}



\subsection{Self-dual modules}

For a $C^*$-category $\mc A$ it is clear that $\hom(A,B)$ is a Hilberty $C^*$-module over $A$, see Remark~\ref{rem:to_modules}.  When $\mc A$ is $W^*$ we can say more, as we now explain, expanding upon the end of \cite[Section~2]{GLR_Wstar_categories}.

A Hilbert $C^*$-module $E$ over a $C^*$-algebra $A$ is \emph{self-dual} if every bounded module map $t \colon E \to A$ (so $t(x\cdot a) = t(x)a$ for $x\in E, a\in A$) is of the form $t(x) = (t_0|x)$ for some $t_0\in E$; see \cite{paschke_inner_prod_mods} for example.  It is shown in \cite[Proposition~3.8]{paschke_inner_prod_mods} that when $E$ is a self-dual module over a $W^*$-algebra we have that $E$ is isometrically a dual space.  Furthermore, then $\mc L(E)$ is a $W^*$-algebra, \cite[Proposition~3.10]{paschke_inner_prod_mods}, and $E$ admits a polar-decomposition theorem, \cite[Proposition~3.11]{paschke_inner_prod_mods}.

\begin{proposition}[{\cite[Proposition~2.14]{GLR_Wstar_categories}}]\label{prop:Wstar_means_hom_selfdual}
Let $\mc A$ be a $W^*$-category, and let $A,B,C \in \mc A$.  Given a bounded $A$-module map $T\colon\hom(A,B) \to \hom(A,C)$ (so $T(xa) = T(x)a$ for $x\in\hom(A,B), a\in A$) there is $x_0 \in \hom(B,C)$ with $T(x) = x_0 x$ for each $x\in\hom(A,B)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
This is attributed to Roberts, using ideas of Rieffel, in \cite{GLR_Wstar_categories}.  Instead, we give a proof motivated by \cite[Lemma~8.5.4]{BlecherLeMerdy_Book}.\footnote{If we don't do so later, give a more precise reference here.}
Let $B_0$ be the closure of $B_{00} = \lin\{ x y^* : x,y\in \hom(A,B) \} \subseteq B$ so $B_0$ is a $C^*$-algebra (actually an ideal in $B$) and so has an approximate identity, $(e_i)$ say.  Similar to \cite[page~5]{Lance_HilbModsBook}, for $x\in\hom(A,B)$ we have
\[ \lim_i (x - e_i x)(x - e_i x)^*
= \lim_i xx^* - e_i xx^* - xx^*e_i + e_ixx^*e_i = 0, \]
as $xx^*\in B_0$.  So $e_ix \to x$ in norm in $\hom(A,B)$.  By approximation, for each $i$ we may suppose that $e_i$ is actually in $B_{00}$.  Given $x,y,z\in\hom(A,B)$, as $T$ is an $A$-module map, $T(y)z^* x = T(yz^*x)$, so given $i$, if we have $e_i = \sum_{j=1}^n y_j z_j^*$, then setting $x_i = \sum_j T(y_j) z_j^* \in \hom(B,C)$, we see that $x_i x = T(e_ix)$ for $x\in\hom(A,B)$.  Notice also that for each $j$, we have $\lim_k e_k z_j = z_j$ so $\lim_k z_j^* e_k = z_j^*$ and hence $\lim_k x_i e_k = x_i$.  So $\|x_i\| = \limsup_k \|x_i e_k\| = \limsup_k \|T(e_i e_k)\| \leq \|T\|$.  Hence $(x_i)$ is a bounded net in $\hom(B,C)$ with $\lim_i x_i x = \lim_i T(e_ix) = T(x)$ for each $x\in\hom(A,B)$.

All of this holds in a $C^*$-category, but when additionally $\mc A$ is $W^*$, we may let $x_0 \in \hom(B,C)$ be a weak$^*$-accumulation point of the bounded net $(x_i)$.  By Corollary~\ref{corr:sep_ws_cty}, the composition $\hom(B,C) \times \hom(A,B) \to \hom(A,C)$ is separately weak$^*$-continuous, and so $x_0 x = \lim_i x_i x = T(x)$ for each $x\in\hom(A,B)$, as required.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[{\cite[Proposition~2.15]{GLR_Wstar_categories}}]\label{prop:self-dual_to_wstar}
Let $\mc A$ be a $C^*$-category.  The following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}[(a)]
  \item\label{prop:self-dual_to_wstar:one}
  $\mc A$ is a $W^*$-category;
  \item\label{prop:self-dual_to_wstar:two}
  each $A\in\mc A$ is a $W^*$-algebra, and each hom space $\hom(A,B)$ is self-dual over $A$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
When \ref{prop:self-dual_to_wstar:two} holds, each $\hom(A,A)$ is isometrically a dual space, and by Paschke's result, \cite[Proposition~3.8]{paschke_inner_prod_mods}, each $\hom(A,B)$ is also isometrically a dual space, so \ref{prop:self-dual_to_wstar:one} holds.  The converse is Proposition~\ref{prop:Wstar_means_hom_selfdual}.
\end{proof}


\subsection{Functor categories and commutants}

The following is a little observation not in \cite{GLR_Wstar_categories}.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:func_cat_wstar}
Let $\mc A$ be a small $C^*$-category and let $\mc B$ be a $W^*$-category.  Then the functor category $(\mc A,\mc B)$ is a $W^*$-category.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
This continues from the idea of the proof of Proposition~\ref{prop:functor_cat}.  Given functors $F,G\colon\mc A\to\mc B$ the hom space is $\hom(F,G) = \{ \alpha \colon F \Rightarrow G \}$.  As $\alpha \colon F\Rightarrow G$ has components $(\alpha_A)_{A\in\mc A}$ where $\alpha_A \colon F(A) \to G(A)$, and as $A$ is small, we have an isometric embedding
\[ \hom(F,G) \to \ell^\infty - \bigoplus_{A\in\mc A} \mc B(F(A), G(A)). \]
As $\mc B$ is $W^*$ each hom space $\mc B(F(A), G(A))$ is a dual space, and so the right-hand-side is a dual space, with isometric predual $\ell^1-\bigoplus_A \mc B(F(A), G(A))_*$.  We claim that the image of $\hom(F,G)$ is weak$^*$-closed, and so $\hom(F,G)$ is isometrically a dual space, which gives the result.

By the Krein--Smulian Theorem, \cite[Theorem~12.1]{Conway_FunctionalAnalysisBook} for example, it suffices to show that if $(\alpha_i)$ is a bounded net in $\hom(F,G)$ converging weak$^*$ to $\alpha$ in the $\ell^\infty$-direct-sum, then $\alpha$ is also in $\hom(F,G)$.  For each $i$ let $\alpha_i = (\alpha^{(i)}_A)$ and similarly let $\alpha = (\alpha_A)$.  As the net is bounded, weak$^*$-convergence is equivalent to $\alpha^{(i)}_A \to \alpha_A$ weak$^*$ in $\mc B(F(A), G(A))$, independently for each $A$.  We need to show that given $x\in\mc A(A,B)$ we have that $G(x) \circ \alpha_A = \alpha_B \circ F(x)$.  However, by Corollary~\ref{corr:sep_ws_cty}, we have that $G(x) \alpha_A = \lim_i G(x) \alpha^{(i)}_A = \lim_i \alpha^{(i)}_B F(x) = \alpha_B F(x)$, the limits in the relevant weak$^*$-topologies, and using that each $\alpha_i$ is a natural transformation.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}[{\cite[Definition~4.1]{GLR_Wstar_categories}}]\label{defn:commutant}
Let $\mc A$ be a $C^*$-catgeory and $F\colon\mc A\to\catHilb$ be a $*$-functor.  The \emph{commutant} of $F$ is $F' = \hom(F,F)$ the space of natural transformations $F\Rightarrow F$, which is a von Neumann algebra.  The \emph{bicommutant} $F''$ is the $W^*$-category having the same objects as $\mc A$ and
\[ \hom(A,B)_{F''} = \{ x\colon F(A)\to F(B) : \alpha_B x = x\alpha_A \ (\alpha\in F') \}. \]
\end{definition}

That $\hom(F,F)$ is a von Neumann algebra follows immediately from Proposition~\ref{prop:func_cat_wstar}, for example.  Following \cite[Section~4]{GLR_Wstar_categories} we situate these ideas in a wider context.  Given a set $S$ (typically $S$ is the collection of objects of $\mc A$ where here $\mc A$ is assumed small) let $F$ be a field of Hilbert spaces, that is, a map from $S$ to the collection of Hilbert spaces.  Define $\field(F)$ to be the collection of bounded maps $\alpha \colon S \to \mc B(F(A))$ turned into a $W^*$-algebra for the isomorphism with the $\ell^\infty$ direct sum of the spaces $\mc B(F(A))$.  Define $\catHilbField{S}$ to be the $W^*$-category whose objects are $(F(A))_{A\in S}$ and with $\hom(F(A), F(B))$ the space of bounded linear maps $F(A) \to F(B)$.  (This is not quite a subcategory of $\catHilb$ as we could take $F(A) = H$ a fixed Hilbert space for all $A$, for example.)  

One can then define the following.  By writing $X \subseteq\catHilbField{S}$ we mean that for each $A,B\in S$ we make a choice, denoted $X\cap\hom(A,B)$, of a subset of $\hom(F(A), F(B))$, which could be $\{0\}$.  Then define
\[ X' = \{ \alpha \in \field(S) : \alpha_B x = x\alpha_A \ (x\in X\cap\hom(A,B), A,B\in S) \}. \]
When $X=X^*$, so that $X \cap \hom(B,A) = (X\cap\hom(A,B))^*$, we see that $X'$ is a $W^*$-subalgebra of $\field(S)$ (the weak$^*$-closed property follows by separate weak$^*$-continuity).
Given $Y \subseteq \field(S)$ a subset, define $Y' \subseteq \catHilbField{S}$ by
\[ Y'\cap\hom(A,B) = \{ x \in \hom(F(A), F(B)) : \alpha_B x = x\alpha_A \ (\alpha\in Y) \}. \]
When $Y=Y^*$, we similarly see that $Y'$ is a $W^*$-subcategory of $\field(S)$.  This gives a sort of Galois Connection: if $X_1 \subseteq X_2$ then $X_1' \supseteq X_2'$, and similarly if $Y_1 \subseteq Y_2$ then $Y_1' \supseteq Y_2'$.  Similarly, $X \subseteq X''$ and $Y \subseteq Y''$.  Finally, we see how these definitions generalise the ideas of Definition~\ref{defn:commutant}.

\begin{proposition}[{\cite[Theorem~4.2]{GLR_Wstar_categories}}]
Let $M \subseteq \field(F)$ be a unital $*$-subalgebra.  Then $M''$ is the $W^*$-algebra generated by $M$.  Let $\mc A$ be a $*$-subcategory of $\catHilbField{S}$ with the same objects (i.e.\@ a full subcategory).  Then $\mc A''$ is the $W*$-subcategory generated by $\mc A$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Define $F(S)$ to be the Hilbert space direct sum of all the spaces $F(A)$.  Then $\field(F)$ acts ``diagonally'' on $F(S)$, and so as $M$ is a unital $*$-subalgebra, von Neumann's bicommutant theorem gives that $M''$ is the $W^*$-algebra generated by $M$.  Similarly $\mc A$ acts on $F(S)$ where a morphism $x\in\mc A(A,B)$ acts in a canonical way between the subspaces $F(A)$ and $F(B)$.  In particular, $1_A \in \mc A(A)$ is the projection of $F(S)$ on $F(A)$, for each $A$.    Let $A_0$ be the resulting subset of $F(S)$, which is seen to be $*$-closed, and closed under multiplication, as $\mc A$ is a $*$-category, so $\lin A_0$ is a $*$-algebra.  Let $\alpha\in A_0'$, and regard $1_B \alpha 1_A$ as a map $F(A) \to F(B)$, for each $A,B\in S$.  However, then $\alpha 1_A = 1_A \alpha$ for each $\alpha$, so $\alpha$ is ``diagonal'', $1_B \alpha 1_A = 0$ unless $A=B$, and hence we can regard $\alpha \in \field(S)$ with $\alpha_A = 1_A \alpha 1_A$.  We now see that $A_0'$ is identified with $\mc A'$.  While $\lin A_0$ may not be unital, as it contains $1_A$ for each $A\in S$, the weak$^*$-closure of $\lin A_0$ is unital, and so $A_0''$ agrees with the weak$^*$-closure of $\lin A_0$.

Given $x\in \mc A'' \cap \hom(A,B)$ regarded as an operator on $F(S)$ we have $x\in A_0''$.  So there is a net $(x_i)$ in $\lin A_0$ converging to $x$ in the weak operator topology.  Then $1_B x_i 1_A \to 1_B x 1_A$ weakly, hence weak$^*$, in $\hom(F_A, F_B)$.  So $\mc A'' \cap \hom(A,B)$ is contained in the weak$^*$-closure of $\mc A(A,B) \subseteq \hom(F(A), F(B))$.  The other inclusion is clear, as $\mc A(A,B) \subseteq \mc A'' \cap \hom(A,B)$ and $\mc A'' \cap \hom(A,B)$ is weak$^*$-closed, and so we have equality.  Hence $\mc A''$ is the $W*$-subcategory generated by $\mc A$.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
Let $\mc A$ be a $C^*$-catgeory and $F \colon \mc A \to \catHilb$ a $*$-functor.  Then $F''$ is the $W^*$-category generated by $\mc A$ and $F$.  That is, $F''$ has the same objects as $\mc A$, and $F''(A,B) \subseteq \hom(F(A), F(B))$ is the weak$^*$-closure of $\{ F(x) : x\in \mc A(A,B) \}$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{corollary}\label{corr:normal_functor_equals_bicomm}
Let $\mc A$ be a $W^*$-catgeory and $F \colon \mc A \to \catHilb$ a normal $*$-functor.  Then $F''$ equals the image of $F$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
By Proposition~\ref{prop:meaning_of_normal_functor}, the linear map $F\colon \mc A(A,B) \to \hom(F(A), F(B))$ is weak$^*$-weak$^*$-continuous.  By Proposition~\ref{prop:normal_functor_all_wsclosed}, $\{ F(x) : x\in\mc A(A,B) \} \subseteq \hom(F(A), F(B))$ is weak$^*$-closed, hence equal to $F''(A,B)$.
\end{proof}




\section{Woronowicz's take on \texorpdfstring{$W^*$}{WStar}-categories}

Woronowicz has a different definition of a \emph{$W^*$-category} first presented in the lecture \cite{Woronowicz_DualityCStar} with proofs deferred to a preprint which was apparently never published.  The ideas were re-visited (in a narrower context, but with full proofs) in \cite[Section~5]{Woronowicz_GenUnbounded}.  We thank Piotr So{\l}tan for bibliographic references here.

For Woronowicz, a $W^*$-category consists of a \emph{fixed} Hilbert space $H$ and collections of operators in $\mc B(H)$, with axioms giving a $W^*$-category.  There are then further axioms: IV which is a sort of ``uniqueness'' condition (which I find a little hard to formalise below), V which tells us about ``subobjects'' and VI which says that direct sums exist.  In this section we give some indication of how one might treat these ideas more in the language of $W^*$-categories and the ideas of \cite{GLR_Wstar_categories}.  The end goal is to be able to reconstruct a $C^*$-algebra from it's representation theory, and to perhaps characterise which $W^*$-categories arise from representations of $C^*$-algebras.

\begin{definition}\label{defn:subobj_sums}
Let $\mc A$ be a $W^*$-category.  We say that $\mc A$ has \emph{all subobjects} if whenever $A\in\mc A$ and $e\in \mc A(A)$ is a projection, there is $A_e \in \mc A$ and an isometry $u\in\mc A(A_e, A)$ (meaning $u^*u=1$) with $uu^* = e$.

We say that $\mc A$ has \emph{all direct sums} if whenever $(A_i)_{i\in I}$ is a family of objects in $\mc A$, there is $A\in\mc A$ and isometries $u_i \in \mc A(A_i, A)$ with $\sum_i u_i u_i^* = 1$ (the possibly infinite sum converging weak$^*$).
\end{definition}

Consider the subobjects condition.  As $e\in\mc A(A)$ is a projection, $e \mc A(A) e$ is a $W^*$-algebra (with $e$ being its unit) sometimes called the \emph{reduced} $W^*$-algebra.   The map $A_e \to A; a \mapsto u a u^*$ is a $*$-homomorphism (as $u^*u=1$) into $e\mc A(A)e$ as $eu = uu^*u = u$ and $u^*e = u^*uu^* = u^*$.  Conversely, if $x\in e\mc A(A)e$ then set $a = u^*xu \in \mc A(A_e)$ so that $uau^* = uu^* x uu^* = exe = x$.  Thus the object $A_e$ is somehow ``canonically'' isomorphic to $e \mc A(A) e$.

Consider now the condition of having all direct sums.  The sum $\sum_i u_i u_i^*=1$ is in $\mc A(A)$ a $W^*$-algebra: each $e_i = u_i u_i^*$ is a projection, and as they sum to $1$ they must be mutually orthogonal.  Again $\mc A(A_i) \to \mc A(A); a \mapsto u_i a u_i^*$ is a $*$-isomorphism from $\mc A(A_i)$ to $e_i \mc A(A) e_i$.  Hence $\mc A(A)$ contains as a ``diagonal'' all the $W^*$-algebras $\mc A(A_i)$.

\begin{example}
The motivating example in this whole section is $\Rep(A)$, see Example~\ref{eg:RepA}.  This has subobjects, as given $e\in\hom(\pi)$ a projection, as $e\pi(a) = \pi(a)e$ for $a\in A$, the map $A\to \hom(e(H_\pi)); a \mapsto e\pi(a)e$ is a $*$-representation, non-degenerate, say giving $\pi_e$ on $e(H_\pi)$.  The inclusion $u \colon e(H_\pi) \to H_\pi$ is an isometry and $u \pi_e(a) = u e\pi(a)|_{e(H_\pi)} = \pi(a) e|_{e(H_\pi)} = \pi(a) u$ so $u\in\hom(\pi_e, \pi)$.

We also have all direct sums: given $(\pi_i)$ let $H$ be the Hilbert space direct sum of the $H_{\pi_i}$ with $u_i$ the inclusion, an isometry.  Let $\pi$ be the direct sum of the $\pi_i$, so a non-degenerate $*$-homomorphism, with $u_i \in \hom(\pi_i, \pi)$.
\end{example}

Actually, having all direct sums is perhaps too strong a condition, as $I$ could be a very large set.  We indicate this below with examples.  We ultimately wish to compare morphism spaces, and so we introduce a special class of $*$-functors.  Let $\mc A$ be a $C^*$-category and $H$ a fixed Hilbert space.  We consider functors $F \colon \mc A \to \catHilb$ where $F(A) = H$ for all $A$.

\begin{example}
%Again consider $\Rep(A)$, so a functor $F \colon \Rep(A) \to \catHilb$ with this property.  So $H$ is a fixed Hilbert space, and we have $*$-maps $F \colon \hom(\pi, \pi') \to \mc B(H)$ which respects the product.  
Fix a Hilbert space $H$ and let $\Rep(A,H)$ be the collection of all representations of a $C^*$-algebra $A$ on this fixed $H$.  We may, or may not, wish to only allow non-degenerate representations.  Then each $\hom(\pi,\pi')$ is by definitions a subspace of $\hom(H)$, and so the identity functor gives a normal $*$-functor $\Rep(A,H) \to \catHilb$ sending all objects to $H$.
\end{example}

Notice that $\Rep(A,H)$ has subojects, but not all direct sums (as $I$ could be too large to allow $\sum_i u_i u_i^*=1$ in $\hom(H)$).  However, we will have all direct sums where the cardinately of $I$ does not exceed the dimension of $H$ (assuming $H$ is infinite-dimensional).

Given such a $*$-functor $F$ the commutant $F'$, Definition~\ref{defn:commutant}, is the space of natural transformations $F \Rightarrow F$, that is, families $(\alpha_A)_{A\in\mc A}$ with $\alpha_A\in\hom(H)$ and $F(x) \alpha_A = \alpha_B F(x)$ for each $x\in\mc A(A,B)$.  This is essentially the same as Woronowicz's notion of an \emph{operator function}.  Then Corollary~\ref{corr:normal_functor_equals_bicomm} shows the same as \cite[Theorem~2]{Woronowicz_DualityCStar}, namely
\[ \{ F(x) : x\in\mc A(A,B) \} = \{ x\colon H\to H : x \alpha_A = \alpha_B x \ (\alpha\in F') \}. \]

We now suppose that the object set of $\mc A$ carries a topology, and we consider those $\alpha\in F'$ which are continuous in a certain sense:
\[ C(F') = \{ \alpha \in F' : \mc A \to H, A \mapsto \alpha_A\xi \text{ and } A \mapsto \alpha_A^*\xi \text{ are continuous for each } \xi\in H \}. \]

\begin{proposition}
We have that $C(F')$ is a unital $C^*$-algebra.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
By definition, $C(F')$ is unital and $*$-closed.  Given $\alpha,\beta\in F'$ let $(A_i)$ be a net in $\mc A$ converging to $A$.  For $\epsilon>0$ there is $i_0$ so that $i\geq i_0 \implies \| \alpha_{A_i}\xi-\xi\|<\epsilon$ and $\|\beta_{A_i}\xi-\xi\| < \|\alpha\|^{-1}\epsilon$.  Then for $i\geq i_0$ we have
\[ \| \alpha_{A_i} \beta_{A_i} \xi - \xi \| \leq \|\alpha_{A_i}\beta_{A_i}\xi - \alpha_{A_i}\xi\| + \|\alpha_{A_i}\xi-\xi\|
\leq \|\alpha\| \|\beta_{A_i}\xi-\xi\| + \|\alpha_{A_i}\xi-\xi\| < 2\epsilon. \]
So $\alpha_{A_i} \beta_{A_i} \xi \to \xi$ and similarly for the adjoints.  So $C(F')$ is a $*$-algebra.

Let $(\alpha_n)$ be an absolutely summable sequence in $C(F')$.  Then $\alpha = \sum_n \alpha_n$ exists in the Banach space $F'$, and it is routine to check that $A \mapsto \sum_n \alpha^{(n)}_A\xi$ is continuous, for each $\xi\in H$ (and for the adjoints).  So $C(F')$ is complete, hence a unital $C^*$-algebra.
\end{proof}

\clearpage
\section{Tensor \texorpdfstring{$C^*$}{CStar}-Categories}

Two modern sources are \cite{EGNO_TensorCategories}\footnote{A book available on Etingof's website, 2026 url: \url{https://math.mit.edu/~etingof/egnobookfinal.pdf}.  Note that the bibliography is ordered in an extremely strange way, IMHO!} and \cite{NT_CQGsBook}.  In the Operator Algebras world, the article \cite{LR_Theory_dimension} is often cited as a foundational article, but it's notable that \cite{EGNO_TensorCategories} doesn't list this paper in its bibliography!\footnote{While I am very, very far from an expert here, it seems to me that, as for Quantum Groups, there is quite a lot of parallel development of the theory here.}  The book \cite{EGNO_TensorCategories} is comprehensive, and readable, but algebraically focused; \cite{NT_CQGsBook} is quicker in pace, but much more tightly focused on topics we'll explore here.

There is also a graphical language which gets used a lot in this area.  I find \cite{HV_CatsForQuantumTheory} to be a nice introduction\footnote{Although I find this book to be slightly unclear about very technical issues.  A very related set of lectures notes can be found at \url{https://www.cs.ox.ac.uk/files/10510/notes.pdf} at least in June 2026.} (with emphasis on Hilbert spaces).  See also the nice survery\footnote{Available at \url{https://arxiv.org/abs/0908.3347}} \cite{Selinger_survery_graphical} and \cite{JS_GeometryTensorCalculus}.

Terminology is a bit unclear in this area.  We will essentially say "tensor category" or "monoidal category" interchangably\footnote{Anecdotally, searching for ``tensor category'' leads to more useful, to me, lecture notes etc.\@ than the search term ``monoidal catgeory''.}, but note that this clashes with \cite{EGNO_TensorCategories}.  A $C^*$-tensor category is, roughly, a $C^*$-category with a categorical tensor product: for $\catHilb$ it is exactly the usual tensor product of Hilbert spaces.

A \emph{$C^*$-tensor category} is a $C^*$-category $\mc A$ equipped with a bilinear functor $\otimes \colon \mc A\times\mc A \to \mc A; (A,B) \mapsto A \otimes B$, natural unitary isomorphisms
\[ \alpha = \alpha_{A,B,C} \colon (A\otimes B)\otimes C \to A \otimes (B\otimes C), \]
called the \emph{associativity morphisms}, an object $1$ called the \emph{unit object}, and natural unitary isomorphisms
\[ \lambda_A \colon 1 \otimes A \to A, \quad \rho_A \colon A\otimes 1 \to A, \]
called the \emph{unit isomorphisms}.

That $\otimes$ is a functor means that for $x\in\mc A(A,A')$ and $y\in\mc A(B,B')$ we have an arrow $x\otimes y \in \mc A(A\otimes B, A'\otimes B')$, and $(x,y) \mapsto x\otimes y$ is bilinear.  Furthermore, we have $(x\otimes y)\circ (x'\otimes y') = (x\circ x') \otimes (y\circ y')$.

That $\alpha$ is natural means that for $x\in\mc A(A,A')$ we have that $\alpha_{A',B,C} \circ ((x\otimes 1_B)\otimes 1_C) = (x\otimes 1_{B\otimes C})\circ \alpha_{A,B,C}$, and similarly for morphisms applied to the $B$ and $C$ objects.  Similarly for $\lambda$ and $\rho$.

These need to satisfy the following axioms:
\begin{enumerate}[(1), start=6]
  \item\label{cst-axioms:6} the ``pentagonal diagram''
  \[ \begin{tikzcd}
    & ((A\otimes B)\otimes C) \otimes D \ar[ld, "\alpha_{A,B,C}\otimes\id_D"']   \ar[rd, "\alpha_{A\otimes B,C,D}"] \\
    (A\otimes (B\otimes C))\otimes D \ar[d, "\alpha_{A,B\otimes C,D}"'] && (A\otimes B) \otimes (C\otimes D) \ar[d, "\alpha_{A,B,C\otimes D}"] \\
    A \otimes ((B\otimes C)\otimes D) \ar[rr, "\id_A\otimes\alpha_{B,C,D}"'] && A \otimes (B\otimes(C\otimes D)),
  \end{tikzcd} \]
  commutes for all objects $A,B,C,D$;
  \item\label{cst-axioms:7} $\lambda_1 = \rho_1 \colon 1\otimes 1\to 1$ and the ``triangle diagram''
  \[ \begin{tikzcd}
    (A\otimes 1)\otimes B \ar[rr, "\alpha_{A,1,B}"] \ar[rd, "\rho_A\otimes 1_B"'] && A\otimes (1\otimes B) \ar[ld, "1_A\otimes \lambda_B"] \\
    &   A\otimes B,
  \end{tikzcd} \]
  commutes for all objects $A,B$;
  \item\label{cst-axioms:8} $(x\otimes y)^* = x^* \otimes y^*$ for all morphisms $x,y$.
\end{enumerate}

Hopefully axiom \ref{cst-axioms:8} is clear.  The idea behind the associativity morphisms is that we want the tensor product to be associative, but it is too much to expect that this is exactly true: we have that it is true up to isomorphism.  Then axiom \ref{cst-axioms:6} expresses that all the ways to tensor four objects together are also, up to isomorphism, the same.  Similarly, the unit object should act as the identity under the tensor product, but only up to isomorphism.

The book \cite{EGNO_TensorCategories} has a weaker definition, see Section~2.1, and then shows in Section~2.2 that the definition is equivalent to that given here (which is the more standard definition).  As expressed at the end of \cite[Section~2.2]{EGNO_TensorCategories}, the unit object and isomorphisms in some sense have ``little choice'' involved, and it's not quite necessary to worry about them too hard.

I find fully understanding what the pentagonal diagram is trying to capture to be hard, though there is some useful discussion in \cite[Section~2.9]{EGNO_TensorCategories}.  The answer seems to lie in Mac Lane's ``coherence theorem'', see \cite[Section~VII.2]{MacLane_CFTWM2} for example.  This, roughly, says that any diagram involving the associativity morphisms and unit isomorphisms will commute, given the axioms above.



\subsection{Examples}

We develop some examples with a little care.

\begin{example}\label{eg:hilb_tensor}
We turn $\catHilb$ into a $C^*$-tensor category by using the usual Hilbert space tensor product.
