Whatever your favourite construction of the tensor product is, you will have that $(H_1\otimes H_2)\otimes H_3 \to H_1\otimes (H_2\otimes H_3); (\xi_1\otimes\xi_2)\otimes\xi_3 \mapsto \xi_1\otimes(\xi_2\otimes\xi_3)$ is a unitary isomorphism, though not an actual \emph{equality}.  This gives the associativity morphisms, which are easily seen to satisfy the pentagonal diagram.

The unit object is $\mathbb C$, and again $\lambda_H$ and $\rho_H$ are obvious, and satisfy the triangle diagram.

Restricting to finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces gives a sub-category $\catHilbFD$.
\end{example}

\begin{example}\label{eq:hilb_skel_tensor}
Let $\catMat$ have objects $\mathbb N_0$, and morphisms $\hom(n, m) = \mathbb M_{m,n}$ the space of $m\times n$ matrices.  Composition is matrix multiplication, the adjoint is the hermitian conjugate, and the norm on $\mathbb M_n$ is given by the operator norm (so $\|x^*x\|$ is the value of the largest eigenvalue of $x^*x$).  This gives a $C^*$-category: some intuition comes from thinking of the object $n\in\mathbb N_0$ as really being $\mathbb C^n$ with the standard inner-product.  In what follows, it is helpful to index matrix entries from $0$, not $1$, and so $x\in \mathbb M_{m,n}$ has entries $x = (x_{i,j})_{0\leq i<m, 0\leq j<n}$.

We define $n\otimes m = nm$ the product, and given $x\in\hom(n_1, m_1) = \mathbb M_{m_1, n_1}, y\in\hom(n_2,m_2) = \mathbb M_{m_2, n_2}$ we define $x\otimes y \in \hom(n_1n_2, m_1m_2) = \mathbb M_{m_1m_2, n_1n_2}$ by using the ``lexicographic ordering'':
\[ (x\otimes y)_{i,j} = x_{i_1, j_1} y_{i_2,j_2} \quad\text{where } i = i_1m_2 + i_2, j=j_1n_2 + j_2. \]
This is, we fix an isomorphism $\mathbb M_{m_1, n_1} \otimes \mathbb M_{m_2, n_2} \cong \mathbb M_{m_1m_2, n_1n_2}$.

Axiom \ref{cst-axioms:8} is clear.  We define the associativity morphisms $\alpha$ to all be the identity maps, and then \ref{cst-axioms:6} is clear.  The unit object is $1$ and define the unit isomorphisms to be the identity maps.  Then axiom \ref{cst-axioms:7} holds.

Define an inclusion functor $G \colon \catMat \to \catHilbFD$ by $G(n) = \mathbb C^n$ (with the standard inner-product) and for $x\in\hom(n,m) = \mathbb M_{m,n}$ let $G(x) \colon \mathbb C^n \to \mathbb C^m$ be the linear map induced by $x$.  This is a $*$-functor, and $G$ is full and faithful (bijective on hom sets).  For each $H\in\catHilbFD$ fix an orthonormal basis, equivalently, fix a unitary $u_H \colon H \to \mathbb C^{\dim H}$; when $H=\mathbb C^n$ with the standard inner-product, we choose $u_H$ to be the identity.  This defines a functor $F \colon \catHilbFD \to \catMat$ by $F(H) = \dim(H)$ and for $x\colon H\to K$ set $F(x)$ to be the matrix of the linear map $u_K x u_H^*$.  Then $F$ is again full and faithful.  Notice that $FG$ is the identity.

We define a natural isomorphism $\eta \colon 1 \Rightarrow GF$ to have components $\eta_H \colon H \to GF(H) = \mathbb C^{\dim H}$ given by $\eta_H = u_H$.  We have naturality as for $x\colon H\to K$ we have $GF(x) \circ \eta_H = u_K x u_H^* \circ u_H = U_K x = \eta_K \circ x$.  So we have an equivalence of categories.  Furthermore, as $\epsilon=1 \colon FG \Rightarrow 1$, we have $\epsilon_{F(H)} \circ F(\eta_H) = u_{\mathbb C^{\dim H}} \eta_H u_H^* = u_{\mathbb C^{\dim H}}$ which we choose to be the identity matrix.  Similarly, $G(\epsilon_n) \circ \eta_{G(n)} = u_{\mathbb C^n}$ the identity.  So \eqref{eq:triangle_iden} holds and we have an adjunction as well as an equivalence, compare Proposition~\ref{prop:adjunctions_and_equivs}.
\end{example}

\begin{definition}
A $C^*$-tensor category is \emph{strict} if the associativity morphisms and the unit isomorphisms are all identity maps.
\end{definition}

The example above shows that $\catMat$ is strict, and $\catMat$ can be thought of as a ``strictified'' version of $\catHilbFD$.  A result of Mac Lane shows that every $C^*$-tensor category is equivalent (but perhaps not isomorphic) to a strict $C^*$-tensor category.  See \cite[page~38]{NT_CQGsBook} or \cite[Section~2.8]{EGNO_TensorCategories} for further discussions.  The paper \cite{LR_Theory_dimension} only works with strict categories, but this seems a little restrictive to us, as examples are often not strict, and the proof of Mac Lane's theorem is not very ``constructive''.

\begin{example}\label{eg:tensor_bimods}
Let $A,B$ be $C^*$-algebras.  A \emph{correspondance} from $B$ to $A$ is a Hilbert $C^*$-module $E$ over $A$ together with a $*$-homomorphism $\pi \colon B \to \mc L(E)$ (almost always we assume $\pi$ to be non-degenerate).  This is a weaker notion than that of a $B$-$A$-bimodule (see \cite{BMS_quasimults}) in that $E$ is not assumed to be a left Hilbert module over $B$.

Given a Hilbert $C^*$-module $F$ over $B$, we can form the interior tensor product $F\otimes_B E$, which is the separation completion of $F \otimes E$ for the $A$-valued pre-inner-product
\[ (x_1 \otimes y_1 | x_2\otimes y_2) = (y_1|\pi((x_1|x_2))y_2) \qquad (x_1,x_2\in F, y_1,y_2\in E). \]
When $F$ is a $C$-$B$-correspondance, $F\otimes_BE$ has the structure of a $C$-$A$-correspondance.  Regard $B$ as a (non-degenerate) $B$-$B$-correspondance, and then we see that $B \otimes_B E \to E; b \otimes x \mapsto \pi(b)x$ extends to an isometry, which is a unitary when $\pi$ is non-degenerate.  Similarly, $E \otimes_A A; x \otimes a \mapsto x\cdot a$ is an isometry, and a unitary, as $\lin\{ x \cdot a : x\in E, a\in A \}$ is dense in $E$, compare \cite[page~5]{Lance_HilbModsBook}.  See \cite[Chapter~4]{Lance_HilbModsBook} for more on these constructions, for example.

Let $\catHCStarBi{A}$ be the category of (non-degenerate) $A$-$A$-correspondances, with adjointable $A$-bimodule maps as morphisms.  
We supress the $*$-homomorphisms $\pi \colon A \to \mc L(E)$ and just write $a\cdot x$ for $\pi(a)x$.  So $t\colon E \to F$ must be adjointable, $t\in\mc L(E,F)$, and also satisfy $t(a\cdot x)=a\cdot t(x)$ for $x\in E, a\in A$.  Then $\hom(E,F)$ is a closed subspace of $\mc L(E,F)$, and $\hom(E)$ is a $*$-subalgebra of $\mc L(E)$, and so $\catHCStarBi{A}$ is a $*$-subcategory of $\catHCStar{A}$.

We define a monoidal structure on $\catHCStarBi{A}$ by using the interior tensor product.  As above, we can form morphisms of the form $t\otimes 1$, and by Lemma~\ref{lem:bimodmaps_act_tensor}, also morphisms of the form $1\otimes s$, and so composition gives $t\otimes s$.  The unit object is $A$, with the unit isomorphisms given above.  The associativity maps are the obvious ones.

By Lemma~\ref{lem:bimod_is_centre}, we see that the endomorphism space of the unit object is $\hom(1) = {}_A \mc L_A(A) \cong ZM(A)$ can be quite a large algebra (compare to assumptions we might make later).
\end{example}

\begin{example}
We can build correspondances from CP maps, see \cite[Chapter~5]{Lance_HilbModsBook}.  Given a strict CP map $\theta \colon A \to \mc L(E)$ where $E$ is a Hilbert $C^*$-module over $B$, we let $A \otimes_\theta E$ be the separation completion of $A\otimes E$ for the $B$-valued pre-inner-product
\[ (a\otimes x|b\otimes y) = (x|\theta(a^*b)y)_E \in B \qquad (a,b\in A, x,y\in E). \]
Then $A\otimes_\theta E$ is a Hilbert $C^*$-module over $B$.  There is a left action of $A$ on $A\otimes_\theta E$ which is non-degenerate.  For each $x\in E$ and approximate identity $(e_i)$ for $A$ we have that $(e_i\otimes x)$ is a Cauchy net in $E\otimes_\theta E$ say converging to $v(x)$.  Then $v\in\mc L(E,A\otimes_\theta E)$, and $\theta(a) = v^*\pi(a)v$ where $\pi$ is given by the right $A$-action.  Recall that $\theta$ is \emph{strict} means that $(\theta(e_i))$ is a Cauchy net in $\mc L(E)$ for the strict topology.  For more details see \cite[Theorem~5.6]{Lance_HilbModsBook}.

In the special case when $\theta$ is a homomorphism, we see that $A\otimes_\theta E$ is isomorphic to a subspace of $E$ via $a\otimes x\mapsto \theta(a)x$.  Suppose further that $E=A$, and consider $\theta' \colon A \to M(A)$ another $*$-homomorphism.  Then we can form $(A\otimes_\theta A)\otimes_A(A\otimes_{\theta'}A)$ which has inner-product
\begin{align*}
& \bigl( (a_1\otimes x_1)\otimes(b_1\otimes y_1) \big| (a_2\otimes x_2)\otimes(b_2\otimes y_2) \bigr)
= \bigl( (b_1\otimes y_1) \big| x_1^* \theta(a_1^*a_2) x_2 b_2\otimes y_2 \bigr)  \\
&= y_1^* \theta'( b_1^* x_1^* \theta(a_1)^* \theta(a_2) x_2 b_2 ) y_2
= \bigl( a_1 \otimes \theta'(x_1b_1)y_1 \big| a_2 \otimes \theta'(x_2b_2)y_2 \bigr)_{A\otimes_{\theta'\theta}A}.
\end{align*}

Hence we have a map from (say) non-degenerate $*$-automorphisms of $A$ to $\catHCStarBi{A}$ which is a functor for appropriate structures (and a monoidal functor, see later).
\end{example}

We next give two examples coming from groups: these shall be central examples for the further axioms given below.

\begin{example}
Let $G$ be a locally compact group, and let $\Rep(G)$ be the $C^*$-category of unitary representations of $G$.  Define a monoidal product by using the tensor product of representations.  Chose the obvious associativity morphisms.  The unit object is the trivial representation $1 \colon G \to \mathbb C$ which sends each group element to $1\in\mathbb C$; again the unit isomorphisms are the obvious ones.  This gives a $*$-subcategory of $\catHilb$.

We might restrict to just finite-dimensional representations, say if $G$ is compact.

This has an obvious generalisation to quantum groups, the main subject of \cite{NT_CQGsBook}, for example.
\end{example}

\begin{example}\label{eg:dualreps}
Let $G$ be a (discrete) group, and let $H$ be a $G$-graded Hilbert space.  That is, $H$ is the Hilbert space direct sum of Hilbert spaces $(H_g)_{g\in G}$.  Let $\Rep(\hat G)$ be the collection of such $H$.  A morphism $x \colon H\to K$ between graded Hilbert spaces is a bounded linear map which respects the grading: $x(H_g) \subseteq K_g$ for all $g$.  This gives a $*$-subcategory of $\catHilb$.

We define a monoidal product by
\[ (H\otimes K)_g = \bigoplus_{\{s,t\in G : st=g \}} H_s \otimes K_t, \]
and then of course $H\otimes K$ is the direct-sum of these spaces.  Given $x\in\hom(H,H')$ and $y\in\hom(K,K')$ we define the $g$-component of $x\otimes y$ by setting $(x\otimes y)_g \colon (H\otimes K)_g \to (H'\otimes K')_g$ to be the direct sum of $x_s \otimes y_t \colon H_s \otimes K_t \to H'_s \otimes K'_t$.  Then
\[ \|x\otimes y\| = \sup_g \|(x\otimes y)_g\| = \sup \big\{ \|x_s \otimes y_t\| : st=g \in G \big\}
\leq \sup \{ \|x_s\| \|y_t\| : s,t\in G \} = \|x\| \|y\|, \]
so $x\otimes y$ exists.  This respects composition and is bilinear.  Again, the associativity morphisms are the obvious choice.  The unit element is the one-dimensional Hilbert space $1$ with grading $1_e=\mathbb C, 1_g=\{0\}$ for $g\not=e$ and then the unit isomorphisms of the obvious choice: $(1\otimes K)_g = \mathbb C \otimes K_g \cong K_g$.

There is another way to think about $\Rep(\hat G)$.  Below we shall formally define what is meant by a subobject, and by direct sums (see also Definition~\ref{defn:subobj_sums} from above) but in this fairly concrete example, the notions should be clear.  Then any graded Hilbert space is the direct sum of copies of the simple objects $\delta_g$, for $g\in G$.  Here $\delta_g$ is the one-dimensional space with grading $(\delta_g)_g = \mathbb C, (\delta_g)_h = \{0\}$ for $h\not=g$; so $\delta_e$ is the unit object.  Then $\delta_g \otimes \delta_h = \delta_{gh}$, with actual equality, if we define $\mathbb C \otimes \mathbb C$ to be actually equal to $\mathbb C$.  This viewpoint perhaps makes it clearer how the group product is involved, and why it might be reasonable to denote this $\Rep(\hat G)$.  Again, quantum groups would give an even clearer picture.
\end{example}

\begin{example}
Again let $G$ be a discrete group.  Let $\omega Z^3(G, \mathbb T)$ be a $3$-cocycle.  This means that $\omega \colon G\times G\times G \to \mathbb T = \{ z\in\mathbb C : |z|=1\}$ is a function satisfying the cocycle identity:
\[ \omega(h, k, l) \omega(g, hk, l) \omega(g, h, k) = \omega(g, h, kl) \omega(gh, k, l) \qquad (g,h,k,l\in G). \]
Let $\mc A$ be the $C^*$-category with objects $\delta_g$ for $g\in G$ and morphisms graded linear maps, as in Example~\ref{eg:dualreps}.  This means in practise that $\hom(\delta_g, \delta_h) = \{0\}$ for $g\not=h$ and the endomorphism algebras $\hom(\delta_g) = \mathbb C$.

We define a monoidal product on $\mc A$ again by $\delta_g \otimes \delta_h = \delta_{gh}$ but now define by associativity morphisms by
\[ \alpha_{g,h,k} \colon (\delta_g\otimes\delta_h)\otimes\delta_k \to \delta_g\otimes(\delta_h\otimes\delta_k); \quad
\delta_{ghk} \mapsto \omega(g,h,k) \delta_{ghk}. \]
Here $\alpha_{g,h,k}$ is shorthand for the more correct $\alpha_{\delta_g, \delta_h, \delta_k}$.  Notice that both $(\delta_g\otimes\delta_h)\otimes\delta_k$ and $\delta_g\otimes(\delta_h\otimes\delta_k)$ equal $\delta_{ghk}$.  The pentagonal diagram axiom \ref{cst-axioms:6} becomes exactly the cocycle identity.

We define the unit isomorphisms by setting $\lambda_g \colon \delta_e \otimes \delta_g \to \delta_g$ to be $\omega(e,e,g)^{-1} \id_{\delta_g}$ and similarly $\rho_g = \omega(g,e,e) \id_{\delta_g}$ where $e\in G$ is the group unit.  The triangle diagram then becomes equivalent to
\[ \omega(e,e,h)^{-1} \omega(g,e,h) = \omega(g,e,e) \qquad (g,h\in G). \]
This follow from the cocycle identity by taking $h=k=e$.  Recall that a cocycle is \emph{normalised} when $\omega(g,e,e) = \omega(e,e,g) = 1$ for each $g$ (which hence implies also that $\omega(g,e,h)=1$ for each $g,h\in G$).

See \cite[Example~2.3.8]{EGNO_TensorCategories} (which we find very readable) and the somewhat briefer \cite[Example~2.1.2]{NT_CQGsBook}.  We can extend this construction linearly to all of $\Rep(\hat G)$ if we wish.  There is of course a cohomology theory here, see \cite[Section~2.6]{EGNO_TensorCategories} for example.  This example is also an example of a category which is not isomorphic to a strict monoidal category, see \cite[Remark~2.8.6]{EGNO_TensorCategories} (almost of course it is equivalent to a strict monoidal category).
\end{example}



\subsection{Some basic theory and definitions}

In Example~\ref{eg:tensor_bimods} we found that $\hom(1)$ was a commutative $C^*$-algebra.  This always holds.

\begin{lemma}[Compare {\cite[Proposition 2.2.10.]{EGNO_TensorCategories}}]\label{lem:hom1_comm}
Let $\mc A$ be a $C^*$-tensor catgeory.  Then $\hom(1)$ is a commutative $C^*$-algebra.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
That the unit isomorphism $\lambda_1$ is natural means that for $x\colon 1\to 1$ we have $\lambda_1(1\otimes x) = x \lambda_1$ and so $1\otimes x = \lambda_1^* x \lambda_1$.  Similarly for $y\colon 1\to 1$ we have $y\otimes 1 = \rho_1^* y \rho_1$.  Thus
\begin{align*}
y\otimes x = (y\otimes 1)(1\otimes x) = \rho_1^* y \rho_1 \lambda_1^* x \lambda_1 = \lambda_1^* yx \lambda_1, \\
y\otimes x = (1\otimes x)(y\otimes 1) =  \lambda_1^* x \lambda_1 \rho_1^* y \rho_1= \lambda_1^* xy \lambda_1,
\end{align*}
because $\rho_1 = \lambda_1$.  So $yx = xy$ and hence $\hom(1)$ is commutative.
\end{proof}

The following two definitions were already made above, see Definition~\ref{defn:subobj_sums}.  We repeat the discussion to be self-contained; these are further axioms which will be useful, but we won't always assume them.

\begin{enumerate}[(1), start=9]
  \item\label{cst-axioms:9} $\mc A$ has \emph{subobjects}, meaning that whenever $A\in\mc A$ and $e\in \mc A(A)$ is a projection, there is $A_e \in \mc A$ and an isometry $u\in\mc A(A_e, A)$ (meaning $u^*u=1$) with $uu^* = e$.
\end{enumerate}

As $e\in\mc A(A)$ is a projection, $e \mc A(A) e$ is a $C^*$-algebra (with $e$ being its unit).   The map $A_e \to A; a \mapsto u a u^*$ is a $*$-homomorphism (as $u^*u=1$) into $e\mc A(A)e$ as $eu = uu^*u = u$ and $u^*e = u^*uu^* = u^*$.  Conversely, if $x\in e\mc A(A)e$ then set $a = u^*xu \in \mc A(A_e)$ so that $uau^* = uu^* x uu^* = exe = x$.  Thus the object $A_e$ is somehow ``canonically'' isomorphic to $e \mc A(A) e$.

Conversely, given any $B$ and $u\in\hom(B,A)$ an isometry, set $e = uu^* \in \hom(A)$, a projection.  If \ref{cst-axioms:9} holds, there is $A_e$ and an isometry $u_e\colon A_e\to A$.  Then $v = u_e^* u \colon B \to A_e$ is unitary, as $v^*v = u^* u_e u_e^* u = u^* e u = u^* u u^* u = 1_B$ and $vv^* = u_e^* u u^* u_e = u_e^* e u_e = u_e^* u_e u_e^* u_e = 1_A$.  So a \emph{subobject} of $A$ is determined either by a projection in $\hom(A)$, or an isometry in $\hom(B,A)$ for some $B$.

Axiom \ref{cst-axioms:9} includes the possibility that $e=0$.  The following statement might seem confusing, as how can we have $u^*u=1$ if $\mc A(A_0)=\{0\}$.  The point is that, if we allow $\{0\}$ to be a $C^*$-algebra at all (which we do here!), then $0=1$ in this algebra.

\begin{lemma}
Let $\mc A$ be a $C^*$-catgeory with subobjects.  There is, up to unique isomorphism, exactly one object $A_0$ with $\mc A(A_0, A) = \{0\}$ and $\mc A(A,A_0)=\{0\}$ for every $A\in\mc A$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Take $e=0$ in axiom \ref{cst-axioms:9}, so there is $A_0$ with $1=0$ in $\mc A(A_0)$.  For any $A$, it follows that for $x\colon A_0 \to A$ we have $x = x\circ 1 = x\circ 0 = 0$ so $\mc A(A_0,A)=\{0\}$, and similarly $\mc A(A,A_0)=\{0\}$.  Suppose $A_0'$ is another object with this property.  Then $0 \in \mc A(A_0, A_0')$ is unique (!) and a unitary, as $u^*u = 0 = 1$ in $\mc A(A_0)$, and similarly $uu^*=0=1$ in $\mc A(A_0')$.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}\label{defn:zero_obj}
The object $A_0$ in the previous lemma is the \emph{zero object} of $\mc A$ and is usually denoted by $0$.
\end{definition}

There is little to be lost by defining all the zero objects to actually be equal.  Indeed, in our examples, perhaps we did not originally include a zero object: it is not usual to consider $\{0\}$ as a Hilbert space, for example!  However, we can always just add a single object to a $C^*$-category.

\begin{definition}\label{defn:simple_obj}
An object $A$ is \emph{simple} when $\hom(A)$ has no proper projections, equivalently, if $u\colon B\to A$ is an isometry, for some $B$, then $B=0$ or $u$ is unitary.
\end{definition}

We come to the next axiom:

\begin{enumerate}[(1), start=10]
  \item\label{cst-axioms:10} $\mc A$ has \emph{(finite) direct sums}, meaning that whenever $A_1, A_2$ are objects in $\mc A$, there is $A\in\mc A$ and isometries $u_i \in \mc A(A_i, A)$ for $i=1,2$ with $u_1u_1^* + u_2u_2^* = 1$.
\end{enumerate}

The sum $\sum_i u_i u_i^*=1$ is in the $C^*$-algebra $\mc A(A)$: each $e_i = u_i u_i^*$ is a projection, and as they sum to $1$ they must be mutually orthogonal.  Again $\mc A(A_i) \to \mc A(A); a \mapsto u_i a u_i^*$ is a $*$-isomorphism from $\mc A(A_i)$ to $e_i \mc A(A) e_i$.  Hence $\mc A(A)$ contains as a ``diagonal'' the two algebras $\mc A(A_1)$ and $\mc A(A_2)$.

\begin{lemma}
Let $\mc A$ be a $C^*$-category with direct sums.  Given $A_1, A_2$, the $A$ we find is unique in the following sense: if $A'$ and $u_1', u_2'$ also satisfy the definition, then there is a unique unitary $v\colon A\to A'$ with $v u_i = u_i'$ for $i=1,2$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Define $v = u_1' u_1^* + u_2' u_2^* \in \mc A(A,A')$.  Then $v u_i = u_i'$ because $u_1^* u_2 = 0$ and $u_2^* u_1=0$ as the $u_i$ are isometries with orthogonal ranges.  We also see that $v^*v = \sum_i u_i u_i'^* u_i' u_i^* = \sum_i u_i u_i^* = 1$ for the same reason, and $vv^*=1$, so $v$ is unitary.  If $w$ is a unitary with $w u_i = u_i'$ for $i=1,2$ then $w = w\sum_i u_iu_i^* = \sum_i u_i' u_i^* = v$, so $v$ is unique.
\end{proof}

\begin{definition}\label{defn:direct_sum_notation}
We write $A_1 \oplus A_2$ for the unique (up to isomorphism) $A$ occurring in axiom \ref{cst-axioms:10}.
\end{definition}

Unlike for zero objects, the direct sum will rarely be actually unique-- having formed $A_1 \oplus A_2$ just twist by a unitary in $\mc A(A_1\oplus A_2)$-- but in most examples there will be a canonical choice.  Notice that $0 \oplus A \cong A$, and again it is helpful to \emph{define} $0\oplus A = A = A\oplus 0$.\footnote{If our category is a $*$-subcategory of $\catHilb$ then we might identify $0$ with the zero Hilbert space $\{0\}$, and also certainly use the usual direct-sum construction.  However, then $0 \oplus H$ would be, formally, ordered pairs $(0,\xi)$ with $\xi\in H$, which is not \emph{equal} to $H$.}

\begin{remark}
We have implicitly been using that $\mc A(A)$ is unital for every $A$.  This is true in the examples we've looked at, but perhaps is not the most general setting one could explore the theory in.  Again, working with multiplier algebras, Remark~\ref{rem:mults}, would seem to be the best general setting to work in, though I have not explored this.
\end{remark}

\begin{definition}
Let $\mc A$ be a $C^*$-category.  We say that $A\in\mc A$ is \emph{simple} if it is not isomorphic to a non-trivial direct sum.
\end{definition}

By ``non-trivial'' we mean not involving the zero object.  It is not clear to me if this definition is quite the correct one in all circumstances, nor if it fully captures what we might want it to (that is, will be interaction between algebraic and analytic considerations work correctly).  However, in many examples this notion is useful-- see Example~\ref{eg:dualreps} for example.

We will often assume that:

\begin{enumerate}[(1), start=11]
  \item\label{cst-axioms:11} $\hom(1) \cong \mathbb C$.
\end{enumerate}

We now explore $\hom(1)$ a little more, and give an example of using the axioms to prove things.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:L1_R1_equivs}
Let $\mc A$ be a $C^*$-tensor category.  The functor $L_1 \colon \mc A\to\mc A$ defined by $L_1(A) = 1\otimes A$ on objects, and $L_1(x)=1\otimes x$ on morphisms, is an equivalence of categories.  Similarly $R_1 \colon \mc A\to\mc A; A\mapsto A\otimes 1, x\mapsto x\otimes 1$ is an equivalence.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By Theorem~\ref{thm:cat_equiv_FFES}, this is equivalent to the claim that $\mc A(A,B) \to \mc A(A,B); x\mapsto 1\otimes x$ is a bijecton, and $L_1$ is essentially surjective on objects.  This follows from using the $\lambda$ maps.  Indeed, that $\lambda$ is natural means that for $x\colon A\to B$ we have $\lambda_B(1\otimes x) = x\lambda_A$.  So $x = \lambda_B(1\otimes x)\lambda_A^*$, so $x\mapsto 1\otimes x$ is a bijection.  Similarly, $\lambda_A$ shows that $A$ is isomorphic to $1\otimes A$, for all $A$.  The claim for $R_1$ follows by using $\rho$.
\end{proof}

Indeed, the approach of \cite{EGNO_TensorCategories} is to take the above lemma as an axiom, and to derive e.g.\@ the triangle identity from this, see \cite[Section~2.2]{EGNO_TensorCategories}.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:unit_unit_one_side}
Let $\mc A$ be a $C^*$-tensor category, and let $A\in\mc A$.  We have that $\lambda_{1\otimes A} = 1 \otimes \lambda_A$ and $\rho_{A\otimes 1} = \rho_A \otimes 1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
That $\lambda$ is natural means that for $x\colon A\to B$ we have $\lambda_B(1\otimes x) = x \lambda_A \colon 1\otimes A \to B$.  Apply this with $x=\lambda_A$ to see that $\lambda_A(1\otimes\lambda_A) = \lambda_A \lambda_{1\otimes A}$.  As $\lambda_A$ is unitary, the first claim follows; the second claim is similar.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[{\cite[Proposition~2.2.4]{EGNO_TensorCategories}}]\label{lem:other_triangles}
Let $\mc A$ be a $C^*$-tensor category, and let $A,B\in\mc A$.  The following diagrams commute:
\begin{equation}
\begin{tikzcd}
  (1\otimes A)\otimes B \ar[rr, "\alpha_{1,A,B}"] \ar[rd, "\lambda_A\otimes 1"'] && 1\otimes (A\otimes B) \ar[ld, "\lambda_{A\otimes B}"] \\
  & A\otimes B,
\end{tikzcd} \label{eq:other_triangle_1}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\begin{tikzcd}
  (A\otimes B)\otimes 1 \ar[rd, "\rho_{A\otimes B}"'] \ar[rr, "\alpha_{A,B,1}"] && A\otimes(B\otimes 1) \ar[ld, "1\otimes\rho_B"] \\
  & A\otimes B.
\end{tikzcd} \label{eq:other_triangle_2}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For $A,B,C$ consider the diagram:
\[ \begin{tikzcd}
((A\otimes 1)\otimes B) \otimes C \ar[rr, "\alpha_{A,1,B}\otimes 1"] \ar[rd, "(\rho_A\otimes 1)\otimes 1"'] \ar[ddd, "\alpha_{A\otimes 1,B,C}"'] &&
   (A\otimes(1\otimes B))\otimes C \ar[ld, "(1\otimes\lambda_B)\otimes 1"] \ar[ddd, "\alpha_{A,1\otimes B,C}"] \\
   & (A\otimes B)\otimes C \ar[d, "\alpha_{A,B,C}"] \\
   & A\otimes(B\otimes C) \\
(A\otimes 1)\otimes(B\otimes C)  \ar[rd, "\alpha_{A,1,B\otimes C}"'] \ar[ru, "\rho_A\otimes 1"] &&
   A\otimes((1\otimes B)\otimes C) \ar[ld, "1\otimes\alpha_{1,B,C}"] \ar[lu, "1\otimes(\lambda_B\otimes 1)"'] \\
   & A\otimes (1\otimes (B\otimes C)). \ar[uu, "1\otimes\lambda_{B\otimes C}"]
\end{tikzcd} \]
The outside is the pentagonal axiom \ref{cst-axioms:6} (reflected and rotated slightly), and so commutes.  The two middle square shapes commute because $\alpha$ is natural.  The top triangle is just the triangle diagram, axiom \ref{cst-axioms:7}, tensored with $1$, and so commutes.  The lower left triangle is also the triangle diagram, and so commutes.  We conclude that the bottom right triangle must also commute.  Set $A=1$, and this becomes the diagram in \eqref{eq:other_triangle_1}, tensored with $1$ on the left.  By Lemma~\ref{lem:L1_R1_equivs}, also \eqref{eq:other_triangle_1} must commute.  \eqref{eq:other_triangle_2} is shown similarly.
\end{proof}

% \begin{lemma}\label{lem:rho_lambda_comm}
% Let $\mc A$ be a $C^*$-tensor category and let $A\in\mc A$.  The following diagram commutes:
% \[ \begin{tikzcd}
%   & 1\otimes A \ar[rd, "\lambda_A"] \\
%   1 \otimes (A\otimes 1) \ar[ru, "1\otimes\rho_A"] \ar[rd, "\lambda_{A\otimes 1}"'] && A. \\
%   & A\otimes 1 \ar[ru, "\rho_A"']
% \end{tikzcd} \]
% \end{lemma}
% \begin{proof}
% This follows from Mac Lane's coherence theorem.  I would really like a proof along the lines of the above, arguing directly from the axioms!

% Doesn't this just follow from $\lambda$ being natural: set $B = A\otimes 1$ and $x=\rho_A \colon B\to A$, so by naturality, $x \lambda = \lambda(1\otimes x) \colon 1\otimes B \to A$, so we have
% \[\begin{tikzcd}
% 1\otimes (A\otimes 1) \ar[r, "1\otimes\rho_A"] \ar[d, "\lambda_{A\otimes 1}"] & 1\otimes A \ar[d, "\lambda_A"] \\
% A\otimes 1 \ar[r, "\rho_A"] & A
% \end{tikzcd}\]
% So, yes!
% \end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
For $A\in\mc A$, define $\pi_L = \pi^A_L, \pi_R = \pi^A_R \colon \hom(1) \to \hom(A)$ by
\[ \pi_L(a) = \lambda_A (a\otimes 1)\lambda_A^{-1},
\quad \pi_R(a) = \rho_A (1\otimes a)\rho_A^{-1}
\qquad (a\in \hom(1)). \]
Then $\pi_L, \pi_R$ are homomorphisms.  For objects $A,B$ we may define a $\hom(1)$-bimodule structure of $\hom(A,B)$ by $a\cdot x = \pi^B_L(a)x, x\cdot a = x \pi^A_R(a)$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
It is clear that these are homomorphisms.  As $\hom(1)$ is commutative, we have defined a left $B$ action and a right $A$ action on $\hom(A,B)$, which is a bimodule structure by associativity.
\end{proof}

Notice that for $a\in\hom(1), x\in\hom(A,B)$ we have
\begin{gather*}
a\cdot x = \lambda_B(a\otimes 1)\lambda_B^{-1}x = \lambda_B(a\otimes x)\lambda_A^{-1},   \\
x\cdot a = x \rho_A(1\otimes a)\rho_A^{-1} = \rho_B(x\otimes a)\rho_A^{-1}
\end{gather*}
using that $\lambda, \rho$ are natural.

This helps to explain axiom \ref{cst-axioms:11} a little: if $\hom(1)$ is not just the complex numbers, then $\hom(1)$ is a non-trivial commutative algebra which acts on every morphism space.  We can hence decompose $\mc A$ into sub-categories, at least morally, if not in actual fact.


\subsection{Functors}

We follow \cite{EGNO_TensorCategories} here which makes the minimal definition and then shows that certain further properties are automatic.  Again, while we state these results for $C^*$-tensor categories, the underlying ideas work for general monoidal categories.

\begin{definition}\label{defn:monoidal_functor}
Let $\mc A, \mc B$ to two tensor $C^*$-categories.  A \emph{monoidal functor} from $\mc A$ to $\mc B$ is a functor $F \colon \mc A \to \mc B$ together with a natural isomorphism $J \colon F(\cdot) \otimes F(\cdot) \to F(\cdot\otimes\cdot)$, such that $F(1)$ is isomorphic to $1$, and for each $A,B,C\in\mc A$ the following diagram commutes:
\[\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=8ex]
( F(A) \otimes F(B) ) \otimes F(C) \ar[r, "J_{A,B}\otimes 1"] \ar[d, "\alpha_{A,B,C}"]
   & F(A\otimes B) \otimes F(C) \ar[r, "J_{A\otimes B,C}"]
   & F((A\otimes B)\otimes C) \ar[d, "F(\alpha_{A,B,C})"]  \\
F(A) \otimes (F(B) \otimes F(C)) \ar[r, "1 \otimes J_{B,C}"]
   & F(A) \otimes F(B\otimes C) \ar[r, "J_{A,B\otimes C}"] 
   & F(A\otimes(B\otimes C))  
\end{tikzcd}\]
We say that $F$ is \emph{unitary} when $F$ is a $*$-functor and each component of $J$ is unitary.
\end{definition}

It is important to note that $J$ is part of the definition, and $J$ is not uniquely defined by $F$.  I had to read \cite{EGNO_TensorCategories} several times at this point.\footnote{\url{https://math.stackexchange.com/questions/3470045} helped to eventually let me come up with the explaination here.}  The following lemma could have been stated before, and indeed is essentially Lemma~\ref{lem:L1_R1_equivs} if we had more category theory courage.

% \begin{lemma}\label{lem:form_of_map_involving_tensor_1}
% Let $\mc A$ be a $C^*$-tensor category, and let $A_0\in\mc A$ be an object isomorphic to the unit object $1$.  For $A,B\in\mc A$ any $x\colon A\otimes A_0 \to B\otimes A_0$ is of the form $x_0 \otimes 1$ for some $x_0\in\mc A(A,B)$.  Similarly, any $x\colon A_0 \otimes A \to A_0\otimes B$ is of the form $1\otimes x_0$ for some $x_0\in\mc A(A,B)$.
% \end{lemma}
% \begin{proof}
% Let $\phi\colon 1 \to A_0$ be an isomorphism, and let $y = (1\otimes \phi^{-1}) x (1\otimes \phi) \colon A\otimes 1 \to B\otimes 1$.  We will show that $y = x_0 \otimes 1$ for some $x_0$, in which case $x = (1\otimes\phi) y (1\otimes \phi^{-1}) = x_0 \otimes \phi\phi^{-1} = x_0\otimes 1$ as required.

% As $\rho_A, \rho_B$ are isomorphisms, we can define $x_0 = \rho_B y \rho_A^{-1} \colon A \to B$.  However, $\rho$ is natural, so $x_0 \circ \rho_A = \rho_B \circ (x_0\otimes 1)$, and hence $x_0 \otimes 1 = \rho_B^{-1} x_0 \rho_A =  \rho_B^{-1} \rho_B y \rho_A^{-1} \rho_A = y$ as required.

% The claim on the other side follows similarly by using $\lambda$.
% \end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:form_of_map_involving_tensor_1}
Let $\mc A$ be a $C^*$-tensor category, and let $A_0\in\mc A$ be an object isomorphic to the unit object $1$.  Define a functor $R_0 \colon \mc A\to\mc A$ by $R_0(A)=A\otimes A_0$ on objects and $R(x)=x\otimes 1$ on morphisms.  That $R_0$ is fully faithful (i.e.\@ a bijection on hom spaces).  Similarly, $L_0 \colon \mc A\to\mc A$ defined by $L_0(A)=A_0\otimes A$ and $L_0(x) = 1\otimes x$ is fully faithful.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $\phi\colon 1 \to A_0$ be an isomorphism.  We first show that $R_0$ is full, so any $z\colon A\otimes A_0 \to B\otimes A_0$ is of the form $R_0(x_0) = x_0 \otimes 1_{A_0}$ for some $x_0\in\mc A(A,B)$.  Let $y = (1\otimes \phi^{-1}) z (1\otimes \phi) \colon A\otimes 1 \to B\otimes 1$.  We will show that $y = x_0 \otimes 1$ for some $x_0$, in which case $z = (1\otimes\phi) y (1\otimes \phi^{-1}) = x_0 \otimes \phi\phi^{-1} = R_0(x_0)$ as required.

As $\rho_A, \rho_B$ are isomorphisms, we can define $x_0 = \rho_B y \rho_A^{-1} \colon A \to B$.  However, $\rho$ is natural, so $x_0 \circ \rho_A = \rho_B \circ (x_0\otimes 1)$, and hence $x_0 \otimes 1 = \rho_B^{-1} x_0 \rho_A =  \rho_B^{-1} \rho_B y \rho_A^{-1} \rho_A = y$ as required.

Now given $x\in \mc A(A,B)$, we have $x\otimes 1 = x\otimes \phi^{-1}\phi = (1\otimes \phi^{-1}) R_0(x) (1\otimes \phi) \colon A\otimes 1 \to B\otimes 1$.  So again $x\rho_A = \rho_B(x\otimes 1)$ and hence $x = \rho_B (1\otimes \phi^{-1}) R_0(x) (1\otimes \phi) \rho_A^{-1}$.  Hence $R\colon \hom(A,B) \to \hom(A\otimes A_0, B\otimes A_0)$ is injective, and $R_0$ is fully faithful.

The claim about $L_0$ follows similarly by using $\lambda$.
\end{proof}

Suppose we have some isomorphism $\phi \colon 1 \to F(1)$.  By Lemma~\ref{lem:form_of_map_involving_tensor_1} applied to the map $J_{1,1}^{-1} \circ F(\lambda_1)^{-1} \circ \lambda_1$, there is $F_0 \colon 1 \to F(1)$ making the following diagram commute:
\begin{equation}
\begin{tikzcd}
  1 \otimes F(1) \ar[r, "\lambda_{F(1)}"] \ar[d, "F_0\otimes 1"'] & F(1) \\
  F(1) \otimes F(1) \ar[r, "J_{1,1}"'] & F(1\otimes 1) \ar[u, "F(\lambda_1)"']
\end{tikzcd}.   \label{eq:defn_F0}
\end{equation}
Necessarily $F_0$ is invertible, and when $F$ is unitary, $J_{1,1}^{-1} \circ F(\lambda_1)^{-1} \circ \lambda_1$ is unitary, and so necessarily $F_0$ is unitary.  Notice this reasoning used no assumption about $\phi$.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:F0_action_general}
Given a monoidal functor $(F, J)$ for any $A\in\mc A$ the following diagrams commute.
\[\begin{tikzcd}
  1 \otimes F(A) \ar[r, "\lambda_{F(A)}"] \ar[d, "F_0\otimes 1"'] & F(A) \\
  F(1) \otimes F(A) \ar[r, "J_{1,A}"'] & F(1\otimes A) \ar[u, "F(\lambda_A)"']
\end{tikzcd}
\qquad\qquad
\begin{tikzcd}
F(A)\otimes 1 \ar[r, "\rho_{F(A)}"] \ar[d, "1\otimes F_0"']  & F(A) \\
F(A) \otimes F(1) \ar[r, "J_{A,1}"']  & F(A\otimes 1) \ar[u, "F(\rho_A)"']
\end{tikzcd}
\]
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Here\footnote{Perhaps there is a better way to prove this, but this is what I have.} we follow \cite{MS5061586}, at least initially.
Consider the following diagram, where we omit the morphisms for clarity: the back face is \eqref{eq:defn_F0} tensored on the right with $F(A)$, while the front face is the first claim, but for the object $1\otimes A$.
\[\begin{tikzcd}
&   (1 \otimes F(1))\otimes F(A)  \ar[rr] \ar[dd] \ar[ld] && F(1)\otimes F(A) \ar[ld]   \\
1 \otimes F(1\otimes A) \ar[dd] \ar[rr, crossing over]  &&  F(1\otimes A)  \\
&   (F(1) \otimes F(1))\otimes F(A) \ar[rr] \ar[ld]  &&  F(1\otimes 1)\otimes F(A) \ar[ld] \ar[uu] \\
F(1) \otimes F(1\otimes A)   \ar[rr]   && F(1\otimes (1\otimes A))  \ar[uu, crossing over]
\end{tikzcd}. \]
We now consider the other faces in turn, showing that they are commutative.

The top face being commutative is $J_{1,A} \circ (\lambda_{F(1)}\otimes 1) = \lambda_{F(1\otimes A)} \circ (1\otimes J_{1,A}) \circ \alpha_{1,F(1),F(A)}$.  As $\lambda$ is natural, we have $\lambda_{F(1\otimes A)} (1\otimes J_{1,A}) = J_{1,A} \lambda_{F(1)\otimes F(A)}$ and then by \eqref{eq:other_triangle_1} we have $J_{1,A} \lambda_{F(1)\otimes F(A)}\alpha_{1,F(1),F(A)} = J_{1,A} (\lambda_F(1)\otimes 1)$ as required.

The bottom face being commutative is $F(\alpha_{1,1,A}) \circ J_{1\otimes 1,A}\circ (J_{1,1}\otimes 1) = J_{1,1\otimes A} \circ (1\otimes J_{1,A})\circ \alpha_{F(1), F(1), F(a)}$ which is true by the assumption on $J$.

The left face being commutative is $(1\otimes J_{1,A})\circ \alpha_{F(1), F(1), F(a)}\circ (F_0\otimes 1)\otimes 1 = (F_0\otimes 1)\circ (1\otimes J_{1,A}) \circ \alpha_{1,F(1),F(A)}$.  As $\alpha$ is natural, $(F_0\otimes 1) (1\otimes J_{1,A})  \alpha_{1,F(1),F(A)} = (F_0\otimes J_{1,A}) \alpha_{1,F(1),F(A)} =(1\otimes J_{1,A})(F_0\otimes (1\otimes 1)) \alpha_{1,F(1),F(A)} = (1\otimes J_{1,A}) \alpha_{F(1),F(1),F(A)}((F_0\otimes 1)\otimes 1)$, as required.

Finally, the right face being commutative is $F(\lambda_{1\otimes A}^{-1})\circ J_{1,A} = F(\alpha_{1,1,A}) \circ J_{1\otimes 1,A}\circ (F(\lambda_1^{-1})\otimes 1)$.  As $J$ is natural, $F(\alpha_{1,1,A}) J_{1\otimes 1,A} (F(\lambda_1^{-1})\otimes 1)
= F(\alpha_{1,1,A}) F(\lambda_1^{-1}\otimes 1) J_{1,A} = F(\alpha_{1,1,A} \lambda_1^{-1}\otimes 1) J_{1,A} = F(\lambda_{1\otimes A}^{-1}) J_{1,A}$ in the final step using \eqref{eq:other_triangle_1} again.

Thus the front face is also commutative, showing the first claim for the object $1\otimes A$.
Now consider another diagram:
\[\begin{tikzcd}
&  1\otimes F(1\otimes A) \ar[rr, "\lambda_{F(1\otimes A)}"] \ar[dd, "F_0\otimes 1"', pos=0.2]
   \ar[ld, bend right=10, "1\otimes F(\lambda_A)"']
   && F(1\otimes A) \ar[ld, "F(\lambda_A)"]  \\
1\otimes F(A) \ar[rr, crossing over, "\lambda_{F(A)}", pos=0.2] \ar[dd, "F_0\otimes 1"']  && F(A)  \\
&  F(1)\otimes F(1\otimes A) \ar[rr, "J_{1,1\otimes A}", pos=0.2] \ar[ld, "1\otimes F(\lambda_A)"']
  && F(1\otimes(1\otimes A)) \ar[uu, "F(\lambda_{1\otimes A})"'] \ar[ld, "F(1 \otimes \lambda_{A})"] \\
F(1)\otimes F(A) \ar[rr, "J_{1,A}"']  && F(1\otimes A) \ar[uu, crossing over, "F(\lambda_A)", pos=0.15]
\end{tikzcd}. \]
Again we consider each face.  The top face commutes as $\lambda$ is natural; similarly the right face (before we apply $F$ to all the morphisms).  The bottom face commutes by naturality of $J$ (of course, $1=F(1)$).  The left face commutting is obvious ($\otimes$ is a bifunctor).  We conclude that the front face commutes, as we want.

Consider now the following huge diagram:
\[ \begin{tikzcd}
  & F(A) \otimes (1 \otimes F(1))) \ar[r, "1\otimes\lambda_{F(1)}"] \ar[d, "1\otimes(F_0\otimes 1)"']
    \ar[ldd, bend right=30, "\alpha_{F(A),1,F(1)}^{-1}"']
      & F(A) \otimes F(1)  \ar[r, "J_{A,1}"]   \ar[ldd, equal]  & F(A\otimes 1) \\
  & F(A) \otimes(F(1) \otimes F(1)) \ar[r, "1 \otimes J_{1,1}"'] \ar[ldd, bend left=5, "\alpha_{F(A),F(1),F(1)}^{-1}" description]
      & F(A) \otimes F(1\otimes 1) \ar[u, "1\otimes F(\lambda_1)"'] \ar[r, "J_{A,1\otimes 1}"']  &  F(A\otimes (1\otimes 1)) 
        \ar[ldd, "F(\alpha_{A,1,1}^{-1})"] \ar[u, "F(1\otimes\lambda_1)"]   \\
  (F(A)\otimes 1)\otimes F(1) \ar[d, "(1\otimes F_0)\otimes 1"'] \ar[r, bend left=10, "\rho_{F(A)}\otimes 1"]
         & F(A) \otimes F(1) \\
  ((F(A)\otimes F(1)) \otimes F(1) \ar[r, "J_{A,1}\otimes 1"']  & F(A\otimes 1) \otimes F(1)  \ar[u, dotted, "F(\rho_A)\otimes 1"']
    \ar[r, "J_{A\otimes 1,1}"'] & F((A\otimes 1)\otimes 1)  \ar[ruuu, bend right=70, "F(\rho_A\otimes 1)"']
\end{tikzcd} \]
The top square on the left is \eqref{eq:defn_F0} tensored on the left with $F(A)$.  The left square commutes by naturality of $\alpha$.  The bottom rectangle commutes by the axiom on $J$.  The top-right square commutes by naturality of $J$.  The upper left square involving the equality on $F(A)\otimes F(1)$ commutes by the triangle diagram axiom.  Similarly, the far right triangle commutes by the triangle diagram (and applying $F$).

Let's pull out the sub-diagram involving the morphism we don't yet know commutes:
\[ \begin{tikzcd}
F(A)\otimes F(1) \ar[r, "J_{A,1}"]  & F(A\otimes 1)   \\
F(A\otimes 1)\otimes F(1) \ar[u, dotted, "F(\rho_A)\otimes 1"'] \ar[r, "J_{A\otimes 1}\otimes 1"]
& F((A\otimes 1)\otimes 1) \ar[u, "F(\rho_A\otimes 1)"']
\end{tikzcd}. \]
This commutes by naturality of $J$.  Hence the bottom square commutes, but note that this is the second diagram we wish to prove, tensored with $F(1)$.  By Lemma~\ref{lem:form_of_map_involving_tensor_1}, this shows that the original diagram (before tensoring with $F(1)$) also commutes, as desired.
\end{proof}

As ever, in examples, usually the choice of $F_0$ is clear, and that it satisfies these conditions is also clear.

\begin{definition}\label{defn:nt_monoidal}
A \emph{natural transformation} between two monoidal functors $(F_1, J_1)$ and $(F_2, J_2)$ is $\alpha \colon F_1 \Rightarrow F_2$ such that the component $\alpha_1 \colon F_1(1)\to F_2(1)$ is an isomorphism, and for each $A,B$ we have
\[ \begin{tikzcd}[column sep=10ex]
  F_1(A)\otimes F_1(B) \ar[d, "\alpha_A\otimes\alpha_B"'] \ar[r, "J^{(1)}_{A,B}"]  & F_1(A\otimes B) \ar[d, "\alpha_{A\otimes B}"]  \\
  F_2(A)\otimes F_2(B)  \ar[r, "J^{(2)}_{A,B}"']  & F_2(A\otimes B)
\end{tikzcd}. \]
A natural isomorphism is \emph{unitary} when $\alpha$ is unitary.
\end{definition}

Again, the following is often part of the definition.

\begin{proposition}
Let $F_1, F_2$ be monoidal functors, and define $F_0^1, F_0^2$ using \eqref{eq:defn_F0}.  When $\alpha$ is natural transformation, we have that $\alpha_1 \circ F_0^1 = F_0^2$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Consider the diagram
\[\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=9ex]
1\otimes F_2(1) \ar[r, "1\otimes\alpha_1^{-1}"] \ar[d, "\lambda_{F_2(1)}"]
   & 1\otimes F_1(1) \ar[r, "F_0^1\otimes 1"] \ar[d, "\lambda_{F_1(1)}"]
   & F_1(1)\otimes F_1(1) \ar[r, "\alpha_1\otimes\alpha_1"] \ar[d, "J_{1,1}"]
   & F_2(1)\otimes F_2(1) \ar[d, "J_{1,1}"]  \\
F_2(1) \ar[r, "\alpha_1^{-1}"]
   & F_1(1) \ar[r, "F_1(\lambda_1^{-1})"]
   & F_1(1\otimes 1) \ar[r, "\alpha_{1\otimes 1}"]
   & F_2(1\otimes 1)
\end{tikzcd}.\]
From the left, the first square commutes by naturality of $\lambda$, the second square by definition of $F_0^1$, and the third square by hypothesis on $\alpha$.  Notice that the composition on the top row is $\alpha_1\circ F_0^1\otimes 1$, while by naturality of $\alpha$ we have $\alpha_{1\otimes 1}\circ F_1(\lambda_1^{-1}) = F_2(\lambda_1^{-1}) \circ \alpha_1$ and so the composition on the bottom row is $F_2(\lambda_1^{-1})$.  That is, the outer rectangle defines $F_0^2$ and so $\alpha_1\circ F_0^1\otimes 1 = F_0^2$ as claimed (Lemma~\ref{lem:form_of_map_involving_tensor_1} again).
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:compose_monoidal_functors}
We can compose monoidal functors $(F,J)$ and $(G,K)$ by forming $GF$ and defining $L \colon GF(\cdot)\otimes GF(\cdot) \Rightarrow GF(\cdot\otimes\cdot)$ to have components
\[ \begin{tikzcd}[column sep=9ex]
  GF(A) \otimes GF(B) \ar[r, "K_{F(A), F(B)}"] \ar[rd, "L_{A,B}"', dotted]
  & G(F(A)\otimes F(B)) \ar[d, "G(J_{A,B})"] \\
  & GF(A\otimes B)
\end{tikzcd}. \]
If $F,G$ are $*$-functors are $J,K$ unitary, then $L$ will also be unitary.  A simple calculation shows that $L$ is natural.  As $K$ is natural, we have the equalities
\begin{gather*}
  (G(J_{A,B}) \otimes 1)  \circ K_{F(A) \otimes F(B), F(C)} = K_{F(A\otimes B), F(C)}  \circ (G(J_{A,B})\otimes 1), \\
  (1 \otimes G(J_{B,C})) \circ K_{F(A), F(B)\otimes F(C)} = K_{F(A), F(B\otimes C)} \circ (1\otimes G(J_{B,C})).
\end{gather*}
Then, omitting subscripts, we have
\begin{align*}
GF(\alpha) \circ L \circ (L\otimes 1)
&=  GF(\alpha) G(J) K (GJ\otimes 1)(K\otimes 1)
=  GF(\alpha) G(J)  (GJ\otimes 1) K (K\otimes 1)  \\
&= G\big(F(\alpha) J (J\otimes 1) \big) K (K\otimes 1)
= G\big( J (1\otimes J) \alpha ) K (K\otimes 1)
= G(J) (1\otimes G(J)) G(\alpha) K (K\otimes 1) \\
&= G(J) (1\otimes G(J)) K (1\otimes K) \alpha
=  G(J) K (1\otimes G(J)) (1\otimes K) \alpha \\
&= L\circ (1\otimes L) \circ \alpha,
\end{align*}
hence verifying the condition in Definition~\ref{defn:monoidal_functor}.  Finally, if $\phi \colon 1\to F(1)$ is an isomorphism, then $G(\phi) \colon G(1) \to GF(1)$ is an isomorphism, and $1 \cong G(1)$, so $1 \cong GF(1)$.

Consider now the canonical isomorphisms $F_0, G_0$, see \eqref{eq:defn_F0}.  In the following diagram, the top square is from Proposition~\ref{prop:F0_action_general} applied to $G_0$, while the bottom-right square is the definition \eqref{eq:defn_F0} of $F_0$ with $G$ applied.  The remaining squares commute obviously and by naturality of $K$.
\[\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=8ex]
  1 \otimes GF(1) \ar[r, "\lambda_{GF(1)}"] \ar[d, "G_0\otimes 1"] & GF(1) \ar[d, "G(\lambda_{F(1)}^{-1})"] \\
  G(1) \otimes GF(1) & G(1\otimes F(1)) \ar[l, "K_{1,F(1)}^{-1}"] \\
  G(1) \otimes GF(1) \ar[u, equals] \ar[r, "K_{1,F(1)}"] \ar[d, "G(F_0) \otimes 1"] & G(1\otimes F(1)) \ar[u, equals] \ar[r, "G(\lambda_{F(1)})"] \ar[d, "G(F_0\otimes 1)"] & GF(1) \ar[d, "GF(\lambda_1^{-1})"] \\
  GF(1) \otimes GF(1) & G(F(1)\otimes F(1)) \ar[l, "K_{F(1), F(1)}^{-1}"] & GF(1\otimes 1) \ar[l, "G(J_{1,1}^{-1})"]
\end{tikzcd}. \]
The composition on the left is $G(F_0)G_0 \otimes 1$.  The bottom composition is $L_{1,1}^{-1}$, and the composition down the right is $GF(\lambda_1^{-1}) \circ G(\lambda_{F(1)}) \circ G(\lambda_{F(1)}^{-1}) = GF(\lambda_1^{-1})$, and so $(GF)_0 = G(F_0)\circ G_0$.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:upgrade_to_monoidal_equiv}
Let $\mc A,\mc B$ be $C^*$-tensor categories.  Let $F\colon \mc A \to \mc B$ be a monoidal functor (with respect to $J$ say), and let $G\colon \mc A \to \mc B$ be a functor (not assumed monoidal) such that $(F,G)$ is an equivalence: that is, there are natural isomorphisms $\epsilon \colon FG\Rightarrow 1_{\mc B}$ and $\eta\colon 1_{\mc A} \to GF$.  Then $G$ can be given the structure of a monoidal functor (say with respect to $K$) such that $\epsilon$ becomes monoidal, and if $\eta$ is adjusted to satisfy \eqref{eq:triangle_iden}, then also $\eta$ is monoidal.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
We make use of \cite{JT_Notes}\footnote{These are very useful for reading any of \cite{EGNO_TensorCategories}!} and Theorem~\ref{thm:cat_equiv_FFES}.  For $A,B\in\mc B$ consider the diagram:
\[\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=9ex]
  F(G(A)\otimes G(B)) \ar[d, "J_{G(A), G(B)}^{-1}"'] \ar[r, dotted, "F(K_{A,B})"]
   & FG(A\otimes B) \ar[d, "\epsilon_{A\otimes B}"] \\
  FG(A) \otimes FG(B) \ar[r, "\epsilon_A\otimes\epsilon_B"] & A\otimes B
\end{tikzcd}. \]
There is a unique morphism $K_{A,B} \colon G(A)\otimes G(B) \to F(A\otimes B)$ making the diagram commute, as $F$ is fully faithful.  Then $K_{A,B}$ is an isomorphism.  We then claim that $K$ is natural: $K_{A,B}(G(x)\otimes G(y)) = G(x\otimes y)K_{A',B'}$ for $x\colon A'\to A, y\colon B'\to B$.  It suffices to show that $F(K_{A,B}) \circ F(G(x)\otimes G(y)) = FG(x\otimes y) \circ F(K_{A',B'})$, but this is just a calculation:
\begin{align*}
F(K_{A,B}) \circ F(G(x)\otimes G(y))
&= \epsilon_{A\otimes B}^{-1} (\epsilon_A\otimes\epsilon_B) J^{-1}_{G(A), G(B)} F(G(x)\otimes G(y)) \\
&= \epsilon_{A\otimes B}^{-1} (\epsilon_A\otimes\epsilon_B) (FG(x) \otimes FG(y)) J^{-1}_{G(A'), G(B')} \\
&= \epsilon_{A\otimes B}^{-1} (x\otimes y) (\epsilon_{A'}\otimes\epsilon_{B'}) J^{-1}_{G(A'), G(B')} \\
&= FG(x\otimes y)\epsilon_{A'\otimes B'}^{-1} (\epsilon_{A'}\otimes\epsilon_{B'}) J^{-1}_{G(A'), G(B')} \\
&=  FG(x\otimes y) \circ F(K_{A',B'}),
\end{align*}
using naturality throughout.

Next we claim that $\epsilon \colon FG\Rightarrow 1_{\mc B}$ becomes a \emph{monoidal} natural transformation, Definition~\ref{defn:nt_monoidal}.  We need the following diagram to commute
\[\begin{tikzcd}
  FG(A)\otimes FG(B) \ar[r, "L_{A,B}"] \ar[d, "\epsilon_A\otimes\epsilon_B"]  &  FG(A\otimes B) \ar[d, "\epsilon_{A\otimes B}"] \\
  A\otimes B \ar[r, equals] & A\otimes B
\end{tikzcd}, \]
where $L$ is associated to $FG$, Remark~\ref{rem:compose_monoidal_functors}.  As $L_{A,B} = F(K_{A,B}) \circ J_{G(A), G(B)}$, this is just the diagram above which we used to define $K_{A,B}$.

From \cite{JT_Notes}, we now have a problem: is $\eta$ monoidal?  It's suggested there that in general this is not the case, but we can find another $\eta$, still giving an equivalence, which is monoidal, but that the proof is messy and omitted.

I think this is the same as Proposition~\ref{prop:adjunctions_and_equivs}, namely that we want $\eta$ to satisfy the triangle identities \eqref{eq:triangle_iden}.  The proposition shows that we can choose a new $\eta$ that does this.  Then $F(\eta_A) = \epsilon^{-1}_{F(A)}$.  Let $L'$ be associated to $GF$, see Remark~\ref{rem:compose_monoidal_functors}, so $L'_{A,B} = G(J_{A,B}) \circ K_{F(A), F(B)}$.  We take Definition~\ref{defn:nt_monoidal}, expand out $L'$ and apply the faithful functor $F$, so we need to show that the following diagram commutes:
\[\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=10ex]
F(GF(A)\otimes GF(B))  \ar[r, "F(K_{F(A), F(B)})"]  &  FG(F(A)\otimes F(B)) \ar[d, "FG(J_{A,B})"]  \\
F(A\otimes B) \ar[u, "F(\eta_A\otimes\eta_B)"] \ar[r, "F(\eta_{A\otimes B})"]  &  FGF(A\otimes B)
\end{tikzcd}.\]
We now transform this into the following diagram: we have expanded the definition of $K$, while the left square commutes by using naturality of $J$ and the relation between $\eta$ and $\epsilon$:
\[\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=7.5ex]
FGF(A) \otimes FGF(B) \ar[r, "J_{GF(A),GF(B)}"] \ar[d, "\epsilon_{F(A)}\otimes\epsilon_{F(B)}"]
  &  F(GF(A)\otimes GF(B)) \ar[r, "J^{-1}_{GF(A),GF(B)}"]
  &  FGF(A) \otimes FGF(B) \ar[r, "\epsilon_{F(A)}\otimes\epsilon_{F(B)}"]
  &  F(A)\otimes F(B) \ar[d, "\epsilon_{F(A)\otimes F(B)}^{-1}"]  \\
F(A)\otimes F(B) \ar[r, "J_{A,B}"]
  &  F(A\otimes B) \ar[u, "F(\eta_A\otimes\eta_B)"] \ar[r, "F(\eta_{A\otimes B})", "\epsilon^{-1}_{F(A\otimes B)}"' ]
  &  FGF(A\otimes B)
  &  FG(F(A)\otimes F(B)) \ar[l, "FG(J_{A,B})"]
\end{tikzcd}. \]
However, this diagram cancels down to this one:
\[\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=10ex]
F(A)\otimes F(B) \ar[r, "\epsilon_{F(A)\otimes F(B)}^{-1}"] \ar[d, "J_{A,B}"]
  &  FG(F(A)\otimes F(B)) \ar[d, "FG(J_{A,B})"]  \\
F(A\otimes B)  \ar[r, "\epsilon^{-1}_{F(A\otimes B)}" ]
  & FGF(A\otimes B)
\end{tikzcd}, \]
which commutes as $\epsilon$ is natural.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
Let $\mc A,\mc B$ be $C^*$-tensor categories.  Let $F\colon \mc A \to \mc B$ be a unitary monoidal functor, and suppose that $F$ is an equivalence of categories (ignoring the monoidal structure).  Then there is a unitary monoidal functor $G\colon \mc B\to\mc A$ and unitary natural monoidal isomorphisms $FG\Rightarrow 1_{\mc B}$ and $1_{\mc A} \Rightarrow GF$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Use Proposition~\ref{prop:equiv_star_cats} to find a $*$-functor $G$ and unitary natural isomorphisms, as stated.  By Proposition~\ref{prop:upgrade_to_monoidal_equiv}, we can find $K$ which makes $G$ monoidal.  From the way $K$ is constructed, as $F,G$ are $*$-functors, $F$ is unitary (so the components of $J$ are unitary) and $\epsilon$ is unitary, it follows that $K$ is unitary.  The way $\eta$ is constructed, it will satisfy \eqref{eq:triangle_iden}, and so be monoidal.
\end{proof}

The above partially explains the remark at the bottom of page~39 in \cite{NT_CQGsBook}.\footnote{TODO: There are some more remarks there about simple objects}

We haven't given any examples.  \cite[Example~2.1.7]{NT_CQGsBook} and \cite[Sections~2.5, 2.6]{EGNO_TensorCategories} give some examples.

\begin{example}
Consider again Example~\ref{eg:hilb_tensor} of $\catHilb$ and Example~\ref{eq:hilb_skel_tensor} of $\catMat$.  There we defined functors $F\colon \catHilbFD \to \catMat$ and $G \colon \catMat \to \catHilbFD$.  We are a little more precise than before, and fix associativity morphisms on $\catHilb$ such that $\alpha_{H,K,L} \colon (\xi_0\otimes\xi_1)\otimes\xi_2 \mapsto \xi_0\otimes(\xi_1\otimes\xi_2)$.

Consider $G$ and how to define a suitable $K$.  We again use the lexicographic ordering order and define
\[ K_{n,m} \colon G(n)\otimes G(m) = \mathbb C^n \otimes \mathbb C^m \to \mathbb C^{nm} = G(n\otimes m); \quad
e_i\otimes e_j \mapsto e_{im+j}, \]
where $(e_i)_{i=0}^{n-1}$ is the canonical basis of $\mathbb C^n$, and so forth.  Then $K$ is natural because of the way we defined composition in $\catMat$.  As the associativity morphisms for $\catMat$ are trivial, Definition~\ref{defn:monoidal_functor} becomes
\[ \begin{tikzcd}
  (\mathbb C^n\otimes\mathbb C^m)\otimes\mathbb C^p \ar[r, "\phi"] \ar[d, "\alpha"] &  \mathbb C^{nmp}  \\
  \mathbb C^n\otimes(\mathbb C^m\otimes\mathbb C^p) \ar[ru, "\psi"']
\end{tikzcd}
\quad\text{where}\quad
\begin{matrix}
\phi((e_i\otimes e_j)\otimes e_k) = J_{nm,p}(e_{im+j}\otimes e_k) = e_{(im+j)p+k},   \\
\psi(e_i\otimes(e_j\otimes e_k)) = J_{n,mp}(e_i\otimes e_{jp+k}) = e_{imp+jp+k}.
\end{matrix} \]
This diagram commutes by the assumption on $\alpha$.  So $(G,K)$ is monoidal.  As $G(1) = \mathbb C$ we find that $G_0$ is the identity.

To define $F$ we fix unitaries $u_H \colon H \to \mathbb C^{\dim H}$ with $F(x) = u_K x u_H^*$ for $x\colon H\to K$, and we choose $u_{\mathbb C^n} = 1$ for each $n$.  We now follow (the proof of) Proposition~\ref{prop:upgrade_to_monoidal_equiv} (with the roles of $F$ and $G$ swapped) to see that we can define $J$ for $F$ by
\[\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=9ex]
  \mathbb C^{\dim H \dim K} \ar[d, "K_{\dim H, \dim K}^{-1}"'] \ar[r, dotted, "G(J_{H,K})"]
   & \mathbb C^{\dim H \dim K} \ar[d, "\eta^{-1}_{H\otimes K}"] \\
  \mathbb C^{\dim H} \otimes \mathbb C^{\dim K} \ar[r, "\eta^{-1}_H\otimes\eta^{-1}_K"] & H\otimes K
\end{tikzcd}. \]
Recall that $\eta \colon 1 \Rightarrow GF$ has components $\eta_H = u_H$.  So $J_{H,K} \in \mathbb M_{\dim H\dim K}$ is the matrix giving the linear map $u_{H\otimes K} \circ (u_H^* \otimes u_K^*) \circ K_{\dim H \dim K}^*$.  Again, $F_0$ is the identity.

Proposition~\ref{prop:upgrade_to_monoidal_equiv} tells us that $\eta \colon 1 \Rightarrow GF$ and $\epsilon=1\colon FG\Rightarrow 1$ are monoidal.  We compute the natural isomorphism $L$ for $GF$ first, which by Remark~\ref{rem:compose_monoidal_functors} is $L_{H,K} = G(J_{H,K}) \circ K_{\dim H,\dim K} = u_{H\otimes K}(u_H^* \otimes u_K^*) \colon \mathbb C^{\dim H} \otimes \mathbb C^{\dim K} \to \mathbb C^{\dim H\dim K}$ as we might have suspected.  That $\eta$ is monoidal, Definition~\ref{defn:nt_monoidal}, is that the following diagram commutes,
\[\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=10ex]
H \otimes K \ar[r, equals] \ar[d, "\eta_H\otimes\eta_K"', "=u_H\otimes u_K"] & H\otimes K  \ar[d, "\eta_{H\otimes K}"', "=u_{H\otimes K}"] \\
\mathbb C^{\dim H} \otimes \mathbb C^{\dim K} \ar[r, "L_{H,K}"] & \mathbb C^{\dim H\dim K}
\end{tikzcd},\]
but this is clearly the case.
\end{example}


\subsection{Rigid monoidal categories}

As ever, let $\mc A$ be a $C^*$-tensor category, though again in this section, we'll only use properties which hold in any monoidal category.

\begin{definition}\label{defn:leftdual}
Given $A\in\mc A$ we say that an object $A^*$ is a \emph{left dual} of $A$ when there are morphisms $\ev_A \colon A^*\otimes A \to 1, \coev_A \colon 1 \to A \otimes A^*$, the \emph{evaluation} and \emph{coevaluation} morphisms, making the following compositions
\[\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=6.9ex]
  A \ar[r, "\lambda_A^{-1}"]
    & 1 \otimes A \ar[r, "\coev_A \otimes 1"]
    & (A\otimes A^*)\otimes A \ar[r, "\alpha_{A,A^*,A}"]
    & A\otimes (A^*\otimes A) \ar[r, "1\otimes \ev_A"]
    & A\otimes 1 \ar[r, "\rho_{A}"]
    & A  \\
  A^* \ar[r, "\rho_{A^*}^{-1}"]
    & A^* \otimes 1 \ar[r, "1\otimes\coev_A"]
    & A^*\otimes (A\otimes A^*) \ar[r, "\alpha_{A^*,A,A^*}^{-1}"]
    & (A^*\otimes A)\otimes A^* \ar[r, "\ev_A\otimes 1"]
    & 1 \otimes A^* \ar[r, "\lambda_{A^*}"]
    & A^*
\end{tikzcd}\]
both be the respective identity morphisms.  We call these equations the \emph{conjugate equations}.
\end{definition}

We often say that ``$\ev$ and $\coev$ solve the conjugate equations''.

\begin{definition}\label{defn:rightdual}
Given $A\in\mc A$ we say that an object ${^*}A$ is a \emph{right dual} of $A$ when there are morphisms $\ev'_A \colon A\otimes {^*}A \to 1, \coev'_A \colon 1 \to {^*}A \otimes A$, making the following compositions
\[\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=6.9ex]
  A \ar[r, "\rho_A^{-1}"]
    & A \otimes 1 \ar[r, "1 \otimes \coev'_A"]
    & A\otimes ({^*}A\otimes A) \ar[r, "\alpha^{-1}_{A,{}^*A,A}"]
    & (A\otimes {}^*A)\otimes A \ar[r, "\ev'_A \otimes 1"]
    & 1\otimes A \ar[r, "\lambda_{A}"]
    & A  \\
  {^*}A \ar[r, "\lambda_{A^*}^{-1}"]
    & 1 \otimes {}^*A \ar[r, "\coev'_A\otimes 1"]
    & ({}^*A\otimes A)\otimes {}^*A \ar[r, "\alpha_{{}^*A,A,{}^*A}"]
    & {}^*A\otimes (A\otimes {}^*A) \ar[r, "1\otimes \ev'_A"]
    & {}^*A \otimes 1 \ar[r, "\rho_{{}^*A}"]
    & {^*}A
\end{tikzcd}\]
both be the respective identity morphisms.
\end{definition}

There is an obvious symmetry here, in that $A^*$ is a left dual to $A$ if and only if $A$ is a right dual to $A^*$.  Similarly reversing the order of the tensor product swaps the roles of these notions.  Consequently, we tend to just prove results about left duals.  Indeed, in the $C^*$ case, the following remark makes this even clearer.

\begin{remark}\label{rem:cstar_dual_case}
Definition~2.2.1 in \cite{NT_CQGsBook} looks different to Definition~\ref{defn:leftdual}, but it's really the same.  If we set $\ev = R^*$ and $\coev=\overline R$, then the 2nd equation in \cite{NT_CQGsBook} is exactly as above, while the 1st equation is just the adjoint applied to the 1st equation above.  Notice that as we don't assume anything about $\ev, \coev$ (e.g. they are not assumed unitary) taking the adjoint is just a matter of convention.

Furthermore, suppose $A$ has left dual $A^*$.  Define $\ev_A' = \coev_A^* \colon A\otimes A^* \to 1$ and $\coev'_A = \ev_A^* \colon 1 \to A^*\otimes A$.  As $\rho, \lambda$ and $\alpha$ are unitary, applying the adjoint to the (left) conjugate equations gives
\begin{gather*}
A \xrightarrow{\rho_A^{-1}}
  A\otimes 1 \xrightarrow{1\otimes\coev'_A}
  A\otimes (A^*\otimes A) \xrightarrow{\alpha_{A,A^*,A}^{-1}}
  (A\otimes A^*)\otimes A \xrightarrow{\ev'_A\otimes 1}
  1\otimes A \xrightarrow{\lambda_A} A, \\
A^* \xrightarrow{\lambda_{A^*}^{-1}}
  1\otimes A^* \xrightarrow{\coev'_A\otimes 1}
  (A^*\otimes A)\otimes A^* \xrightarrow{\alpha_{A^*,A,A^*}}
  A^*\otimes (A\otimes A^*) \xrightarrow{1\otimes\ev'_A}
  A^*\otimes 1 \xrightarrow{\rho_{A^*}} A^*,
\end{gather*}
which are precisely the right conjugate equations.  So $A^*$ is also a right dual for $A$.
\end{remark}

We have $1^*=1={}^*1$ with evaluation being $\lambda_1=\rho_1$ and coevaluation being the inverse.

\begin{example}\label{eg:hilbfd_rigid}
Again consider $\catHilbFD$.  For $H$ define $H^* = \overline{H}$ the conjugate space to $H$, and $ev_H \colon \overline H \otimes H \to \mathbb C$ by $\overline\xi\otimes\eta = (\xi|\eta)$ the inner-product.  (This is bounded, as $H$ is finite-dimensional.)  Given an orthonormal basis $(e_i)$ for $H$ define $\coev_H(1) = \sum_i e_i \otimes \overline{e_i}$.  This is actually independent of the choice of $(e_i)$, indeed, let $(f_i)$ also be an orthonormal basis, so
\begin{align*}
\sum_i e_i\otimes\overline{e_i}
&= \sum_{i,j} (f_j|e_i)f_j \otimes\overline{e_i}
= \sum_{i,j} f_j \otimes\overline{(e_i|f_j)e_i}
= \sum_j f_j \otimes \overline{f_j}.
\end{align*}
Alternatively, identify $H\otimes\overline{H}$ with $HS(H)$, the Hilbert-Schmidt operators on $H$, by $(\xi\otimes\overline\eta) \colon \theta \mapsto (\eta|\theta) \xi$.  Then $\coev_H(1) = I_H$ the identity operator.

Suppressing the unit maps (which are the identity here) we have
\begin{gather*}
(1\otimes\ev_H)\alpha(\coev_H\otimes 1)(\xi)
= \sum_i e_i \otimes \ev_H(\overline{e_i}\otimes \xi)
= \sum_i (e_i|\xi) e_i = \xi, \\
(\ev_H\otimes 1)\alpha(1\otimes\coev_H)(\overline\xi)
= \sum_i \ev_H(\overline\xi\otimes e_i) \overline{e_i}
= \sum_i (\xi|e_i) \overline{e_i}
= \sum_i (\overline{e_i}|\overline\xi) \overline{e_i}
= \overline{\xi},
\end{gather*}
as required.
\end{example}

\begin{example}\label{eg:hilbfd_rigid_diffmaps}
Consider $\catHilb$, and suppose $H$ has some (left) dual, $K$ say.  We will show that $H$ is finite-dimensional, that we can identify $\overline H$ with $K$ (compare Proposition~\ref{prop:left_dual_unique}) and then fully describe all choices of (co)evaluation morphisms.

Let $(e_i)$ be an orthonormal basis of $H$, possibility infinite.  Then $\coev_H(1) = \sum_i e_i \otimes \xi_i$ for some family $(\xi_i)$ in $K$ with $\sum_i \|\xi_i\|^2 < \infty$ (by definition of the normal on the Hilbert space tensor product).  Then for $\xi\in H$ we have
\begin{equation}
\xi = (1\otimes\ev_H)(\coev_H\otimes 1)\xi = \sum_i \ev_H(\xi_i\otimes\xi) e_i, \notag
\end{equation}
and so for each $i$ we must have $(e_i|\xi) = \ev_H(\xi_i\otimes\xi)$.  However, taking $\xi=e_i$ then shows
\begin{equation}
\ev_H(\xi_i\otimes e_i) = 1 \quad\implies\quad 1 \leq \|\ev_H\| \|\xi_i\|, \notag
\end{equation}
so each $\xi_i$ must be non-zero, but also only finitely many of the $\xi_i$ can be non-zero, if they are to be a square-summable family.  So $H$ is finite-dimensional.

For each $i$ the map $K\ni \eta \mapsto \ev_H(\eta\otimes e_i) \in\mathbb C$ is bounded, so there is $\eta_i\in K$ with $(\eta_i|\eta) = \ev_H(\eta\otimes e_i)$ for all $\eta\in K$.  Then, for $\eta\in K$,
\begin{equation}
\eta = (\ev_H\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_H)\eta = \sum_i \ev_H(\eta\otimes e_i) \xi_i
= \sum_i (\eta_i|\eta) \xi_i. \notag
\end{equation}
So the finite-rank tensor $\sum_i \overline{\eta_i} \otimes \xi_i \in \overline{K} \otimes K$ induces the identity operator on $K$, and hence $K$ is finite-dimensional, and if $(f_j)$ is an orthonormal basis for $K$, then $\sum_i \overline{\eta_i} \otimes \xi_i = \sum_j \overline{f_j} \otimes f_j$.  For each $i$, as $(\eta_i|\xi_i) = \ev_H(\xi_i\otimes e_i) = 1$ from above, we find that $\dim(H) = \sum_i (\eta_i|\xi_i) = \sum_j (f_j|f_j) = \dim(K)$.

So $K$ is isomorphic to $\overline H$ the (usual, Hilbert-space) conjugate to $H$: we identify $(f_j)$ with $(\overline{e_i})$.  Then $\overline{\xi_i} \in H$ and so
\[ H\otimes \overline H \ni \coev_H(1) = \sum_j e_j \otimes \xi_j
= \sum_{i,j} e_j \otimes (\overline{e_i}|\xi_j) \overline{e_i}
= \sum_i u(e_i) \otimes \overline{e_i}, \]
say, where $u\colon H\to H$ is defined by $u(\xi) = \sum_j (\overline{\xi_j}|\xi) e_j$.  Also define $v\colon H\to H$ by $v(\xi) = \sum_i (e_i|\xi) \overline{\eta_i}$, so in particular $v(e_j) = \overline{\eta_j}$ for each $j$.  Then
\[ vu(\xi) = \sum_j (\overline{\xi_j}|\xi) v(e_j) = \sum_j (\overline{\xi_j}|\xi) \overline{\eta_j}
= \sum_i (e_i|\xi) e_i = \xi, \]
as $\sum_j \overline{\eta_j} \otimes \xi_j = \sum_i e_i \otimes \overline{e_i}$.  (Indeed, given $\xi$, we have $f \colon \overline H \to \mathbb C; \overline\eta \mapsto (\overline\xi|\overline\eta) = (\eta|\xi)$ is linear and so $\sum_j (\overline{\xi_j}|\xi) \overline{\eta_j} = (1\otimes f) \sum_j \overline{\eta_j} \otimes \xi_j = \sum_i f(\overline{e_i}) e_i = \sum_i (e_i|\xi) e_i$.)
So $vu=1$ and hence also $uv=1$ as $H$ is finite-dimensional.  Finally,
\[ \ev_H(\overline\eta\otimes\xi) = \sum_i (e_i|\xi) \ev_H(\overline\eta\otimes e_i)
= \sum_i (e_i|\xi) (\eta_i|\overline\eta)
= \sum_i (e_i|\xi) (\eta|v(e_i))
= (\eta|v(\xi)) = (\eta|u^{-1}(\xi)). \]
So $\coev_H$ and $\ev_H$ are described by the invertible operator $u$.

Conversely, given any finite-dimensional $H$ and $u\in\mc B(H)$ invertible, define
\[ \coev_H(1) = \sum_i u(e_i)\otimes \overline{e_i}, \quad \ev_H(\overline\eta\otimes\xi) = (\eta|u^{-1}(\xi)). \]
We then easily calculate that
\begin{align*}
(1\otimes\ev_H)(\coev_H\otimes 1)(\xi)
  &= \sum_i u(e_i) \otimes \ev_H(\overline{e_i}\otimes\xi)
  = \sum_i (e_i|u^{-1}(\xi)) u(e_i) = uu^{-1}(\xi) = \xi, \\
(\ev_H\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_H)(\overline\xi)
  &= \sum_i \ev_H(\overline\xi \otimes u(e_i)) \overline{e_i}
  = \sum_i (\xi|u^{-1}u(e_i)) \overline{e_i} = \overline\xi.
\end{align*}
So we conclude that choices of (co)evaluation maps biject with invertible elements of $\mc B(H)$.
\end{example}

\begin{remark}
From now on, we are going to use ``coherence'' to an increasing degree.  This is because the coevaluation morphism requires us to ``insert'' the unit object into an interated tensor product, while the evaluation morphism requires us to ``absorb'' the unit.  We'll also be dealing with interated tensor products, and so implicitly applying associativity maps freely, often to more than 3 components.  Again, this is where graphical arguments start to seem very appealing.

For example, we wish to consider a map like $1\otimes\coev_A\otimes 1 \colon B\otimes C \to B\otimes A\otimes A^*\otimes C$.  This should be formally interpretted as
\[ B\otimes C \xrightarrow{\rho_B^{-1}\otimes 1}
(B\otimes 1)\otimes C \xrightarrow{(1\otimes\coev_A)\otimes 1}
(B\otimes(A\otimes A^*))\otimes C, \]
after which we could apply various associativity morphisms.  However, we could also formalise this as
\[ B\otimes C \xrightarrow{1\otimes\lambda_C^{-1}}
B\otimes (1\otimes C) \xrightarrow{1\otimes(\coev_A\otimes 1)}
B\otimes((A\otimes A^*)\otimes C) \xrightarrow{\alpha_{B,A\otimes A^*,C}^{-1}}
(B\otimes(A\otimes A^*))\otimes C, \]
which is the same, thanks to axiom \ref{cst-axioms:6} and using that $\alpha$ is natural.

I am not quite sure how to proceed here.  It would be too tedious to fully justify everything formally from the axioms.  I find both \cite{EGNO_TensorCategories} and \cite{NT_CQGsBook} unsatisfactory here, as they seem to skip too much detail, without fully commiting to using coherence: maybe arguing with diagrams would actually be better?
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:left_dual_unique}
If $A\in\mc A$ has a left dual, then the dual is unique up to unique isomorphism (in a way which preserves the (co)evaluation morphisms).
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Suppose $A_1^*, A_2^*$ are both left duals, with (co)evaluation maps $c_i, e_i$ for $i=1,2$.  Define $T \colon A_1^* \to A_2^*$ as the composition
\[\begin{tikzcd}[column sep=10ex]
A_1^* \ar[r, "(1\otimes c_2)\rho_{A_1^*}^{-1}"]
& A_1^*\otimes(A\otimes A_2^*) \ar[r, "\alpha^{-1}"]
& (A_1^*\otimes A) \otimes A_2^* \ar[r, "\lambda_{A_2^*}(e_1\otimes 1)"]
& A_2^*
\end{tikzcd}.\]
Similarly define $S \colon A_2^* \to A_1^*$.  We show that $ST=1$ and $TS=1$.

If we have courage, then we can write $T = (e_1\otimes 1)(1\otimes c_2)\colon A_1^*\to A_2^*$ and $S = (e_2\otimes 1)(1\otimes c_1)\colon A_2^*\to A_1^*$, and then
\begin{align*}
ST &= (e_2\otimes 1)(1\otimes c_1)(e_1\otimes 1)(1\otimes c_2)
= (e_2\otimes 1)(e_1\otimes 1 \otimes c_1)(1\otimes c_2) \\
&= (e_2\otimes 1)(e_1\otimes 1\otimes 1\otimes 1)(1\otimes 1\otimes 1\otimes c_1)(1\otimes c_2) \\
&= (e_1\otimes e_2\otimes 1)(1\otimes c_2\otimes c_1) \\
&= (e_1\otimes 1)(1\otimes 1\otimes e_2\otimes 1)(1\otimes c_2\otimes 1\otimes 1)(1\otimes c_1) \\
&= (e_1\otimes 1)(1\otimes c_1) = 1_{A_1^*},
\end{align*}
and similarly $TS=1$ (which is just by symmetry).

Notice that also $e_2 (T\otimes 1) \colon A_1^* \otimes A \to 1$ is
\begin{align*}
e_2 (T\otimes 1)
&= e_2 (e_1\otimes 1\otimes 1)(1\otimes c_2\otimes 1) 
= e_1 (1\otimes 1\otimes e_2)(1\otimes c_2\otimes 1) \\
&= e_1 (1 \otimes (1\otimes e_2)(c_2\otimes 1)) = e_1 (1\otimes 1_A) = e_1.
\end{align*}
So also $e_1 (S\otimes 1) = e_2$.  Similarly,
\begin{align*}
(1\otimes T)c_1 &= (1\otimes e_1\otimes 1)(1\otimes 1\otimes c_2)c_1
= (1\otimes e_1\otimes 1)(c_1\otimes 1\otimes 1) c_2
= c_2
\end{align*}
and $(1\otimes S)c_2 = c_1$.

Suppose $T' \colon A_1^* \to A_2^*$ also satisfies $e_2(T'\otimes 1) = e_1$.  Then
\[ T' = (e_2\otimes 1)(1\otimes c_2) T' = (e_2 \otimes 1)(T'\otimes 1\otimes 1)(1\otimes c_2)
= (e_1\otimes 1)(1\otimes c_2) = T. \]
Similarly, if $(1\otimes T')c_1 = c_2$ then $T' = T' (e_1\otimes 1)(1\otimes c_1) = (e_1\otimes 1)(1\otimes 1\otimes T')(1\otimes c_1) = (e_1\otimes 1)(1\otimes c_2) = T$.  So, in this way, the isomorphism is unique.
\end{proof}

For the next lemma, remember Example~\ref{eg:hilbfd_rigid_diffmaps}.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:one_ev_for_coev}
Given some objects $A, A^*\in\mc A$ and a map $\ev \colon A^*\otimes A\to 1$ there is at most one map $\coev\colon 1\to A\otimes A^*$ which solves the conjugate equations.  Similarly, given $\coev$ there is at most one $\ev$ solving the conjugate equations.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $\ev, \coev$ solve the conjugate equations.  If $c \colon 1 \to A\otimes A^*$ also satisfies $(\ev\otimes 1)(1\otimes c) = 1_{A^*}$ then
\begin{align*}
c &= (1\otimes 1)c = ((1\otimes\ev)(\coev\otimes 1)\otimes 1)c = (1\otimes\ev\otimes 1)(\coev\otimes 1\otimes 1)c \\
&= (1 \otimes \ev \otimes 1)(1\otimes 1\otimes c) \coev = (1\otimes 1)\coev = \coev.
\end{align*}
Similarly, if $e \colon A^*\otimes A \to 1$ satisfies $(1\otimes e)(\coev\otimes 1) = 1_A$ then
\begin{align*}
e = e ((\ev\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev) \otimes 1) = \ev(1\otimes1\otimes e)(1\otimes\coev\otimes 1) = \ev.
\end{align*}
\end{proof}

% \begin{example}
% While the (left/right) dual is unique, the choice of morphism might not be.  Continue with Example~\ref{eg:hilbfd_rigid} for $\catHilbFD$.  Given $H$ let $u\in\mc B(H)$ be invertible, let $(e_i)$ be an orthonormal basis of $H$, and define
% \[ \ev'(\overline\xi\otimes\eta) = (\xi|u^{-1}(\eta)), \quad \coev'(1) = \sum_i u(e_i) \otimes \overline{e_i}. \]
% Computing as before, we find that
% \begin{gather*}
% (1\otimes\ev')(\coev'\otimes 1)(\xi) = \sum_i u(e_i) \ev'(\overline{e_i}\otimes\xi)
%    = u \sum_i (e_i|u^{-1}\xi) e_i = uu^{-1}\xi = \xi, \\
% (\ev'\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev')(\overline\eta) = \sum_i \ev'(\overline\eta \otimes u(e_i)) \overline{e_i}
% = \sum_i (\eta|u^{-1}u(e_i)) \overline{e_i} = \overline\eta,
% \end{gather*}
% and so $\ev'$ and $\coev'$ solve the conjugate equations.

% Again, if we view $H\otimes\overline H$ as $\mc B(H)$ (with the Hilbert--Schmidt norm) then $\coev'$ is the map $\xi \mapsto \sum_i (e_i|\xi) u(e_i) = u(\xi)$.  By Lemma~\ref{lem:one_ev_for_coev}, $\ev'$ is uniquely determined by $\coev'$.  Thus the possible choices for (co)evaluation morphisms are in bijection with the invertible maps in $\mc B(H)$.
% \end{example}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:func_preserves_dual}
Let $F \colon \mc A\to\mc B$ be a monoidal functor with respect to $J$.  Given $A\in\mc A$ with dual $A^*$, we have that $F(A^*)$ is a dual to $F(A)$, with (co)evaluation morphisms
\begin{gather*}
\ev_{F(A)} : F(A^*) \otimes F(A) \xrightarrow{J_{A^*,A}} F(A^*\otimes A) \xrightarrow{F(\ev_A)} F(1) \xrightarrow{F_0^{-1}} 1, \\
\coev_{F(A)} : 1 \xrightarrow{F_0} F(1) \xrightarrow{F(\coev_A)} F(A\otimes A^*) \xrightarrow{J_{A,A^*}^{-1}} F(A) \otimes F(A^*).
\end{gather*}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
This is a tedious but simple diagram chase; or as here, algebraically:
\begin{align*}
& \rho_{F(A)}(1\otimes\ev_{F(A)})\alpha(\coev_{F(A)}\otimes 1)\lambda^{-1}_{F(A)} \\
&= \rho_{F(A)} (1\otimes F_0^{-1})(1\otimes F(\ev_A))(1\otimes J) \alpha (J^{-1}\otimes 1)(F(\coev_A)\otimes 1)(F_0\otimes 1) \lambda^{-1}_{F(A)} \\
&= \rho_{F(A)} \rho_{F(A)}^{-1} F(\rho_A) J (1\otimes F(\ev_A))(1\otimes J) \alpha (J^{-1}\otimes 1)(F(\coev_A)\otimes 1)J^{-1}F(\lambda_A^{-1})\lambda_{F(A)} \lambda^{-1}_{F(A)} \\
&= F(\rho_A) J (1\otimes F(\ev_A)) J^{-1} F(\alpha) J (F(\coev_A)\otimes 1)J^{-1}F(\lambda_A^{-1}) \\
&= F(\rho_A) F(1\otimes \ev_A) F(\alpha) F(\coev_A\otimes 1) F(\lambda_A^{-1}) = F(1_A) = 1_A.
\end{align*}
In the first line we expand the definitions; in the second we use Proposition~\ref{prop:F0_action_general}; in the third we cancel and use the condition $J$ satisfies; in the fourth that $J$ is natural; and then finally the first conjugate equation.  Verifying the other conjugate equation is similar.
\end{proof}

We quickly look at \emph{dual} morphisms for which we use a different notation, to avoid clashing with the $C^*$-adjoint.  Let $A,B\in\mc A$ have left duals, and let $x\colon A\to B$.  Then the \emph{left dual} of $x$ is $x^\sharp \colon B^* \to A^*$ given by
\begin{align*}
B^* \xrightarrow{\lambda^{-1}_{B^*}}
B^*\otimes 1 \xrightarrow{1\otimes\coev_A}
B^*\otimes(A\otimes A^*) \xrightarrow{\alpha^{-1}}
(B^*\otimes A)\otimes A^* \xrightarrow{(1\otimes x)\otimes 1}
(B^*\otimes B)\otimes A^* \xrightarrow{\ev_B\otimes 1}
1\otimes A^* \xrightarrow{\lambda_{A^*}}
A^*
\end{align*}
or more succiently, $x^\sharp = (\ev_B\otimes 1)(1\otimes x\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_A)$.

\begin{example}\label{eg:conj_Hilb}
Continue with Example~\ref{eg:hilbfd_rigid_diffmaps} of $\catHilbFD$ and $u\in\mc B(H)$ defining $\ev_H$ and $\coev_H$.  Similarly let $v\in\mc B(K)$ define $\ev_K, \coev_K$.  Let $x\colon H\to K$.  Then for $\xi\in K$,
\begin{align*}
x^\sharp(\overline\xi)
= (\ev_K\otimes 1)(1\otimes x\otimes 1)\sum_i \overline\xi \otimes v(e_i) \otimes \overline{e_i}
= \sum_i (\xi|u^{-1}xv(e_i)) \overline{e_i}
= \overline{v^*x^*(u^{-1})^*(\xi)}
= (u^{-1}xv)^\top \overline\xi.
\end{align*}
Hence when $u=1, v=1$ we find that $x^\sharp = x^\top \colon \overline K \to \overline H$, the ``transpose'' of $x$.
\end{example}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:dual_morphism_properties}
The map $\hom(A,B) \to \hom(B^*, A^*); x \mapsto x^\sharp$ is bijective, with inverse $y \mapsto {}^\sharp y = (1_B\otimes\ev_A)(1\otimes y\otimes 1)(\coev_B\otimes 1_A)$.  When it makes sense, we have $(x\circ y)^\sharp = y^\sharp \circ x^\sharp$.
%and that $B^*\otimes A^*$ is a (left) dual to $A\otimes B$.  (TODO: Reference this below!)
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
We compute that
\begin{align*}
{}^\sharp(x^\sharp)
&= (1\otimes\ev_A)(1 \otimes x^\sharp\otimes 1)(\coev_B\otimes 1) \\
&= (1\otimes\ev_A)(1\otimes\ev_B\otimes1^{\otimes 2})(1^{\otimes 2}\otimes x\otimes 1^{\otimes 2})(1^{\otimes 2}\otimes\coev_A\otimes 1)(\coev_B\otimes 1) \\
&= (1\otimes\ev_B\otimes\ev_A)(1^{\otimes 2}\otimes x\otimes 1^{\otimes 2})(\coev_B\otimes\coev_A\otimes 1) \\
&= (1\otimes\ev_B)(1\otimes 1\otimes x)(\coev_B\otimes 1) (1\otimes\ev_A)(\coev_A\otimes 1) \\
&= (1\otimes\ev_B)(\coev_B\otimes 1) x = x,
\end{align*}
(here abusing notation, as the unit morphisms act on different objects, so $1^{\otimes 2}$ doesn't quite make sense) and more quickly,
\begin{align*}
({}^\sharp y)^\sharp
%&= (\ev_B\otimes 1)(1\otimes {}^\sharp y\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_A) \\
&= (\ev_B\otimes 1)(1\otimes 1\otimes \ev_A\otimes 1)(1^{\otimes 2}\otimes y\otimes1^{\otimes 2})(1\otimes\coev_B\otimes 1^{\otimes 2})(1\otimes\coev_A) \\
&= (\ev_A\otimes 1)(y\otimes 1\otimes 1) (\ev_B\otimes 1^{\otimes3})(1\otimes\coev_B\otimes\coev_A) \\
&= (\ev_A\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_A) y = y.
\end{align*}
So we have mutual inverses.

Given $x \colon A\to B, y\colon B\to C$ we have $(yx)^\sharp = (\ev_C \otimes 1)(1\otimes yx\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_A) \colon C^* \to A^*$.  Thus
\begin{align*}
(yx)^\sharp
&= (\ev_C \otimes 1)(1\otimes y\otimes 1) \bigl(1\otimes (1\otimes\ev_B)(\coev_B\otimes 1)\otimes 1\bigr) (1\otimes x\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_A) \\
&= (\ev_C(1\otimes y)\otimes 1) (1\otimes 1\otimes\ev_B\otimes 1) (1\otimes\coev_B\otimes 1\otimes 1) (1\otimes (x\otimes 1)\coev_A) \\
&= (\ev_B\otimes 1)(\ev_C(1\otimes y)\otimes 1^{\otimes3})(1^{\otimes 3}\otimes (x\otimes 1)\coev_A) (1\otimes\coev_B) \\
&= (\ev_B\otimes 1)(1\otimes x\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_A) (\ev_C\otimes 1)(1\otimes y\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_B)
= x^\sharp y^\sharp.
\end{align*}
Again, this sort of proof is much easier if we allow ourselves to use the graphical calculus.
% We define (co)evaluation morphisms for $A\otimes B$ by
% \begin{gather*}
% \ev_{A\otimes B} \colon (B^*\otimes A^*)\otimes(A\otimes B)\to 1; \quad
%   \ev_{A\otimes B} = \ev_B\circ (1\otimes\ev_A\otimes 1), \\
% \coev_{A\otimes B} \colon 1 \to (A\otimes B)\otimes (B^*\otimes A^*); \quad
%   \coev_{A\otimes B} = (1\otimes\coev_B\otimes 1)\circ\coev_A.
% \end{gather*}
% Then, for example, the 2nd conjugate equation holds as
% \begin{align*}
% (\ev_{A\otimes B}\otimes 1_{A\otimes B})(1_{A\otimes B}\otimes\coev_{A\otimes B})
% &= (\ev_B \otimes 1\otimes 1) (1\otimes\ev_A\otimes 1^{\otimes3}) (1^{\otimes3}\otimes\coev_B\otimes 1) (1\otimes 1\otimes\coev_A) \\
% &= (\ev_B \otimes 1\otimes 1)  (1\otimes\coev_B\otimes 1) (1\otimes\ev_A\otimes 1) (1\otimes 1\otimes\coev_A) \\
% &= 1_{A} \otimes 1_{B}.
% \end{align*}
\end{proof}

We now come to \emph{Frobenius reciprocity}.

\begin{proposition}
Given $A\in\mc A$ we have:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item Let $A^*$ be a left dual to $A$.  Then we have natural adjunction isomorphisms
  \begin{gather}
    \hom(B\otimes A, C) \cong \hom(B, C\otimes A^*),   \label{eq:frob_1}  \\
    \hom(A^*\otimes B, C) \cong \hom(B, A\otimes C)   \label{eq:frob_2}
  \end{gather}
  for all $B,C\in\mc A$.
  \item Let ${^*}A$ be a right dual to $A$.  Then we have natural adjunction isomorphisms
  \begin{gather}
    \hom(B\otimes {^*}A, C) \cong \hom(B, C\otimes A),   \label{eq:frob_1r}  \\
    \hom(A\otimes B, C) \cong \hom(B, {^*}A\otimes C)   \label{eq:frob_2r}
  \end{gather}
  for all $B,C\in\mc A$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

By ``natural adjunction isomorphism'' we mean that \eqref{eq:nat_im_adj1} and \eqref{eq:nat_im_adj2} hold (see Appendix~\ref{sec:cat}): see the proof for more details.

\begin{proof}
We prove case \eqref{eq:frob_1} carefully.  Following the notation in Appendix~\ref{sec:cat} we denote the isomorphism by
$\hom(B\otimes A, C) \ni x \mapsto x^\natural = (x\otimes 1_{A^*})(1_B\otimes\coev_A)$ with inverse $\hom(B,C\otimes A^*) \ni y\mapsto y^\natural = (1_C\otimes\ev_A)(y\otimes 1_A)$.  We first check that these are mutual inverses:
\begin{align*}
x^{\natural\natural} &= (1_C\otimes\ev_A)(F(x)\otimes 1_A)
= (1_C\otimes\ev_A)(x\otimes 1_{A^*}\otimes 1_A) (1_B\otimes\coev_A\otimes 1_A) \\
&= x (1_{B\otimes A}\otimes\ev_A) (1_B\otimes\coev_A\otimes 1_A) = x(1_B\otimes 1_A) = x, \\
y^{\natural\natural} &= (G(y)\otimes 1_{A^*})(1_B\otimes\coev_A)
= (1_C\otimes\ev_A\otimes 1_{A^*})(y\otimes 1_A\otimes 1_{A^*})(1_B\otimes\coev_A) \\
&= (1_C\otimes\ev_A\otimes 1_{A^*})(1_{C\otimes A^*}\otimes \coev_A) y
= (1_C\otimes 1_{A^*}) y = y.
\end{align*}
To check that we have an adjunction, we check \eqref{eq:nat_im_adj1} which is
\[  (x'x\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_A) =
(B\otimes A \xrightarrow{x} C_1 \xrightarrow{x'} C_2)^\natural
= B \xrightarrow{x^\natural} C_1\otimes A^* \xrightarrow{x'\otimes 1} C_2\otimes A^*
= (x'\otimes 1) (x\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_A), \]
which obviously holds, and \eqref{eq:nat_im_adj2} which is
\[ (1\otimes\ev_A)(yx\otimes 1)
= (B_1 \xrightarrow{x} B_2 \xrightarrow{y} C \otimes A^*)^\natural
= B_1\otimes A \xrightarrow{x\otimes 1} B_2\otimes A \xrightarrow{y^\natural} C
= (1\otimes\ev_A)(y\otimes 1)(x\otimes 1), \]
again holding.

The isomorphisms in \eqref{eq:frob_2} are
\begin{gather*}
  \hom(A^*\otimes B, C) \ni x \mapsto x^\natural = (1_A\otimes x)(\coev_A\otimes 1_B), \\
  \hom(B, A\otimes C) \ni y \mapsto y^\natural = (\ev_A\otimes 1_C)(1_{A^*}\otimes y).
\end{gather*}
\end{proof}

Notice that by Frobenius reciprocity, $\hom(A, B) \cong \hom(\conj B \otimes A,1) \cong \hom(\conj B, \conj A)$, where the isomorphisms are $\hom(A,B) \ni x \mapsto x^\flat$ with $x^\flat = \ev_B(1\otimes x) \in \hom(\conj B \otimes A,1)$, and $\hom(\conj B\otimes A,1) \ni y \mapsto y^\flat$ where $y^\flat = (y\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_A) \in \hom(\conj B, \conj A)$.  The composition is $x \mapsto (\ev_B(1\otimes x)\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_A) = x^\sharp$ and we cover the dual morphism notion.

\begin{definition}
We say that $\mc A$ is \emph{rigid} if every object has a left and right dual.
\end{definition}

By Remark~\ref{rem:cstar_dual_case}, in the $C^*$-case, we only need left duals.  We explore this situation in the next section.


\subsection{Intrinsic dimension}

We now follow \cite[Section~2.2]{NT_CQGsBook} a little more, just working with $C^*$-tensor categories.  Given Remark~\ref{rem:cstar_dual_case}, we may write $\conj{A}$ for the \emph{conjugate} of $A$ which is both a left and right dual (if it exists) and make the choice that $\ev_A' = \coev_A^*, \coev_A' = \ev_A^*$ (which is consistent with the notation of ``$R$'' (which is $\ev_A^*$) and ``$\conj{R}$'' (which is $\coev_A$) in \cite[Definition~2.2.1]{NT_CQGsBook}).

We will generally now assume the extra axioms \ref{cst-axioms:9} and \ref{cst-axioms:10}, that $\mc A$ has subobjects and (finite) direct-sums.  

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:directsum_subobj_conj}
Let $A_1, A_2$ be objects with conjugates.  Then $A_1\oplus A_2$ has a conjugate, which is identified with $\conj{A}_1 \oplus \conj{A}_2$.  Let $A$ have a conjugate, and let $B$ be a subobject of $A$.  Then $B$ has a conjugate.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Consider $A =A_1\oplus A_2$ with inclusion $u_i \in \hom(A_1,A)$, so $u_i^*u_i = 1_{A_i}$, $\sum_i u_i u_i^* = 1_A$, and $u_1^* u_2 = 0$ and $u_2^* u_1=0$.  Similarly form $\conj{A} = \conj{A_1} \oplus \conj{A_2}$ with inclusions $v_1, v_2$.  Define
\[ \ev_A = \sum_{i=1}^2 \ev_{A_i} \circ (v_i^* \otimes u_i^*), \qquad \coev_A = \sum_{i=1}^2 (u_i\otimes v_i) \circ \coev_{A_i}. \]
We then simply calculate that
\begin{align*}
& (1\otimes\ev_A)(\coev_A\otimes 1)
= \sum_{i,j} (1\otimes \ev_{A_i}(v_i^*\otimes u_i^*)) ((u_j\otimes v_j)\coev_{A_j}\otimes 1) \\
&= \sum_{i,j} u_j (1\otimes\ev_{A_i})(1 \otimes v_i^* v_j \otimes 1)(\coev_{A_j}\otimes 1) u_i^*
= \sum_{i} u_i (1\otimes\ev_{A_i}) (\coev_{A_i}\otimes 1) u_i^* = \sum_i u_i u_i^* = 1,
\\
& (\ev_A\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_A)
= \sum_{i,j} (\ev_{A_i}(v_i^*\otimes u_i^*) \otimes 1) (1 \otimes (u_j\otimes v_j)\coev_{A_j})  \\
&= \sum_{i,j} v_j (\ev_{A_i}\otimes 1) (1\otimes u_i^*u_j \otimes 1) (1 \otimes \coev_{A_j}) v_i^* 
= \sum_{i} v_i (\ev_{A_i}\otimes 1) (1 \otimes \coev_{A_i}) v_i^*
= \sum_i v_i v_i^* = 1
\end{align*}
