verifying the conjugate equations.

Now let $A$ have conjugate $\conj{A}$, and let $B$ be a subobject, described by an isometry $u\in\hom(B,A)$.  Set $e = uu^*\in\hom(A)$ a projection, and recall the dual operator $e^\sharp = (\ev_A\otimes 1)(1\otimes e\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_A) \in \hom(\conj{A})$.  By the axiom, there is an object $\conj{B}$ and an isometry $v\in\hom(\conj B, \conj A)$ with $vv^* = e^\sharp$.  Define
\[ \ev_B = \ev_A \circ (v\otimes u), \qquad \coev_B = (u^*\otimes v^*)\circ \coev_A. \]
We now carefully calculate
\begin{align*}
(1\otimes\ev_B)(\coev_B\otimes 1)
&= (1\otimes\ev_A)(1\otimes v\otimes u)(u^*\otimes v^*\otimes 1) (\coev_A\otimes 1) \\
&= u^* (1\otimes\ev_A) (1\otimes e^\sharp\otimes 1) (\coev_A\otimes 1) u \\
&= u^* (1\otimes\ev_A) (1\otimes (\ev_A\otimes 1)(1\otimes e\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_A) \otimes 1) (\coev_A\otimes 1) u \\
&= u^* (1\otimes\ev_A\otimes\ev_A) (1\otimes (1\otimes e\otimes 1) \otimes 1) (\coev_A\otimes\coev_A\otimes 1) u \\
&= u^* (1\otimes\ev_A)(1\otimes 1\otimes e) (1^{\otimes 3}\otimes\ev_A)(\coev_A\otimes\coev_A\otimes 1) u \\
&= u^* (1\otimes\ev_A)(1\otimes 1\otimes e) (\coev_A\otimes 1) u
= u^* e u = u^* u u^* u = 1,
\\
(\ev_B \otimes 1)(1 \otimes \coev_B)
&= (\ev_A\otimes 1)(v\otimes u\otimes 1)(1\otimes u^*\otimes v^*)(1\otimes\coev_A) \\
&= v^*  (\ev_A\otimes 1)(1\otimes e \otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_A)  v
= v^* e^\sharp v = 1,
\end{align*}
again verifying the conjugate equations.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:tensor_conj}
Let $A_1, A_2$ be objects with conjugates.  Then $A_1 \otimes A_2$ has $\conj{A}_2 \otimes \conj{A}_1$ as a conjugate.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Set $A = A_1 \otimes A_2$ and $\conj A = \conj{A}_2 \otimes \conj{A}_1$, and define
\[ \ev_A = \ev_{A_2}(1\otimes\ev_{A_1}\otimes 1), \qquad
\coev_A = (1\otimes\coev_{A_2}\otimes 1)\coev_{A_1}. \]
As ever, we now calculate:
\begin{align*}
(1_A\otimes\ev_A) & (\coev_A\otimes 1_A) \\
&= (1_{A_1}\otimes 1_{A_2} \otimes \ev_{A_2}) (1_{A_1}\otimes 1_{A_2} \otimes 1_{\conj A_2} \otimes \ev_{A_1} \otimes 1_{A_2})
  \\ &\qquad\qquad
  (1_{A_1} \otimes \coev_{A_2} \otimes 1_{\conj A_1} \otimes 1_{A_1}\otimes 1_{A_2})
  (\coev_{A_1}\otimes 1_{A_1}\otimes 1_{A_2}) \\
&= (1_{A_1}\otimes 1_{A_2} \otimes \ev_{A_2})
  (1_{A_1} \otimes \coev_{A_2} \otimes 1_{A_2})
  (1_{A_1} \otimes \ev_{A_1} \otimes 1_{A_2})
  (\coev_{A_1}\otimes 1_{A_1}\otimes 1_{A_2}) \\
&= 1_{A_1} \otimes 1_{A_2} = 1_A,
\\
(\ev_A\otimes 1_{\conj A}) & (1_{\conj A}\otimes\coev_A) \\
&= (\ev_{A_2}\otimes 1_{\conj A_2}\otimes 1_{\conj A_1})
  (1_{\conj A_2} \otimes \ev_{A_1} \otimes 1_{A_2}\otimes 1_{\conj A_2}\otimes 1_{\conj A_1})
  \\ &\qquad\qquad
  (1_{\conj A_2}\otimes 1_{\conj A_1}\otimes 1_{A_1} \otimes \coev_{A_2} \otimes 1_{\conj A_1})
  (1_{\conj A_2}\otimes 1_{\conj A_1}\otimes \coev_{A_1}) \\
&= (\ev_{A_2}\otimes 1_{\conj A_2}\otimes 1_{\conj A_1})
  (1_{\conj A_2}\otimes \coev_{A_2} \otimes 1_{\conj A_1})
  (1_{\conj A_2} \otimes \ev_{A_1} \otimes 1_{\conj A_1})
  (1_{\conj A_2}\otimes 1_{\conj A_1}\otimes \coev_{A_1}) = 1_{\conj A},
\end{align*}
as required.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
Let $\mc A$ be a $C^*$-tensor category with subobjects and direct sums.  The collection of objects with a conjugate forms a sub-tensor catgeory.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Let $\mc B$ be the full subcategory with objects admitting a conjugate.  By Proposition~\ref{prop:directsum_subobj_conj}, $\mc B$ has subobjects and direct sums.  By Proposition~\ref{prop:tensor_conj}, $\mc B$ is closed under the tensor product.
\end{proof}

We now assume further the axiom \ref{cst-axioms:11}, that $\hom(1)\cong\mathbb C$.  Note that we cannot expect the following to hold without this assumption, compare Example~\ref{eg:tensor_bimods}.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:conj_implies_fd}
Let $A$ have a conjugate.  Then $\hom(A)$ is a finite-dimensional $C^*$-algebra.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Given $x\in\hom(A)$ set $y = (1\otimes x)\ev_A^* \in \hom(1, \conj{A}\otimes A)$.  Then
\[ x = x (\coev_A^*\otimes 1)(1\otimes\ev_A^*) = 
(\coev_A^*\otimes 1)(1\otimes (1\otimes x)\ev_A^*)
= (\coev_A^*\otimes 1)(1\otimes y), \]
where here we identify $\hom(A\otimes 1, 1\otimes A) \cong \hom(A)$.  Define $\varphi \colon \hom(A) \to \hom(1) \cong \mathbb C$ by $\psi(a) = \ev_A(1\otimes a)\ev_A^*$ so $\psi$ is a positive linear functional, and notice that $y^*y = \varphi(x^*x)$.  Thus in the $C^*$-algebra $\hom(A)$ we have
\[ x^*x \leq \|\coev_A \coev_A^*\| (1\otimes y^*y) = \|\coev_A\|^2 \varphi(x^*x) 1. \]

Let $f = \|\coev_A\|^2 \varphi$ a positive linear functional on $\hom(A)$.  We've just shown that $x^*x \leq f(x^*x)1$ for each $x\in\hom(A)$.  This implies that $\hom(A)$ is finite-dimensional.  We give one argument, using \cite[Corollary~1]{Sakai_wkly_cmpt_op_algs} which says that if $\hom(A)$ is isomorphic to a Hilbert space, it is finite-dimensional.  Given such an $f$ let $(H_0, \pi_0, \xi_0)$ be the GNS construction, so $x^*x \leq \|\pi_0(x)\xi_0\|^2 1$ for $x\in\hom(A)$.  Hence $\|x\| \leq \|\pi_0(x)\xi_0\| \leq \|\xi_0\| \|x\|$ for every $x$, so $\hom(A)\to H_0; x\mapsto \pi_0(x)$ is a linear isomorphism, that is, $\hom(A)$ is isomorphic to the Hilbert space $H_0$, and hence $\hom(A)$ is finite-dimensional.
\end{proof}

We recall some facts about finite-dimensional $C^*$-algebras, \cite[Section~I.11]{TakesakiI} for example.  Every finite-dimensional $C^*$-algebra is the (finite) direct sum of matrix algebras, say $A \cong \bigoplus_{\alpha \in I} \mathbb M_{n(\alpha)}$.  The unit of the matrix block $\mathbb M_{n(\alpha)}$ is $1_\alpha\in A$, say, which is a mininal central projection, and $(1_\alpha)_{\alpha\in I}$ forms a complete family of the minimal central projections in $A$.  We see that $A$ has non-trivial projections if and only if $A$ is not just $\mathbb C$ (which can fail for infinite-dimensional $C^*$-algebras!)  

Recall that we define, Definition~\ref{defn:simple_obj}, an object $A$ to be simple if it has no non-trivial subobjects, equivalently, $\hom(A)$ has no non-trivial projections.

\begin{proposition}
Every object with a conjugate decomposes into a finite direct sum of simple objects.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Let $A$ have a conjugate, so by Theorem~\ref{thm:conj_implies_fd}, $\hom(A)$ is a finite-dimensional $C^*$-algebra and so the direct sum of matrix algebras.  Let $(1_\alpha)_{\alpha\in I}$ be the minimal central projections of $\hom(A)$, so the units of each matrix block.  For each $\alpha$, by \ref{cst-axioms:9}, there is $A_\alpha\in\mc A$ and an isometry $u_\alpha \colon A_\alpha \to A$ with $u_\alpha u_\alpha^* = 1_\alpha$.  By \ref{cst-axioms:10}, we can form $\bigoplus_{\alpha\in I} A_\alpha$, as $I$ is finite, with inclusion morphisms $v_\alpha$.  Then the morphism
\[ u \colon A \to \bigoplus_{\alpha\in I} A_\alpha; \quad u =\sum_\alpha v_\alpha u_\alpha^* \]
is unitary, as $u^*u = \sum_{\alpha,\beta} u_\beta v_\beta^* v_\alpha u_\alpha^* = \sum_\alpha u_\alpha u_\alpha^* = \sum_\alpha 1_\alpha = 1$, and similarly $uu^*=1$.  For each $\alpha$, the map $\hom(A_\alpha) \to \hom(A); a \mapsto u_\alpha a u_\alpha^*$ is a $*$-homomorphism with image $1_\alpha \hom(A) 1_\alpha$ which is a single matrix block.

So we've reduced the problem to when $\hom(A)$ is a single matrix block, say $\hom(A) \cong \mathbb M_n$.  Let $(e_{ij})$ be the matrix units, so $(e_{ii})_{i=1}^n$ are minimal projections with $\sum_i e_{ii} = 1$.  Repeating the same argument shows that $A \cong \oplus_{i=1}^n A_i$ where $A_i$ is an object together with an isometry $u_i \colon A_i \to A$ with $u_i u_i^* = e_{ii}$.  Then $\hom(A_i) \cong e_{ii} A e_{ii} \cong \mathbb C$ and so $A_i$ is simple.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
We can actually extract a bit more from the proof.  We claim that for $\alpha\not=\beta$ we have $\hom(A_\alpha, A_\beta) = \{0\}$.  Indeed, let $x \in \hom(A_\alpha, A_\beta)$ so $y = u_\beta x u_\alpha^* \in \hom(A)$ and $y = u_\beta u_\beta^* u_\beta x u_\alpha^* u_\alpha u_\alpha^* = 1_\beta y 1_\alpha = y 1_\beta 1_\alpha$ as $1_\beta$ is central.  As $\alpha\not=\beta$ we have $y = 0$ when $x = u_\beta^* y u_\alpha = 0$ as well.  So $A_\alpha, A_\beta$ are inequivalent objects.

When $\hom(A)$ is a single matrix block, the ``off-diagonal'' matrix entries $e_{ij}\in\hom(A)$ can be used to show that $A_i \cong A_j$ for each $i,j$.  Indeed, $u = u_j^* e_{ji} u_i \hom(A_i, A_j)$ satisfies $u^*u = u_i^* e_{ij} u_j u_j^* e_{ji} u_i = u_i^* e_{ij} e_{jj} e_{ji} u_i = u_i^* e_{ii} u_i = 1$ and similarly $uu^*=1$.

Thus, if $\hom(A) \cong \bigoplus_{\alpha \in I} \mathbb M_{n(\alpha)}$ then $A \cong \bigoplus_\alpha n(\alpha) B_\alpha$ where $(B_\alpha)$ are pairwise non-isomorphic simple objects and $n(\alpha) B_\alpha$ means $n(\alpha)$ copies of $B_\alpha$.
\end{remark}

So now we are assuming all the axioms, and we further assume every object has a conjugate.  

\begin{proposition}[Schur's Lemma]
An object $A$ is simple if and only if $\hom(A) = \mathbb C1_A$.  When $A,B$ are simple objects, $\hom(A,B)=\{0\}$ or $\hom(A,B)=\mathbb C u$ for some unitary $u$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Let $A$ be simple, meaning $\hom(A)$ has no proper projections.  As $\hom(A)$ is finite-dimensional, necessary $\hom(A) = \mathbb C$ (by the spectral theorem, for example).  The converse, that $\hom(A)=\mathbb C$ implies $A$ is simple, is clear.

Let $A,B$ be simple, and suppose $x \in \hom(A,B)$ is non-zero.  Then $x^*x = t 1_A$ for some $t>0$, and $xx^* = s1_B$ for some $s>0$, by the first part.  Let $u = t^{-1/2} x \in \hom(A,B)$ so $u^*u = 1$ and $uu^* = t^{-1}s 1$, but $s1 = xx^* = x 1_A x^* = t^{-1} x (x^*x) x^* = t^{-1} s^2 1$ so $s=t$ and hence $uu^*=1$.  Suppose $v\in\hom(A,B)$ is also unitary, so $u^*v \in \hom(A)$ is unitary and hence non-zero multiple of $1$, so $v \in \mathbb Cu$.
\end{proof}

The following doesn't use the axioms \ref{cst-axioms:9}--\ref{cst-axioms:11}, but it seems sensible to state it here and not earlier.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:simple_passes_to_conj_etc}
Suppose that $A$ is simple, meaning $\hom(A)=\mathbb C$, with conjugate $\conj{A}$.  Then $\conj{A}$ is simple and $\hom(1,\conj{A}\otimes A), \hom(1,A\otimes\conj{A})$ are one-dimensional.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
By Frobenius reciprocity, \eqref{eq:frob_2r}, we have $\hom(1,\conj{A}\otimes A) \cong \hom(A\otimes 1, A) \cong \hom(A) = \mathbb C$, and similarly \eqref{eq:frob_1} shows that $\hom(1,A\otimes\conj{A}) \cong \hom(1\otimes A, A) \cong \mathbb C$.  Finally, $\hom(\conj A) \cong \hom(1\otimes\conj{A}, \conj{A}) \cong \hom(1, \conj{A}\otimes A) \cong \mathbb C$ using, for example, \eqref{eq:frob_1r}.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
Let $A$ be simple.  The quantity $d_i(A) = \|\ev_A\| \|\coev_A\|$ is independent of the particular choice of (co)evaluation morphisms.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
By Proposition~\ref{prop:simple_passes_to_conj_etc}, there is $u\in\hom(1,A \otimes\conj A)$ which spans the whole hom space, and so $\coev_A = tu$ for some $t\in\mathbb C$.  Also there is $v\in\hom(1,\conj A \otimes A)$ spanning so $\ev_A^* = sv$ for some scalar $s$.  Let $\ev_A', \coev_A'$ also solve the conjugate equations, so ${\ev'}_A^* = s'v, \coev'_A = t'u$ for some $t', s'$.  However, then
\[ 1 = (1\otimes\ev_A)(\coev_A\otimes 1) = \overline{s} t (1\otimes v^*)(u\otimes 1), \]
and similarly $1 = \overline{s'}t' (1\otimes v^*)(u\otimes 1)$.  Hence $|st| = |s't'|$ and so $\|\ev_A\| \|\coev_A\| = \|\ev'_A\| \|\coev'_A\|$.
\end{proof}

For any $A$, obviously we can always scale $\ev_A$ by a non-zero $t\in\mathbb C$, and scale $\coev_A$ by $t^{-1}$, without changing the conjugate equations.  Hence we may always rescale to get $\|\ev_A\| = \|\coev_A\|$.

\begin{definition}
The \emph{intrinsic (or quantum) dimension} of a simple $A$ is $d_i(A)$.  For general $A \cong \bigoplus_\alpha A_\alpha$ with each $A_\alpha$ simple, define $d_i(A) = \sum_\alpha d_i(A_\alpha)$.
\end{definition}

We have $d_i(1)=1$.  In $\catHilbFD$ every object is a finite direct sum of copies of $\mathbb C$ and so $d_i(H) = \dim(H)$ the usual Hilbert space dimension.  We now spend some time exploring other characterisations of $d_i(A)$ for non-simple $A$.  Let $A \cong \bigoplus_\alpha A_\alpha$ with inclusion morphisms $u_\alpha \in \hom(A_\alpha, A)$.  By Proposition~\ref{prop:directsum_subobj_conj}, $\conj A \cong \bigoplus_\alpha \conj A_\alpha$; let $v_\alpha \in \hom(\conj A_\alpha, \conj A)$ be the inclusion morphisms.  Then one solution to the conjugate equations for $A$ is given by
\begin{equation}
  \label{eq:std_conj}
  \ev_A = \sum_\alpha \ev_{A_\alpha}(v_\alpha^*\otimes u_\alpha^*), \qquad
  \coev_A = \sum_\alpha (u_\alpha\otimes v_\alpha) \coev_{A_\alpha}.
\end{equation}

\begin{definition}
A \emph{standard} solution to the conjugate equations for $A$ is one of the form \eqref{eq:std_conj} with $\|\ev_{A_\alpha}\| = \|\coev_{A_\alpha}\| = d_i(A_\alpha)^{1/2}$ for each $\alpha$.
\end{definition}

We shall take a slightly different perspective to \cite{NT_CQGsBook} and \emph{define} a standard solution to be one which is unitarily equivalent to \eqref{eq:std_conj}.

Given a standard solution, we have
\[ \ev_A \ev_A^* = \sum_\alpha \ev_{A_\alpha} \ev_{A_\alpha}^* = \sum_\alpha \|\ev_{A_\alpha}\|^2 1 = \sum_\alpha d_i(A_\alpha) 1 = d_i(A) 1, \]
as in the expansion, $u_\alpha^* u_\beta = \delta_{\alpha,\beta}1$ etc.  So also $\|\ev_A\|^2 = d_i(A)$, and similarly $\| \coev_A \|^2 = d_i(A)$.  This remark suggests that it would be useful to have criteria for showing that a solution is standard, a task we now consider.

Given any solution $\ev_A, \coev_A$ to the conjugate equations, we define two linear functionals on $\hom(A)$ (compare the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:conj_implies_fd})
\begin{align*}
&\hom(A) \ni x \mapsto \varphi(x) : \quad
1 \xrightarrow{\ev_A^*} \conj A \otimes A \xrightarrow{1\otimes x} \conj A \otimes A \xrightarrow{\ev_A} 1,
\\
&\hom(A) \ni x \mapsto \psi(x) : \quad
1 \xrightarrow{\coev_A} A \otimes \conj{A} \xrightarrow{x\otimes 1} A \otimes \conj{A} \xrightarrow{\coev_A^*} 1.
\end{align*}

\begin{example}\label{eg:traces_hilb}
Consider Example~\ref{eg:hilbfd_rigid_diffmaps} of $\catHilbFD$, where $\ev_H, \coev_H$ are defined using an invertible $u\in\hom(H)$.  A calculation shows that $\varphi(x) = \tr(u^{-1}x(u^{-1*}))$ and $\psi(x) = \tr(u^*xu)$ for $x\in\hom(H)$, where $\tr$ is the usual trace on $\hom(H)$.
\end{example}

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:std_soln_iff_left_right_agree}
Let $\ev_A, \coev_A$ be solutions to the conjugate equations for $A$, and form $\varphi, \psi$ as above.  Then the solution is unitarily equivalent to a standard solution if and only if $\varphi = \psi$.  When this holds, $\varphi$ and $\psi$ are independent of the chosen standard solution, and $\varphi=\psi$ is a positive, faithful trace on $\hom(A)$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
If $A$ is simple, so $\hom(A)=\mathbb C1$, then $\varphi_A(1) = \ev_A \ev_A^* = \|\ev_A\|^2 1$ and $\psi_A(1) = \|\coev_A\|^2 1$, so as we choose $\|\ev_A\| = \|\coev_A\|$ we have $\psi_A = \varphi_A$.

Now let $A = \bigoplus_\alpha A_\alpha$ be a decomposition into simples, with inclusions $u_\alpha$ and $v_\alpha$ for $\conj A_\alpha$.  Then
\begin{align*}
\varphi(x) &= \sum_{\alpha,\beta} \ev_{A_\beta} (v_\beta^* \otimes u_\beta^*) (1\otimes x) (v_\alpha\otimes u_\alpha) \ev_{A_\alpha}^* \\
&= \sum_\alpha \ev_{A_\alpha} (1\otimes u_\alpha^* x u_\alpha) \ev_{A_\alpha}^*
= \sum_\alpha \varphi_{A_\alpha}( u_\alpha ^* x u_\alpha ), \\
\psi(x) &= \sum_{\alpha,\beta} \coev_{A_\alpha}^* (u_\alpha^*\otimes v_\alpha^*) (x\otimes 1) (u_\beta\otimes v_\beta) \coev_{A_\beta} \\
&= \sum_\alpha \coev_{A_\alpha}^* (u_\alpha^* x u_\alpha \otimes 1) \coev_{A_\alpha}
= \sum_\alpha \psi_{A_\alpha}( u_\alpha ^* x u_\alpha ).
\end{align*}
Hence these agree.  This will also clearly be true up to unitary equivalence.

Now let $A = \bigoplus_\alpha n(\alpha) A_\alpha$ with the $(A_\alpha)$ pariwise non-isomorphic.  Hence $\hom(A) \cong \bigoplus_\alpha \mathbb M_{n(\alpha)}$.  Let $x\in\hom(A)$, as a sum of matrices, be non-zero only in the $\alpha$ block.  Then, with obvious notation, $\varphi(x) = \sum_{i=1}^{n(\alpha)} \varphi_{A_\alpha}(u_i^* x u_i) = \Tr(x) \varphi_{A_\alpha}(1)$.  Thus $\varphi=\psi$ is a weighted sum of the usual traces on $\mathbb M_{n(\alpha)}$ and hence is tracial.

Now let $\conj A'$ be some conjugate to $A$, with morphisms $\ev'_A, \coev'_A$, with $\psi' = \varphi'$. By Proposition~\ref{prop:left_dual_unique} we have that $\conj A'$ is isomorphic to the canonical choice $\conj A = \bigoplus_\alpha \conj A_\alpha$, and so we may absorb this isomorphism into $\ev'_A, \coev'_A$ and suppose $\conj A'=\conj A$.  By Remark~\ref{rem:cstar_dual_case}, also $A$ is a conjugate to $\conj A$ with $\ev_{\conj A'} = \coev_A'^*, \coev_{\conj A'} = \ev_A'^*$, or with $\ev_{\conj A}=\coev_A^*, \coev_{\conj A}=\ev_A^*$.  So by Proposition~\ref{prop:left_dual_unique} again there is $t\colon A\to A$ invertible with $\ev_{\conj A'}(t\otimes 1) = \ev_{\conj A}$ and $(1\otimes t)\coev_{\conj A} = \coev_{\conj A'}$.  That is, $(t^*\otimes 1) \coev'_A = \coev_A$ and $\ev_A (1\otimes t^*) = \ev_A'$.  Then
\[ \varphi'(x) = \ev_A'(1\otimes x) \ev_A'^* = \varphi(t^*xt), \quad
\psi'(x) = \coev_A'^*(x\otimes 1)\coev_A'
= \psi(t^{-1}x t^{-1 *}). \]
As $\psi = \varphi$ is a trace and $\psi'=\varphi'$ we conclude that $\varphi(xtt^*) = \varphi(xt^{-1*}t^{-1}) = \varphi(x(tt^*)^{-1})$ for each $x\in\hom(A)$.  So $tt^* = (tt^*)^{-1}$ but $tt^*$ is positive, so $tt^*=1$ and $t$ is unitary (as $\hom(A)$ is finite-dimensional).  So $\ev'_A, \coev'_A$ are unitarily equivalent to a standard solution, and hence standard.
\end{proof}

\begin{example}
Continuing Example~\ref{eg:traces_hilb}, we see that $\varphi(x) = \psi(x)$ for all $x$ if and only $u$ is unitary (same argument as in the proof!) if and only if $\tr_H = \tr$ the usual trace.
\end{example}

\begin{definition}\label{defn:quantum_trace}
Given standard solutions to the conjugate equations for $A$, the trace $\psi_A = \varphi_A$ is the \emph{quantum trace} on $\hom(A)$, denoted $\tr_A$.
\end{definition}

In the monoidal category setting, this is a bit more complicated, see \cite[Section~4.7]{EGNO_TensorCategories}, for example.  (Here I am much less sure, so won't pursue things.)

Notice that $d_i(A) = \|\ev_A\|^2 = \tr_A(1)$ for any $A$, and so we have an intrinsic characterisation of the quantum dimension.

\begin{proposition}
For $i=1,2$, let $\conj A_i$ be a standard conjugate to $A_i$.  Then $\conj A_1 \oplus \conj A_2$ is a standard conjugate to $A_1\oplus A_2$, and $\conj A_2 \otimes \conj A_1$ is a standard conjugate to $A_1\otimes A_2$.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
By Proposition~\ref{prop:directsum_subobj_conj}, $\conj A_1 \oplus \conj A_2$ is a conjugate to $A_1\oplus A_2$.  Using the notation of that proposition, we have that
\begin{align*}
\varphi_{A_1\oplus A_2}(x)
&= \sum_{i,j} \ev_{A_i}(v_i^*\otimes u_i^*)(1\otimes x)(v_j\otimes u_j) \ev_{A_j}^*
= \sum_i \ev_{A_i} (1\otimes u_i^* x u_i) \ev_{A_j}^*
= \sum_i \varphi_{A_i}(u_i^* x u_i), \\
\psi_{A_1\oplus A_2}(x)
&= \sum_{i,j} \coev_{A_i}^*(u_i^*\otimes v_i^*)(x\otimes 1)(u_j\otimes v_j) \coev_{A_j}
= \sum_i \coev_{A_i}^*(u_i^* x u_i\otimes 1) \coev_{A_i} \\
&\qquad = \sum_i \psi_{A_i}(u_i^* x u_i).
\end{align*}
These agree as we have standard solutions for $A_i$, and hence by Theorem~\ref{thm:std_soln_iff_left_right_agree}, $\conj A_1 \oplus \conj A_2$ is a standard conjugate to $A_1\oplus A_2$.

Proposition~\ref{prop:tensor_conj} shows that $A\otimes B$ has conjugate $\conj B \otimes \conj A$.  Given $x\in\hom(A\otimes B)$, we have
\begin{align*}
\varphi_{A\otimes B}(x)
&= \ev_B (1\otimes\ev_A\otimes1)(1\otimes 1\otimes x)(1\otimes\ev_A^*\otimes1) \ev_B^* \\
&= \varphi_B( (\ev_A\otimes 1)(1\otimes x)(\ev_A^*\otimes 1) )
= \psi_B( (\ev_A\otimes 1)(1\otimes x)(\ev_A^*\otimes 1) ) \\
&= \coev_B^*( (\ev_A\otimes 1)(1\otimes x)(\ev_A^*\otimes 1) \otimes 1 ) \coev_B
= (\ev_A \otimes \coev_B^*) (1\otimes x\otimes 1)(\ev_A^* \otimes \coev_B), \\
\psi_{A\otimes B}(x)
&= \coev_A^*(1\otimes\coev_B^*\otimes 1)(x\otimes 1\otimes 1) (1\otimes\coev_B\otimes 1) \coev_A \\
&= \psi_A( (1\otimes\coev_B^*)(x\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_B) )
= \varphi_A( (1\otimes\coev_B^*)(x\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_B) ) \\
&= \ev_A( 1\otimes (1\otimes\coev_B^*)(x\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_B) )\ev_A^*
= (\ev_A \otimes \coev_B^*) (1\otimes x\otimes 1)(\ev_A^* \otimes \coev_B).
\end{align*}
Again, these agree.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}\label{corr:cat_trace_properties}
Given $x\in\hom(A,B), y\in\hom(B,A)$ we have $\tr_A(yx) = \tr_B(xy)$.  Given $x\in\hom(A), y\in\hom(B)$ we have $\tr_{A\otimes B}(x\otimes y) = \tr_A(x) \tr_B(y)$.  Hence $d_i(A\otimes B) = d_i(A) d_i(B)$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Given $x\in\hom(A,B), y\in\hom(B,A)$ let $C = A\oplus B$ with inclusions $u_A, u_B$.  Then $x_0 = u_B x u_A^*, y_0 = u_A y u_B^* \in \hom(C)$ satisfy $y_0 x_0 = u_A yx u_A^*, x_0 y_0 = u_B xy u_B^*$.  By construction of the standard solution for $C = A \oplus B$ clearly we have $\tr_C(c) = \tr_A(u_A^* c u_A) + \tr_B(u_B^* c u_B)$.  So $\tr_B(xy) = \tr_C(x_0y_0) = \tr_C(y_0x_0) = \tr_A(yx)$ and we've shown the first claim.

From the calculation above, for $x\in\hom(A), y\in\hom(B)$ we see that
\[ \tr_{A\otimes B}(x\otimes y) = (\ev_A \otimes \coev_B^*) (1\otimes x\otimes y\otimes 1)(\ev_A^* \otimes \coev_B)
= \varphi_A(x) \psi_B(y) = \tr_A(x) \tr_B(y), \]
and so in particular, $d_i(A\otimes B) = \Tr_{A\otimes B}(1\otimes1) = d_i(A) d_i(B)$.
\end{proof}

We finally give an analytic characterisation.

\begin{proposition}
Any $A\in\mc A$ we have $d_i(A) = \inf \|\ev_A\| \|\coev_A\|$ with the infimum taken over all solutions to the conjugate equations.  The minimum is attained exactly for scalar multiples of the standard solution.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Let $\ev_A, \coev_A$ be a standard solution, so $\|\ev_A\| = \|\coev_A\| = d_i(A)^{1/2}$.  When $\ev'_A, \coev'_A$ is another solution, then as in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:std_soln_iff_left_right_agree}, there is $t\in\hom(A)$ invertible with $\coev'_A = (t^{-1}\otimes 1)\coev_A$ and $\ev'_A = \ev_A(1\otimes t)$.  Then $\ev'_A \ev_A'^* = \tr_A(tt^*)$ while $\coev_A'^* \coev_A' = \tr_A(t^{-1*}t^{-1}) = \tr_A((tt^*)^{-1})$.  Turn $\hom(A)$ into a pre-Hilbert space for the inner product $(x|y) = \tr_A(x^*y)$, so Cauchy--Schwarz shows that $|\tr_A(x^*y)|^2 \leq \tr_A(x^*x) \tr_A(y^*y)$ with equality only when $x,y$ are linear dependent.  In particular,
\[ d_i(A)^2 = \tr_A(1)^2 = \tr_A(tt^{-1})^2 \leq \tr_A(tt^*) \tr_A(t^{-1*}t^{-1})
= \|\ev'_A\|^2 \|\coev'_A\|^2, \]
with equality only when there is $\lambda\in\mathbb C$ with $t^* = \lambda t^{-1}$.  In this latter case, $t^*t = \lambda t^{-1}t = \lambda 1$ so $\lambda > 0$, and $s = \lambda^{-1/2}t$ satisfies $s^*s = \lambda^{-1} t^*t = 1$ and $ss^* = 1$, so $s$ is unitary, and $\ev'_A = \lambda^{1/2} \ev_A(1\otimes s), \coev'_A = (\lambda^{-1/2})(1\otimes s^*)\coev_A$ are scalar multiples of a standard solution.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}
Let $F \colon \mc A \to \mc B$ be a unitary monoidal functor, where both $\mc A,\mc B$ has conjugates.  Then $d_i(F(A)) \leq d_i(A)$ for each $A\in\mc A$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
By Proposition~\ref{prop:func_preserves_dual}, $F(\conj A)$ is a conjugate for $F(A)$.  Picking a standard solution for $A$, we have, by the form of $\ev_{F(A)}, \coev_{F(A)}$ given in Proposition~\ref{prop:func_preserves_dual}, that $d_i(F(A)) \leq \| F(\ev_A) \| \| F(\coev_A) \| \leq \| \ev_A \| \|\coev_A\| = d_i(A)$.
\end{proof}

%As remarked at the start of this section, the conjugate $\conj A$ is both a left and right dual to $A$.
%As in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:std_soln_iff_left_right_agree}, also $A$ is a conjugate to $\conj A$, if we make the choices that $\ev_{\conj A} = \coev_A^*$ and $\coev_{\conj A} = \ev_A^*$.
Let's now recall the notion of a dual morphism, Proposition~\ref{prop:dual_morphism_properties}: given $x\colon A \to B$ we have $x^\sharp \colon \conj B \to \conj A$ defined by $x^\sharp = (\ev_B\otimes 1)(1\otimes x\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_A)$.  
Note that here we are using a different convention to \cite[Theorem~2.2.21]{NT_CQGsBook}, but in the case we're interested in (when every conjugate is standard), the result is the same.

We used the following morphisms in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:std_soln_iff_left_right_agree}.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:conjconj}
Let $\conj A$ be a standard conjugate to $A$.  Then $A$ is a standard conjugate to $\conj A$ for the morphisms $\ev_{\conj A} = \coev_A^*$ and $\coev_{\conj A} = \ev_A^*$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
As $x\mapsto x^\sharp$ is a bijection, Proposition~\ref{prop:dual_morphism_properties}, it suffices to show that $\varphi_{\conj A}(x^\sharp) = \psi_{\conj A}(x^\sharp)$ for each $x\in\hom(A)$.  However, we find that
\begin{align*}
\varphi_{\conj A}(x^\sharp)
&= \ev_{\conj A}(1\otimes x^\sharp) \ev_{\conj A}^*
= \coev_A^* (1\otimes\ev_A\otimes 1)(1\otimes 1\otimes x\otimes 1)(1\otimes 1\otimes\coev_A) \coev_A \\
&= \coev_A^* (1\otimes\ev_A\otimes 1)(\coev_A\otimes x\otimes 1)\coev_A
= \coev_A^*( x\otimes 1) \coev_A = \psi_A(x), \\
\psi_{\conj A}(x^\sharp)
&= \coev_{\conj A}^* (x^\sharp\otimes 1) \coev_{\conj A}
= \ev_A (\ev_A\otimes 1\otimes 1)(1\otimes x\otimes 1\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_A\otimes 1)\ev_A^* \\
&= \ev_A (1\otimes x)(1\otimes 1\otimes\ev_A)(1\otimes\coev_A\otimes 1)\ev_A^*
= \ev_A (1\otimes x)\ev_A^* = \varphi_A(x).
\end{align*}
As $\conj A$ is standard for $A$ we have that $\psi_A = \varphi_A$, and so  $\varphi_{\conj A}(x^\sharp) = \psi_{\conj A}(x^\sharp)$ as required.
\end{proof}

The following might look a little strange, but be careful.  For each $A$ we choose a conjugate $\conj A$.  In isolation, the lemma tells us that we \emph{could} chose $\conj{\conj A} = A$, but there is no a priori way to do this in a coherent way for all $A$.  The following is the standard category-theory solution: we have that $\conj{\conj A} \cong A$ naturally.

\begin{proposition}
For each $A$ let $\conj A$ be a standard conjugate.  The maps $A\mapsto\conj A; x\mapsto x^\sharp$ define a contravariant $*$-functor on $\mc A$ whose square is unitarily naturally isomorphic to the identity.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
By Proposition~\ref{prop:dual_morphism_properties} the functor is contravariant, so we need to check that it is a $*$-functor, namely that for $x\in\hom(A,B)$ we have $(x^*)^\sharp = (x^\sharp)^*$.  As $\tr_{\conj A}$ is a faithful positive trace it suffices to show that $\tr_{\conj A}((x^{*\sharp*}y) = \tr_{\conj A}(x^\sharp y)$ for each $y\in\hom(\conj A,\conj B)$.  By Lemma~\ref{lem:conjconj}, we take $A$ as a standard conjugate to $\conj A$, and so
\begin{align*}
\tr_{\conj A}((x^{*\sharp*}y)
% &= \psi_{\conj A}( (1\otimes\coev_B^*)(1\otimes x\otimes 1)(\ev_A^*\otimes 1) y ) \\
% &= \ev_A (1\otimes\coev_B^*\otimes 1)   (1\otimes x\otimes 1\otimes 1) (\ev_A^*\otimes 1\otimes 1) (y \otimes 1) \ev_A^* \\
&= \varphi_{\conj A}( (1\otimes\coev_B^*)(1\otimes x\otimes 1)(\ev_A^*\otimes 1) y ) \\
&= \coev_A^* (1\otimes1\otimes\coev_B^*) (1\otimes1\otimes x\otimes 1) (1\otimes\ev_A^*\otimes 1) (1\otimes y) \coev_A \\
&= \coev_B^*(x\otimes 1) (\coev_A^*\otimes 1\otimes 1)(1\otimes\ev_A^*\otimes 1) (1\otimes y) \coev_A \\
&= \coev_B^*(x\otimes y) \coev_A, \\
% \tr_{\conj A}(x^\sharp y)
% &= \psi_{\conj A}( (\ev_B\otimes 1)(1\otimes x\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_A) y ) \\
% &= \ev_A (\ev_B\otimes 1\otimes 1)(1\otimes x\otimes 1\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_A\otimes 1) (y\otimes 1) \ev_A^* \\
% &= \ev_B (1\otimes x) (1\otimes 1\otimes \ev_A) (1\otimes\coev_A\otimes 1) (y\otimes 1) \ev_A^* \\
% &= \ev_B (y\otimes x) \ev_A^*.
\tr_{\conj A}(x^\sharp y)
&= \tr_{\conj B}(y x^\sharp)
= \varphi_{\conj B}( y (\ev_B\otimes 1)(1\otimes x\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_A) ) \\
&= \coev_B^* (1\otimes y) (1\otimes\ev_B\otimes 1) (1\otimes1\otimes x\otimes 1)(1\otimes 1\otimes\coev_A) \coev_B \\
&= \coev_B^* (1\otimes y) (1\otimes\ev_B\otimes 1) (\coev_B\otimes 1\otimes 1) (x\otimes 1)\coev_A \\
&= \coev_B^* (x\otimes y) \coev_A,
\end{align*}
here using Corollary~\ref{corr:cat_trace_properties}.

For each $A$, by Lemma~\ref{lem:conjconj}, $A$ is a standard conjugate for $\conj A$, and so by Theorem~\ref{thm:std_soln_iff_left_right_agree}, there is a unitary $u_A \colon A \to \conj{\conj A}$ intertwining the solution, that is, $\ev_{\conj A}(u_A\otimes 1) = \coev_A^*$ and $(1\otimes u_A)\ev_A^* = \coev_{\conj A}$.  Then
\[ u_A = (\ev_{\conj A} \otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_{\conj A}) u_A
= (\ev_{\conj A} \otimes 1) (u_A\otimes 1\otimes 1) (1\otimes\coev_{\conj A})
= (\coev_A^*\otimes 1) (1\otimes\coev_{\conj A}). \]
We claim that $u$ gives a natural transformation, that is, for $x\colon A\to B$ we have $u_B x = x^{\sharp\sharp} u_A$.  As $(x^*)^\sharp = (x^\sharp)^*$ we have that $x^{\sharp\sharp} = x^{*\sharp * \sharp}$.  We have $x^{*\sharp} = (\ev_A\otimes 1)(1\otimes x^*\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_B)$, and
\begin{align*}
(1\otimes x^{*\sharp}) \coev_A
&= (1\otimes\ev_A\otimes 1)(1\otimes 1\otimes x^*\otimes 1)(\coev_A\otimes\coev_B)   \\
&= (1\otimes\ev_A\otimes 1)(\coev_A\otimes 1\otimes 1)(x^*\otimes 1)\coev_B
= (x^*\otimes 1)\coev_B.
\end{align*}
Set $y = x^{*\sharp}$, so using this relation twice, we have
\begin{align*}
x^{\sharp\sharp} u_A
&= y^{*\sharp} (\coev_A^*\otimes 1) (1\otimes\coev_{\conj A}) \\
&= (\coev_A^*\otimes 1) (1\otimes 1\otimes y^{*\sharp}) (1\otimes\coev_{\conj A})
= (\coev_A^*\otimes 1) (1\otimes y^*\otimes 1) (1\otimes \coev_{\conj B}) \\
&= ((1\otimes x^{*\sharp})\coev_A \otimes 1)^*(1\otimes \coev_{\conj B})
= ((x^*\otimes 1)\coev_B \otimes 1)^*(1\otimes \coev_{\conj B}) \\
&= (\coev_B^*\otimes 1) (x\otimes 1\otimes 1) (1\otimes \coev_{\conj B})
=  (\coev_B^*\otimes 1) (1\otimes \coev_{\conj B}) x
= u_B x.
\end{align*}
Thus $u$ is natural, as required.
\end{proof}




\section{Compact quantum groups}

We mostly just fix some notation.  We work with ``left'' conventions, which leads to some differences with \cite{NT_CQGsBook}.
Let $\G$ be a compact quantum group, with reduced $C^*$-algebra $C(\G)$ and Hopf $*$-algebra $\Pol(\G)$.  Let $h$ be the Haar state, so $h$ is faithful on $\Pol(\G)$ and $C(\G)$, and $(h\otimes\id)\Delta = (\id\otimes h)\Delta = h(\cdot) 1$.

Let $(A,\Delta)$ have reduced form $\G$.  A \emph{left corepresentation} is $U \in M(A \otimes \mc K(H))$ with $(\Delta\otimes\id)(U) = U_{13} U_{23}$.  A subspace $K$ of $H$ is invariant for $U$ when $(1\otimes e)U = U(1\otimes e)$ with $e$ the orthogonal projection of $H$ onto $K$.  $U$ is irreducible if it has no non-trivial invariant subspaces.  When $K$ is invariant, $U_e = (1\otimes e)U(1\otimes e)$ is a corepresentation, a sub-representation of $U$.

\begin{proposition}
Let $U$ be a unitary corepresentation on $H$, and let $e$ be the orthogonal projection on $H$.  Then $e(H)$ is invariant for $U$ if and only if $(1\otimes e) U (1\otimes e) = U (1\otimes e)$.  In this case, also $e(H)^\perp$ is invariant.
\end{proposition}

An \emph{intertwiner} between two corepresentations $U_1, U_2$ on $H_1, H_2$ is $t\colon H_1\to H_2$ with $(1\otimes t)U_1 = U_2(1\otimes t)$, interpretted suitably.  Write $t\in\mor(U_1, U_2)$.  Any invertible corepresentation is equivalent to a unitary corepresentation.  Any unitary corepresentation is equivalent to a direct sum of irreducible finite-dimensional unitary corepresentations.

The left-regular representation is $U \in M(A\otimes \mc K(L^2(\G)))$ defined by
\[ U^*(\xi\otimes \pi(a)\xi_0) = (\id\otimes\pi) \Delta(a)(\xi\otimes\xi_0) \qquad (a\in A, \xi\in K), \]
where $\pi\colon A\to\mc B(L^2(\G))$ is the GNS representation of the Haar state, and $\xi_0$ the cyclic vector.  Here $A$ acts (faithfully and non-degenerately) on some auxiliary Hilbert space $K$.  Any irreducible unitary corepresentation is a sub-corepresentation of $U$.

Let $\overline H$ be the conjugate Hilbert space.  For $x\colon H\to K$ defined $x^\top\overline\xi = \overline{x^*\xi}$ so $x^\top \colon \overline{K} \to \overline{H}$.  (Compare Example~\ref{eg:conj_Hilb} for links with rigid categories.)   When $H$ is finite-dimensional, there is an operation on $A \otimes \mc B(H)$ defined by $t^{\id\otimes\top} = a \otimes x^\top$ when $t = a\otimes x$ is an elementary tensor.  The \emph{contragradient} corepresentation to $U$ is $U^c = (U^{-1})^{\id\otimes\top}$.  When $U$ is unitary, take an orthonormal basis for $H$ and identify $A\otimes\mc B(H)$ with $\mathbb M_n(A)$ where $n = \dim H$.  We can then view $U$ as a matrix $(U_{ij})$.  Then $U^c$ has matrix $\overline{U} = (U_{ij}^*)$.  When $U$ is irreducible also $U^c$ is.  When $U$ is irreducible and unitary, $U^c$ is equivalent to a unitary corepresentation.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:int_to_dbl_conj}
Let $U$ be a finite-dimensional unitary corepresentation on $H$.  Then:
\begin{enumerate}[(1)]
  \item\label{prop:int_to_dbl_conj:1} $t\in\mor(U,U^{cc})$ if and only if $(U^c)^*(1\otimes t^\top)U^c = 1\otimes t^\top$, and $s\in\mor(U^{cc},U)$ if and only if $U^c(1\otimes s^\top)(U^c)^* = 1\otimes s^\top$;
  \item\label{prop:int_to_dbl_conj:2} let $t\in\mc B(\overline H)$ be positive and invertible.  Then $t\in\mor(U,U^{cc})$ if and only if $(1\otimes (t^\top)^{1/2}) U^c (1\otimes (t^\top)^{-1/2})$ is unitary.
\end{enumerate}
Now let $U$ be irreducible and set
\[ Q = (h\otimes\id)((U^c)^* U^c), \qquad P = (h\otimes\id)(U^c (U^c)^*), \]
which are positive operators with $\tr(P)=\tr(Q)=\dim(H)$.  Then:
\begin{enumerate}[resume]
\item $Q^\top \in \mor(U,U^{cc})$ and $P^\top\in\mor(U^{cc}, U)$;
\item $QP = \lambda 1$ for some $\lambda\not=0$, so $U$ is equivalent to $U^{cc}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}

We define the tensor product of two representations as $U \otimes V = U_{12} V_{13}$. 
One can show that $(U\otimes V)^c$ is equivalent to $V^c \otimes U^c$ for the intertwiner being the tensor swap map.

Let $U$ be irreducible unitary.  Then by Schur's Lemma, $\mor(U,U^{cc})$ is one-dimensional, spanned by $Q^\top$.  Set $\rho = \rho_U$ to be the unique scalar multiple of $Q^\top$ with $\tr(\rho) = \tr(\rho^{-1})$.

Let $\irr(\G)$ be the set of equivalence classes of unitary irreducible corepresentations of $\G$.  For each $\alpha\in\irr(\G)$ choose some representative $U_\alpha$ with matrix entries $(U^{(\alpha)}_{i,j})_{i,j=1}^{n(\alpha)}$ and operator $\rho_\alpha$.  We have the \emph{orthogonality relations}
\[ h(U^{(\alpha) *}_{i,j} U^{(\beta)}_{k,l}) = \delta_{\alpha, \beta} \delta_{j,l} (\rho_\alpha^{-1})_{k,i} \tr(\rho)^{-1},
\qquad
h(U^{(\alpha)}_{i,j} U^{(\beta) *}_{k,l}) = \delta_{\alpha, \beta} \delta_{i,k} (\rho_\alpha)_{l,j} \tr(\rho)^{-1}. \]

We can define $\rho_U$ for any finite-dimensional unitary $U$, either by decomposing $U$ as a sum of irreducibles and letting $\rho_U$ be the direct sum, or characterising $\rho_U$ as the unique positive invertible $\rho\in\mor(U,U^{cc})$ with $\tr(x\rho) = \tr(x\rho^{-1})$ for $x\in\mor(U,U)$.  Then
\[ U^{uc} = (1\otimes(\rho_U^\top)^{1/2}) U^c (1\otimes(\rho_U^\top)^{-1/2}) \in A\otimes\mc B(\overline{H_U}) \]
is unitary, the unitary conjugate to $U$.  One can show that $U^{uc} = U^{R\otimes\top}$ where $R$ is the unitary antipode.

%The $F$-matrices are defined by $F^{(\alpha)} = \tr(\rho_\alpha)^{-1} \rho_\alpha^{-1}$.  


\subsection{The representation category}

Let $\G$ be a compact quantum group.  Let $\Rep(\G)$ be the $C^*$-category whose objects are finite-dimensional unitary corepresentations of $\G$, and morphisms are intertwiners.  (So $\Rep(\G)$ is actually $W^*$.)  Denote by $1\in\Rep(\G)$ the trivial corepresentation.  We define a monoidal structure using the tensor product of representations, with the obvious choices for the associativity and unit morphisms.  Note that we shall never explicitly write the unit morphisms, and so $\rho_U$ will always be the positive operator studied in the previous section.

We see that the simple objects are the irreducibles, as $e(H)$ is invariant for $U$ if and only if $e\in\mor(U,U)$.

Given $U$ we define $\conj U$ to be $U^{uc}$ the unitary conjugate, which is a unitary corepresentation equivalent to $U^c$.  We now define (co)evaluation maps.  A morphism $t\in\mor(1,V)$ is identified with a vector $\xi \in H_V$ which is \emph{invariant} for $V$, meaning $V(\cdot\otimes\xi) = \cdot\otimes\xi$.  If we pick an orthonormal basis for $H_V$ this is equivalent to $\sum_j V_{i,j} \xi_j = \xi_i 1$ for each $i$.  Let $(e_i)$ be a basis for $H_U$ and define
\[ \coev_U = \sum_{i,j} (\rho_U^{1/2})_{i,j} e_i \otimes \overline{e_j} \in H_U \otimes \overline{H_U} = H_{U\otimes\conj U}. \]
This is invariant:
\begin{align*}
(U\otimes \conj U)(\cdot\otimes\coev_U)
&= \sum_{i,k,j,l} U_{i,j} U^{uc}_{k,l}(\coev_U)_{j,l} \otimes e_i \otimes \overline{e_k} \\
&= \sum_{i,k,j,l} U_{i,j} \Bigl( \sum_{s,t} (\rho_U^{\top 1/2})_{k,s} U^*_{s,t} (\rho_U^{-1/2\top})_{t,l} \Bigr)  (\rho_U^{1/2})_{j,l} \otimes e_i \otimes \overline{e_k} \\
&= \sum_{i,k,j,s} U_{i,j} (\rho_U^{1/2})_{s,k} (U^*)_{j,s} \otimes e_i \otimes \overline{e_k} \\
&= \sum_{i,k} 1 (\rho_U^{1/2})_{i,k} \otimes e_i \otimes \overline{e_k} = \cdot\otimes\coev_U.
\end{align*}
Similarly define $\ev_U$ by
\[ \ev_U^* = \sum_{i,j} (\rho_U^{-1/2})_{j,i} \overline{e_i} \otimes e_j \in \overline{H_U}\otimes H_U = H_{\conj U\otimes U}. \]
As $\rho_U \in \mor(U,U^{cc})$ we have that $\rho_U^{-1} \in \mor(U^{cc},U)$ so by Proposition~\ref{prop:int_to_dbl_conj}\ref{prop:int_to_dbl_conj:2}, $U^c (1\otimes\rho_U^{\top-1}) (U^c)^* = 1\otimes\rho_U^{\top-1}$ which is equivalent to
\[ (\rho_U^{-1})_{j,i} 1
= \sum_{s,t} (U^c)_{i,s} (\rho_U^{\top-1})_{s,t} ((U^c)^*)_{t,j}
= \sum_{s,t} U^*_{i,s} (\rho_U^{-1})_{t,s} U_{j,t} \]
for each $i,j$.  Using this, we can show that $\ev_U^*$ is invariant:
\begin{align*}
(\conj U \otimes U)(\cdot \otimes \ev_U^*)
&= \sum_{i,j,k,l} U^{uc}_{i,j} U_{k,l} (\ev^*_U)_{j,l} \otimes \overline{e_i} \otimes e_k \\
&= \sum_{i,j,k,l,s,t} (\rho_U^{\top 1/2})_{i,s} U^*_{s,t} (\rho_U^{-1/2\top})_{t,j}  U_{k,l} (\rho_U^{-1/2})_{l,j} \otimes \overline{e_i} \otimes e_k \\
&= \sum_{i,k,l,s,t} (\rho_U^{\top 1/2})_{i,s} U^*_{s,t} (\rho_U^{-1})_{l,t} U_{k,l} \otimes \overline{e_i} \otimes e_k \\
&= \sum_{i,k,s} (\rho_U^{\top 1/2})_{i,s} (\rho_U^{-1})_{k,s}1 \otimes \overline{e_i} \otimes e_k  \\
&= \sum_{i,k} (\rho_U^{-1/2})_{k,i}1 \otimes \overline{e_i} \otimes e_k
= \cdot \otimes \ev_U^*.
\end{align*}
Notice that $\ev_U(\overline{e_i} \otimes e_j) = (\rho_U^{-1/2})_{i,j}$ as $\rho_U = \rho_U^*$.
We now verify the conjugate equations:
\begin{align*}
(\ev_U \otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_U)\overline{e_k}
&= \sum_{i,j} \ev_U(\overline{e_k}\otimes e_i) (\rho_U^{1/2})_{i,j} \overline{e_j}
= \sum_{i,j} (\rho_U^{-1/2})_{k,i}(\rho_U^{1/2})_{i,j} \overline{e_j}
= \overline{e_k}, \\
(1\otimes\ev_U)(\coev_U\otimes 1)e_k
&= \sum_{i,j} (\rho_U^{1/2})_{i,j} \ev_U(\overline{e_j}\otimes e_k) e_i
= \sum_{i,j} (\rho_U^{1/2})_{i,j} (\rho_U^{-1/2})_{j,k} e_i
= e_k.
\end{align*}

The associated linear functionals on $\hom(U)$ are
\begin{align*}
\varphi_U(x) &= \ev_U(1\otimes x)\ev_U^* = \sum_{i,j} (\rho_U^{-1/2})_{j,i}\ev_U(\overline{e_i}\otimes xe_j)
= \sum_{i,j,k} x_{k,j} (\rho_U^{-1/2})_{j,i}\ev_U(\overline{e_i}\otimes e_k) \\
&= \sum_{i,j,k} x_{k,j} (\rho_U^{-1/2})_{j,i} (\rho_U^{-1/2})_{i,k}
= \sum_{j,k} x_{k,j} (\rho_U^{-1})_{j,k}
= \tr(\rho_U^{-1}x), \\
\psi_U(x) &= \coev_U^*(x\otimes 1)\coev_U = \sum_{i,j,k} x_{k,i} (\rho_U^{1/2})_{i,j}\coev_U^*(e_k \otimes \overline{e_j})
= \sum_{i,j,k} x_{k,i} (\rho_U^{1/2})_{i,j} \overline{ (\rho_U^{1/2})_{k,j} } \\
&= \sum_{i,j,k} x_{k,i} (\rho_U){i,k}
= \tr(\rho_U x).
\end{align*}
These agree by the second defining property of $\rho_U$, and so the solution to the conjugate equations is standard.

The intrinsic dimension is $d_i(U) = \tr_U(1) = \tr(\rho_U)$ which is the \emph{quantum dimension} from CQG theory.

There is an interesting interaction with the dual morphism notion.  For $x\in\mor(U,V)$ recall that $x^\sharp = (\ev_V\otimes 1)(1\otimes x\otimes 1)(1\otimes\coev_U) \colon \overline{H_V} \to \overline{H_U}$, so
\begin{align*}
x^\sharp (\overline{e_k})
&= \sum_{i,j} (\rho_U^{1/2})_{i,j} \ev_V(\overline{e_k} \otimes x(e_i)) \otimes \overline{e_j}
&= \sum_{i,j,l} (\rho_U^{1/2})_{i,j} (\rho_V^{-1/2})_{k,l} x_{l,i} \overline{e_j}
= \sum_j (\rho_V^{-1/2} x \rho_U^{1/2})_{k,j} \overline{e_j},
\end{align*}
or $x^\sharp = (\rho_V^{-1/2} x \rho_U^{1/2})^\top$.  I don't quite follow \cite[Remark~2.2.23]{NT_CQGsBook} here, but I think the point is that this formula makes sense for any $x\colon H_U \to H_V$ not just an intertwiner.  Then given $\omega\in L^1(\G)$ we can consider $x = (\omega\otimes\id)(U) \in \mc B(H_U)$.  TODO: $\hh S$ in some form\ldots



\subsection{Discrete quantum groups}

Let $W \in M(C(\G) \otimes \mc K(L^2(\G)))$ be the left-regular representation for the reduced compact quantum group algebra $C(\G)$.  Then
\[ W = \sum_{\alpha\in\irr(\G)} U^\alpha_{i,j} \otimes e^{(\alpha)}_{i,j}, \]
etc etc

Given $\hh\omega \in \ell^1(\hh\G)$, we consider $\hh\lambda(\hh\omega) = (\hh\omega\otimes\id)(\hh W) = (\id\otimes\hh\omega)(W^*) \in C(\G)$.





\subsection{The fiber functor}

TODO: Move this below

Let $F \colon \Rep(\G) \to \catHilbFD$ be the forgetful functor, $F(U) = H_U$ and $F(x)=x$ for morphisms.  This is a faithful $*$-functor, and is clearly a tensor for the identity $J$.



\section{Reconstruction Theorems}

\subsection{Fiber functors}

Let $\mc A$ be a $C^*$-tensor category.  A \emph{fiber functor} is a tensor functor $\mc A \to \catHilbFD$ which is exact and faithful (injective on morphism spaces).  It is unclear to me exactly what ``exact'' means for a general $C^*$-category, as such a category might not be abelian.  Let's take a detour and think about this briefly, before returning to main situation.

For (Banach) Hilbert spaces, we surely mean by ``short exact sequence''
\begin{equation}\notag%\label{eq:general_SES}
\begin{tikzcd}
  0 \ar[r] &
  H \ar[r, "\iota"] &
  K \ar[r, "q"] &
  L \ar[r] &
  0
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
where $0 = \{0\}$ is the zero Hilbert space, and the kernel of each arrow equals the image of the previous one, see \cite[Chapter~2]{CC_Homo_Methods_BanSp} for example.  This means that $\iota$ is injective, $q$ is surjective, and $\ker(q) = \im(\iota)$.  So by the Open Mapping Theorem, $\iota$ is bounded below with (closed) image exactly the kernel of $q$.

\begin{proposition}
In $\catHilb$ consider a diagram
\begin{equation}\notag%\label{eq:general_SES}
\begin{tikzcd}
  0 \ar[r] &
  H \ar[r, "x"] &
  K \ar[r, "y"] &
  L \ar[r] &
  0
\end{tikzcd}
\end{equation}
This is exact if and only if:
\begin{enumerate}[(1)]
  \item $|x| \in \hom(H)$ and $|y^*|\in\hom(K)$ are invertible;
  \item with $u = x |x|^{-1} \in \hom(H,K), v = |y^*|^{-1}y \in \hom(K,L)$, we have $uu^* + v^*v = 1$
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Suppose we have a short exact sequence, so $x$ is an isomorphism onto its range and hence bounded below: there is $\epsilon>0$ with $\|x\xi\| \geq \epsilon \|\xi\|$ for each $\xi\in H$, so $(\xi|x^*x\xi) = \|x\xi\|^2 \geq \epsilon^2 \|\xi\| = \epsilon^2 (\xi|\xi)$ and hence $|x|^2 = x^*x \geq \epsilon^2 1$.  So also $|x| \geq \epsilon 1$ and $|x|$ is invertible.  Then $u^*u = |x|^{-1} x^*x |x|^{-1} = 1_H$ so $u$ is an isometry with $\im(u) = \im(x)$, hence $uu^*$ is the projection onto $\im(x)$.

As $K = \ker(y) \oplus \ker(y)^\perp$ we see that $y_0 \colon \ker(y)^\perp \to L$, the restriction of $y$, is bijective and bounded, and so an isomorphism.  For $\xi\in L$ we have
\begin{align*}
\|y^*(\xi)\| &= \sup\{ |(y\eta|\xi)| : \eta\in K, \|\eta\|\leq 1\}
= \sup\{ |(y\eta|\xi)| : \eta\in\ker(y)^\perp, \|\eta\|\leq 1\} \\
&= \sup\{ |(y_0\eta|\xi)| : \eta\in\ker(y)^\perp, \|\eta\|\leq 1\}
= \|y_0^*\xi\|,
\end{align*}
and so $\|\xi\| = \|y_0^{-1 *} y_0^* \xi\| \leq \|y_0^{-1}\| \|y^*(\xi)\|$ and hence $y^*$ is bounded below, so an isomorphism onto its range.  Hence by the same reasoning, $|y^*|$ is invertible, and $v^* = y^*|y^*|^{-1} \colon L \to K$ is an isometry, with $v^*v$ the projection onto $\im(y^*) = \ker(y)^\perp$.
As $K = \ker(y) \oplus \ker(y)^\perp = \im(x) \oplus \ker(y)^\perp = \im(uu^*) \oplus \im(v^*v)$ we have shown both conditions.

Conversely, $|x|$ invertible means $|x| \geq \epsilon 1$ for some $\epsilon>0$ and so reversing the argument above shows that $x$ is bounded below.  Similarly $y^*$ is bounded below.  Again $u^*u = 1_H$ and $uu^*$ is the projection onto $\im(x)$, while $vv^*=1_L$ with $v^*v$ the projection onto $\im(y^*) = \ker(y)^\perp$.  By hypothesis these projections sum to $1$ so $L = \im(x) \oplus \ker(y)^\perp$ and hence $\ker(y) = \im(x)$, so the sequence is exact.
\end{proof}

I have no idea if the above is standard, known but non-standard, or an invention (though surely not completely original).  Anyway, the point is that the two conditions make sense in any $C^*$-category, and so might be what we take to mean a \emph{short exact sequence} in general.  If $F \colon \mc A \to \catHilb$ is a functor then $F\colon \hom(A) \to \hom(F(A))$ is a $*$-homomorphism, so by uniqueness of positive square roots, $F(|x|) = |F(x)|$ is invertible, also $|F(y)^*|$, and $F(u) = F(x) F(|x|^{-1}) = F(x) |F(x)|^{-1}$ and the same for $v$, so the image will also be short exact.  This unfortunately is probably not what we want!


\subsection{Reconstruction theorems}

We now always let $\mc A$ be a rigid $C^*$-tensor category.  As explained in \cite[Section~2.3]{NT_CQGsBook} the ``exact'' requirement is redundant, and so a \emph{fiber functor} may be defined as a faithful tensor functor (Definition~\ref{defn:monoidal_functor}) $F \colon \mc A \to \catHilbFD$.

\begin{lemma}
A tensor functor $F \colon \mc A \to \catHilbFD$ is always faithful.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
As every object is a finite direct sum of simple objects, $F$ is faithful if and only if $F(A)\not=0$ for each simple $A$.  As $\mc A$ has conjugates, by Proposition~\ref{prop:func_preserves_dual}, $F(A)$ has a conjugate, namely $F(\conj A)$.  For a simple $A$, we have that $F(\coev_A) \colon \mathbb C \cong F(1)\to F(A\otimes\conj A) \cong F(A) \otimes F(\conj A)$ is non-zero (again, Proposition~\ref{prop:func_preserves_dual}) and so $F(A)\not=0$.
\end{proof}

Let $(U_\alpha)_{\alpha\in I}$ be a set of representatives of equivalence classes of simple objects.  It is helpful to have some notation for describing how $A\in\mc A$ decomposes into a sum of simples: let $a(\alpha)$ be the number of times $U_\alpha$ appears in the decomposition of $A$, so $a(\alpha)=0$ for all but finitely many $\alpha$, and $\hom(A) \cong \bigoplus \mathbb M_{a(\alpha)}$.  We have isometries $u_{\alpha,i} \colon U_\alpha \to A$ for $1\leq i\leq a(\alpha)$, with $\sum_{\alpha, i} u_{\alpha,i} u_{\alpha,i}^* = 1_A$.  Similarly for $B\in\mc A$ and $v_{\alpha,i}$.  A morphism $x \colon A \to B$ is associated to matrix decomposition $(x_\alpha)$ where $x_\alpha \in \mathbb M_{b(\alpha), a(\alpha)}$ has entries $x^{(\alpha)}_{i,j} = v_{\alpha,i}^* x u_{\alpha,j} \in \hom(U_\alpha) \cong \mathbb C$.  We reconstruct $x$ as
\[ x = 1_B x 1_A
= \sum_{\beta,j} v_{\beta,j} v_{\beta,j}^* x \sum_{\alpha, i} u_{\alpha,i} u_{\alpha,i}^*
= \sum_{\alpha,i,j} v_{\alpha,j} \bigl( v_{\alpha,j}^* x u_{\alpha,i} \bigr) u_{\alpha,i}^*
= \sum_{\alpha,i,j} v_{\alpha,i} x^{(\alpha)}_{i,j} u_{\alpha,j}^*, \]
because for $\alpha\not=\beta$ we have $v_{\beta,j}^* x u_{\alpha,i} \in \hom(U_\alpha, U_\beta) = \{0\}$.

We can infact describe functors $F\colon \mc A \to \catHilbFD$ quite concretely.  In notation which is not standard let $F_\alpha = F(U_\alpha) \in \catHilbFD$ and $n_\alpha = \dim F_\alpha$.  We claim that up to unitary natural isomorphism, $F$ is determined by the numbers $(n_\alpha)$.  Let $F'$ be another functor with $n_\alpha = \dim F'(U_\alpha)$ for each $\alpha$.  Then for each $\alpha$ we can find a unitary $\eta_\alpha \colon F(U_\alpha) \to F'(U_\alpha)$.  For $A\in\mc A$ choose $u_{\alpha,i}$ as above and define
\[ \eta_A = \sum_{\alpha,i} F'(u_{\alpha,i}) \eta_\alpha F(u_{\alpha,i}^*) \colon F(A) \to F'(A). \]
Then $\eta_A^*\eta_A = \sum_{\alpha,i} F(u_{\alpha,i}) \eta_\alpha^* F'(1) \eta_\alpha F(u^*_{\alpha,i}) = \sum_{\alpha,i} F(u_{\alpha,i}u_{\alpha,i}^*) = F(1) = 1$ and similarly $\eta_A \eta_A^*=1$, so $\eta$ is unitary.  We claim that $\eta \colon F \Rightarrow F'$.  For $x \colon A\to B$ we have
\begin{align*}
\eta_B F(x)
&= \sum F'(v_{\beta,k}) \eta_\beta F(v_{\beta,k}^*) F(v_{\alpha,i}) x^{(\alpha)}_{i,j} F(u^*_{\alpha,j})
= \sum F'(v_{\alpha,i}) \eta_\alpha x^{(\alpha)}_{i,j} F(u^*_{\alpha,j}), \\
F'(x) \eta_A
&= \sum F'(v_{\alpha,i}) x^{(\alpha)}_{i,j} F'(u^*_{\alpha,j}) F'(u_{\beta,k}) \eta_\beta F(u_{\beta,k}^*)
= \sum F'(v_{\alpha,i}) x^{(\alpha)}_{i,j} \eta_\alpha F(u_{\alpha,j}^*),
\end{align*}
and these agree as required.

\appendix
\section{Basic category theory}\label{sec:cat}

We follow for example \cite{Leinster_BasicCatTheory}.

\begin{definition}\label{defn:natural_transformation}
A \emph{natural transformation} between two functors $F,G \colon \mc A \to \mc B$, written $\alpha \colon F \Rightarrow G$, is a collection of morphisms $\alpha_A \colon F(A) \to G(A)$, for each $A\in\mc A$, such that the following diagrams all commute:
\[ \begin{tikzcd}
  A \arrow[d, "x"] &&  F(A) \arrow[r, "\alpha_A"] \arrow[d, "F(x)"'] & G(A) \arrow[d, "G(x)"] \\
  B &&  F(B) \arrow[r, "\alpha_B"'] & G(B).
\end{tikzcd} \]
\end{definition}

\begin{example}
Let $A$ be an algebra, and consider this as a one-object category.  Then $\hom(A)$ satisfies axiom \ref{cstar-cat-axioms:1}, as $\hom(A)$ is just the algebra $A$.  Let $\catVect$ be the category of vector spaces and linear maps, again this satisfies \ref{cstar-cat-axioms:1}.  A functor $F\colon A \to \catVect$ (assumed linear, of course) is given by a choice of vector space $E = F(A)$ and then an algebra homomorphism $A \to L(E); x \mapsto F(x)$.  This is the same as $E$ begin a left $A$-module.

Consider another functor $G$, say $F = G(A)$.  A natural transformation $\alpha \colon F \Rightarrow G$ is then a morphism $\alpha \colon E \to F$, i.e.\@ a linear map, such that $G(x)\alpha = \alpha F(x)$ for each $x\in A$.  This is the same as saying that $\alpha$ is a module homomorphism.
\end{example}

We now consider equivalences of categories, and more generally, adjoints.  An equivalence of categories is a pair of functors $F\colon\mc A\to\mc B, G \colon \mc B \to \mc A$ with natural isomorphisms $\epsilon \colon FG \Rightarrow 1_{\mc B}$ and $\eta \colon 1_{\mc A} \Rightarrow GF$.  For any given $F$ there can be many choices for $G,\epsilon,\eta$, but we do have the following.

\begin{theorem}\label{thm:cat_equiv_FFES}
Given a functor $F\colon\mc A\to\mc B$, we have that $F$ forms part of an equivalence if and only if $F$ is full and faithful (so for each $A,B\in\mc A$ the map $\mc A(A,B) \to \mc B(F(A),F(B))$ is a bijection) and essentially surjective on objects (for each $B\in\mc B$ there is $A\in\mc A$ with $F(A)$ isomorphic to $B$).
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
This is \cite[Proposition~1.3.18, Exercise~1.3.32]{Leinster_BasicCatTheory} for example; we give the details.  If $F$ forms part of an equivalence, then $\eta$ satisfies
\[ \begin{tikzcd}
  A \arrow[d, "x"] &&  A \arrow[r, "\eta_A"] \arrow[d, "x"'] & GF(A) \arrow[d, "GF(x)"] \\
  B &&  B \arrow[r, "\eta_B"'] & GF(B).
\end{tikzcd} \]
Let $x_1,x_2\in\mc A(A,B)$ with $F(x_1) = F(x_2)$.  Then $GF(x_1) = GF(x_2)$ and so $\eta_B \circ x_1 = GF(x_1) \circ \eta_B = GF(x_2) \circ \eta_B = \eta_B \circ x_2$.  As $\eta$ is an isomorphism, $x_1=x_2$ and so $F$ is faithful.  By symmetry (that is, using $\epsilon$) also $G$ is faithful.

Let $y \in \mc B(F(A), F(B))$ so $G(y) \in \mc A(GF(A), GF(B))$ and so $x = \eta_B^{-1} \circ G(y) \circ \eta_A \in \mc A(A,B)$.  By naturality, $GF(x) = \eta_B \circ x \circ \eta_A^{-1} = G(y)$ and as $G$ is faithful, we conclude that $F(x) = y$.  So $F$ is full, similarly, $G$ is full.
As $FG(B) \cong B$ (using $\epsilon$) for any $B$, we see that $F$ is essentially surjective.

Conversely, we start with $F$ and construct $G,\epsilon,\eta$.  As $F$ is essentially surjective, for each $B\in B$ there is $G(B)\in\mc A$ and an isomorphism $\epsilon_B \colon FG(B) \to B$.  Given $y \in \mc B(B_1,B_2)$, consider $\epsilon_{B_2}^{-1} \circ y \circ \epsilon_{B_1} \colon FG(B_1) \to FG(B_2)$ which equals $F(x)$ for some unique $x\in\mc A(G(B_1), G(B_2))$ as $F$ is full and faithful.  Define $G(y)$ to be this $x$, so by definition, $FG(y) = \epsilon_{B_2}^{-1} \circ y \circ \epsilon_{B_1}$, and from this it follows readily that $G$ is a functor.  Furthermore, we have constructed $\epsilon$ and $G$ so that $\epsilon \colon FG \Rightarrow 1$.

For each $A\in\mc A$, consider $\epsilon_{F(A)}^{-1} \colon F(A) \to FGF(A)$ so as $F$ is full and faithful, there is a unique $\eta_A \colon A \to GF(A)$ with $F(\eta_A) = \epsilon_{F(A)}^{-1}$.  Then $\eta_A$ must an isomorphism: the same argument shows that there is $f\colon GF(A) \to A$ with $F(f) = \epsilon_{F(A)}$, and then $1 = F(\eta_A \circ f)$ so $\eta_A\circ f=1$, and similarly $f\circ\eta_A=1$.

As $F$ is full and faithful, to show that $\eta \colon 1 \Rightarrow GF$ it suffices to show that for any $x\in\mc A(A,B)$ the left-hand diagram commutes, but by definition,
\[ \begin{tikzcd}
  F(A) \arrow[r, "F(\eta_A)"] \arrow[d, "F(x)"'] & FGF(A) \arrow[d, "FGF(x)"] \\
  F(B) \arrow[r, "F(\eta_B)"'] & FGF(B)
\end{tikzcd} 
\quad\iff\quad
\begin{tikzcd}
  F(A) \arrow[r, "\epsilon_{F(A)^{-1}}"] \arrow[d, "F(x)"'] & FGF(A) \arrow[d, "FGF(x)"] \\
  F(B) \arrow[r, "\epsilon_{F(B)^{-1}}"'] & FGF(B)
\end{tikzcd} 
\]
and the right-hand diagram holds because $\epsilon$ is a natural transformation.
\end{proof}

We now consider adjunctions, following \cite[Chapter~2]{Leinster_BasicCatTheory}.  An \emph{adjunction} is a pair of functors $F\colon\mc A\to\mc B, G\colon\mc B\to\mc A$ satisfying
\[ \mc A(A,G(B)) \cong \mc B(F(A),B), \]
naturally, for each $A,B$.  We write $F \dashv G$ and say $F$ is a left adjoint to $G$, and $G$ is a right adjoint to $F$.  (The left/right terminology may be remembered by looking at which side of the hom space the functor appears: $G$ is on the right of $\mc A(A,G(B))$ and $F$ on the left of $\mc B(F(A),B)$.)

By ``naturally'' we mean, by definition, the following.  For given $A,B$, denote the bijection $\mc A(A,G(B)) \to \mc B(F(A),B)$ by $x \mapsto x^\natural$, and similarly for $\mc B(F(A),B) \to \mc A(A,G(B)), y\mapsto y^\natural$.  These are mutual inverses, so $x^{\natural\natural} = x$ and so forth.  We then require that
\begin{equation} \big( F(A) \xrightarrow{x} B_1 \xrightarrow{y} B_2  \big)^\natural
\quad = \quad
A \xrightarrow{x^\natural} G(B_1) \xrightarrow{G(y)} G(B_2),
\label{eq:nat_im_adj1}
\end{equation}
and similarly
\begin{equation} \big( A_1 \xrightarrow{x} A_2 \xrightarrow{y} G(B) \big)^\natural
\quad = \quad
F(A_1) \xrightarrow{F(x)} F(A_2) \xrightarrow{y^\natural} B.
\label{eq:nat_im_adj2}
\end{equation}
Equivalently, for fixed $A$, we have two functors $\mc B \to \catSet$ given by $B \mapsto \mc A(A,G(B))$ and $B\mapsto \mc B(F(A),B)$, and we require these to be naturally isomorphic in the usual sense; and for fixed $B$ we have two functors $\mc A\to\catSet^\op$ given by $A\mapsto \mc A(A,G(B))$ and $A\mapsto \mc B(F(A),B)$, and we require also these to be naturally isomorphic.

From an adjunction $F \dashv G$ we construct natural isomorphisms $\eta \colon 1_{\mc A} \Rightarrow GF$ and $\epsilon \colon FG \Rightarrow 1_{\mc B}$, by $\eta_A = (1_{F(A)})^\natural$ and $\epsilon_B = (1_{G(B)})^\natural$.  These satisfy the \emph{triangle identities}
\begin{equation} \begin{tikzcd}
  F(A) \arrow[r, "F(\eta_A)"] \arrow[rd, "1_{F(A)}"']  &  FGF(A) \arrow[d, "\epsilon_{F(A)}"] \\
  &  F(A),
\end{tikzcd} 
\qquad\qquad
\begin{tikzcd}
  G(B) \arrow[r, "\eta_{G(B)}"] \arrow[rd, "1_{G(B)}"']  &  GFG(B) \arrow[d, "G(\epsilon_B)"] \\
  &  G(B).
\end{tikzcd} 
\label{eq:triangle_iden} \end{equation}
These follow from the definitions using \eqref{eq:nat_im_adj1} and \eqref{eq:nat_im_adj2}.
Furthermore, the isomorphisms between hom spaces are determined by
\begin{equation} y^\natural = G(y)\circ \eta_A \quad (y \colon F(A)\to B),
\qquad
x^\natural = \epsilon_B \circ F(x) \quad (x\colon A\to G(B)).
\label{eq:nat_from_ep_eta}
\end{equation}
Conversely, given two functors and natural transformations, we have an adjunction exactly when the triangle identities hold, the bijections being defined by \eqref{eq:nat_from_ep_eta}.

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:reflection_ff}
An adjoint $F \dashv G$ is a \emph{reflection} (meaning $G$ is full and faithful) if and only if $\epsilon$ is an isomorphism.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Let $F\dashv G$ be any adjunction.  By \eqref{eq:nat_im_adj1}, with $A=G(B)$, for $y\colon B_1\to B_2$ we have $(y\circ\epsilon_{B_1})^\natural = (y \circ (1_{G(B_1)})^\natural)^\natural = (1_{G(B_1)})^{\natural\natural} \circ G(y) = 1_{G(B_1)} \circ G(y) = G(y)$ and so
\[ G(y)^\natural = y \circ \epsilon_{B_1} \qquad (y\colon B_1 \to B_2). \]
Hence $G$ is full and faithful if and only if $y \mapsto G(y)^\natural$ is a bijection, if and only if $\mc 
B(B_1,B_2) \ni y \mapsto y \circ \epsilon_B \in \mc B(FG(B_1), B_2)$ is a bijection.

Suppose $G$ is full and faithful.  Given $B\in\mc B$, set $B_1=B, B_2=FG(B)$, so by the previous paragraph, there is $y\in\mc B(B_1, B_2)$ with $y\circ\epsilon_B = 1_{B_2} \in \mc B(B_2, B_2) = \mc B(FG(B_1), B_2)$.
The triangle identities \eqref{eq:triangle_iden} show that $G(\epsilon_B) \circ \eta_{G(B)} = 1_{G(B)}$, so as $G$ is full there is $x$ with $G(x) = \eta_{G(B)}$, and then $G(\epsilon_B \circ x) = G(1_B)$ so $\epsilon_B \circ x=1_B$ as $G$ is faithful.  So $\epsilon_B$ has both a left and a right inverse, and hence is an isomorphism.

Conversely, suppose that $\epsilon$ is a natural isomorphism.  When $G(y_1) = G(y_2)$ also $y_1 \circ \epsilon_B = G(y_1)^\natural = G(y_2)^\natural = y_2 \circ \epsilon_B$, so $y_1=y_2$ as $\epsilon_B$ is a isomorphism.  Given $x\colon G(B_1) \to G(B_2)$ set $y = x^\natural \circ \epsilon_{B_1}^{-1}$, so $G(y)^\natural = y \circ \epsilon_{B_1} = x^\natural$, hence $G(y) = x$.  So $G$ is full and faithful.
\end{proof}

The relation between equivalences and adjunctions is slightly complicated:

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:adjunctions_and_equivs}
Let $F\colon\mc A\to\mc B, G \colon \mc B \to \mc A$ be functors.  Then:
\begin{enumerate}
\item\label{prop:adjunctions_and_equivs:one}
  If $F,G$ forms an equivalence of categories, for natural isomorphisms $\epsilon\colon FG \Rightarrow 1, \eta \colon 1 \Rightarrow GF$, then there is a unique $\eta'$ such that $F \dashv G$ for $\epsilon,\eta'$, and there is a unique $\epsilon'$ such that $F \vdash G$ for $\eta,\epsilon'$.
\item\label{prop:adjunctions_and_equivs:two}
  If $F \dashv G$ with both $F,G$ full and faithful, then $F,G$ forms an equivalence of categories, for the given $\epsilon,\eta$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Suppose that $F \dashv G$ and $F,G$ are both full and faithful.  By Proposition~\ref{prop:reflection_ff}, $\epsilon$ is an isomorphism.  We now use the triangle identities \eqref{eq:triangle_iden} to see that $F(\eta_A) = \epsilon_{F(A)}^{-1}$.  As $F$ is full there is $x$ with $F(x) = \epsilon_{F(A)}$, so $F(x\circ \eta_A) = 1, F(\eta_A\circ x) = 1$, so as $F$ faithful, $\eta_A$ is an isomorphism.  So $\eta$ is a natural isomorphism, and $F,G$ form an equivalence (for these $\epsilon, \eta$).

Conversely, suppose $F, G$ form an equivalence of categories.  By definition, we have natural isomorphisms $\epsilon \colon FG \Rightarrow 1_{\mc B}$ and $\eta \colon 1_{\mc A} \Rightarrow GF$, but these need not satisfy the triangle identities.
%However, then $\epsilon^{-1} \colon 1_{\mc B} \to FG$ and $\eta^{-1} \colon GF \to 1_{\mc A}$ showing that we have symmetry between $F$ and $G$.  By Theorem~\ref{thm:cat_equiv_FFES}, both $F$ and $G$ are full and faithful.  FINISH!
Consider the construction given in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:cat_equiv_FFES}, but now start with the given $F,G,\epsilon$, and then use these to construct a possibly new $\eta$.  The proof still holds, showing that we obtain $\eta\colon 1 \Rightarrow GF$ a natural isomorphism, which is characterised by $F(\eta_A) = \epsilon_{F(A)}^{-1}$.  We verify the triangle identities for $\epsilon,\eta$; obviously $\epsilon_{F(A)} \circ F(\eta_A) = 1$ by construction of $\eta$.  As $F$ is faithful, for the second identity, it suffices to verify that
\[ \begin{tikzcd}
FG(B) \arrow[rr, "F(\eta_{G(B)}) = \epsilon_{FG(B)}^{-1}"] \arrow[rrd, "1_{FG(B)}"'] &&  FGFG(B)  \arrow[d, "FG(\epsilon_B)"]   \\
&&   FG(B).
\end{tikzcd} \]
That is, we want $FG(\epsilon_B) = \epsilon_{FG(B)}$.  However, using the $\epsilon = FG \Rightarrow 1$ is natural, applied to the morphism $x = \epsilon_B \colon FG(B) \to B$ shows that the following commutes,
\[ \begin{tikzcd}
  FGFG(B) \arrow[r, "\epsilon_{FG(B)}"] \arrow[d, "FG(\epsilon_B)"'] &  FG(B)  \arrow[d, "\epsilon_B"]  \\
  FG(B) \arrow[r, "\epsilon_B"']  & B,
\end{tikzcd} \]
which gives exactly what we want, as $\epsilon_B$ is an isomorphism.  Notice that the first triangle identity holding means that $F(\eta_A) = \epsilon_{F(A)}$ is the only choice, and so $\eta$ is unique.  Now swap the roles of $F,G$ to obtain the second statement.
\end{proof}

Given $A\in\mc A$ and a functor $G \colon \mc B \to \mc A$, the category $(A\Rightarrow G)$ has as objects pairs $(h,B)$ where $B\in\mc B$ and $h\colon A\to G(B)$, and a morphism $(h,B) \to (h',B')$ is $y\colon B\to B'$ making the diagram commute:
\[\begin{tikzcd}
& A \arrow[ld, "h"'] \arrow[rd,"h'"] \\
G(B) \arrow[rr, "G(y)"'] && G(B'). \end{tikzcd}\]
For more, see \cite[Section~2.3]{Leinster_BasicCatTheory}.

\begin{lemma}
Given an adjunction and $A\in\mc A$, the unit map $\eta_A \colon A \to GF(A)$ is an initial object in $(A\Rightarrow G)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
We need to show that for any $(h,B)$ in $(A\Rightarrow G)$ there is a unique morphism $(\eta_A, F(A)) \to (h,B)$, that is, a unique $y \colon F(A) \to B$ with
\[\begin{tikzcd}
& A \arrow[ld, "\eta_A"'] \arrow[rd,"h"] \\
GF(A) \arrow[rr, "G(y)"'] && G(B). \end{tikzcd}\]
Motivated by \eqref{eq:nat_from_ep_eta}, we set $y = h^\natural$ as then $G(y) \circ \eta_A = y^\natural = h$, as required.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
Given functors $F\colon\mc A\to\mc B, G \colon \mc B \to \mc A$, when $\eta \colon 1 \Rightarrow GF$ is such that $(\eta_A, F(A))$ is initial in $(A\Rightarrow G)$ for each $A\in\mc A$, there is a unique $\epsilon \colon FG \Rightarrow 1$ giving an adjunction.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
For all the details, see \cite[Theorem~2.3.6]{Leinster_BasicCatTheory}; we just indicate how to define $\epsilon$.  Motivated by the previous proof, and that we set $\epsilon_B = (1_{G(B)})^\natural$ above, we make the following definition: given $B$, set $A=G(B)$ and apply the hypothesis to find a unique $\epsilon_B \colon F(A) = FG(B) \to B$ with
\[\begin{tikzcd}
& A=G(B) \arrow[ld, "\eta_{G(B)}"'] \arrow[rd,"1"] \\
GFG(B) \arrow[rr, "G(\epsilon_B)"'] && G(B). \end{tikzcd}\]
This $\epsilon$ is as required.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}
Let $G\colon \mc B \to \mc A$.  There exists $F$ with $F \dashv G$ if and only if for each $A\in\mc A$, the category $(A\Rightarrow G)$ has an initial object.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
We give the construction; see \cite[Corollary~2.3.7]{Leinster_BasicCatTheory} for details.  For each $A\in\mc A$ let $\eta_A \colon A \to G(F(A))$ be an initial object of $(A\Rightarrow G)$ (that is, we choose some object $F(A)$ and some map $\eta_A$ with the correct properties).  Given $f\colon A_1\to A_2$, as $\eta_{A_1}$ is initial, there is a unique morphism from $\eta_A$ to $\eta_{A_2} \circ f$ in $(A\Rightarrow G)$, call this $F(f) \colon F(A_1) \to F(A_2)$.  This means we have a commutative diagram
\[ \begin{tikzcd}[column sep=large]
A_1 \arrow[d, "\eta_{A_1}"']     %\arrow[dr, "\eta_{A_2}\circ f"]   
    \arrow[r, "f"]  & A_2  \arrow[d, "\eta_{A_2}"]         \\
G(F(A_1))   \arrow[r, "G(F(f))"] &  G(F(A_2)).
\end{tikzcd} \]
Now check that $F$ is a functor and $\eta \colon 1 \Rightarrow GF$, so by the previous proposition, there is $\epsilon$ giving an adjunction.
\end{proof}



\section{Further results}

This appendix contains results and proofs which would break up the main exposition too much.


\subsection{Hilbert bimodules}\label{sec:bimod}

We follow the paper \cite{BMS_quasimults}.  A \emph{left Hilbert $C^*$-module} $E$ over a $C^*$-algebra $B$ is what we expect, say with $B$-valued inner-product denoted by $[x,y] \in B$ for $x,y\in E$, linear in the first variable.  A \emph{Hilbert $B$-$A$-bimodule} is a vector space which is a bimodule, a right Hilbert $C^*$-module over $A$, a left Hilbert $C^*$-module over $B$, with the compatibility that
\[ [x,y] \cdot z = x \cdot (y|z) \qquad (x,y,z\in E). \]
An example comes from taking a (right)  Hilbert $C^*$-module $E$ over $A$ and setting $B=\mc K_A(E)$ the ``compact'' operators, with $[x,y] = \theta_{x,y} \colon E \to E; z\mapsto x\cdot(y|z)$.  That all compact operators are adjointable shows that we have a bimodule (for example).  We'll see shortly that, in some sense, this is the only example.

Given a Hilbert $B$-$A$-bimodule $E$, let $\overline E = \{ \overline x : x\in E \}$ be the conjugate Banach space, made into a $A$-$B$-bimodule for the actions and inner-products
\[ a \cdot \overline x = \overline{x\cdot a^*}, \ 
\overline x\cdot b = \overline{b^*\cdot x}, \quad
[\overline x, \overline y] = (x|y), \ 
(\overline x|\overline y) = [x,y] \qquad (x,y\in E). \]
One can check that all the requirements are satisfied; for example $[\overline x, \overline y] \cdot \overline z = \overline{z\cdot(y|x)} = \overline{ [z,y]\cdot x } = \overline x \cdot (\overline y|\overline z)$ as $(x|y)^* = (y|x)$ and so forth.  Thus often one can prove results just about $A$, say, and then use this mechanism to translate to a result about $B$.

Denote by $I_A$ the closed linear span of $\{(x|y) : x,y\in E \}$, which is an ideal in $A$.  For $x\in E, a\in A$ we have
\[ (x-x\cdot a|x-x\cdot a) = (x|x) - a^*(x|x) - (x|x)a + a^*(x|x)a. \]
So if $a$ runs through an approximate identity for $I_A$ (or for $A$) we conclude that $x \in \overline\lin\{ x\cdot a : a\in I_A \}$.  If $a\in I_A$ is such that $x\cdot a=0$ for all $x\in E$, then $0 = (y\cdot a|x\cdot a) = a^*(y|x)a$ for all $x,y$, so $a^*ba=0$ for all $b\in I_A$, and hence $a^*a=0$ so $a=0$.  For $b\in B$ we have
\[ x \cdot (b\cdot y|z) = [x,b\cdot y]\cdot z = [x,y]b^* \cdot z = x \cdot (y|b^*\cdot z) \qquad (x,y,z\in E), \]
so $(b\cdot y|z) = (y|b^*\cdot z)$ for all $y,z\in E$, that is, we have a natural $*$-homomorphism $B \to \mc L_A(E)$.
Similar results apply for $B$ of course.

\begin{lemma}[{\cite[Proposition~1.10]{BMS_quasimults}}]
Let $E$ be a Hilbert $B$-$A$-bimodule.  Let $\lambda \colon B \to \mc L_A(E)$ be the left action of $B$.  Then $\lambda$ restricts to a $*$-isomorphism $I_B \cong \mc K_A(E)$.  Similarly $I_A$ is anti-$*$-isomorphic to $\mc K_B(E)$ for the restriction of $\mu \colon A \to \mc L_B(E)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
As above, $\lambda$ is a $*$-homomorphism, and is contractive, as $\|b\cdot x\|^2 = \|[b\cdot x , b\cdot x]\| = \|b[x,x]b^*\| \leq \|b\|^2 \|x\|^2$.  As $\lambda([x,y])(z) = [x,y]\cdot z = x\cdot(y|z) = \theta_{x,y}(z)$ for all $x,y,z\in E$, we see that $\lambda([x,y]) = \theta_{x,y}$ and so by continuity, we obtain a restriction $\lambda \colon I_B \to \mc K_A(E)$, which is seen to be a $*$-homomorphism.  It hence suffices to show $\lambda$ is injective on $I_B$, as then it is isometric, with dense range, and hence a $*$-isomorphism.  However, if $\lambda(b)=0$ for some $b\in I_B$, then $b\cdot x=0$ for all $x\in E$, and so $b=0$, by the remarks above.

We similarly have $\mu$ and restrict it to a bijection $I_A \to \mc K_B(E)$.  As $A$ acts on the right, $\mu$ is an anti-homomorphism.
\end{proof}

The previous lemma is proved in \cite{BMS_quasimults} using an approximate identity argument, but we find this misleading.  Here is a result about approximate identities.

\begin{lemma}
Let $(\theta_i)$ be an approximate identity in $\mc K_A(E)$.  Then $\theta_i(x) \to x$ in norm, for each $x\in E$.  So also if $(b_i)$ is an approximate identity in $I_B$, then $b_i \cdot x \to x$ in norm, for each $x\in E$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For $x,y \in E$ we have that $\theta_i \theta_{x,y} \to \theta_{x,y}$ in norm, in $\mc K_A(E)$.  So $\theta_{\theta_i(x), y} \to \theta_{x,y}$ so for each $z$ also $\theta_i(x) \cdot (y|z) \to x\cdot (y|z)$ in norm.  As $(\theta_i(x))$ is a bounded net in $E$, it follows that $\theta_i(x) \cdot a \to x\cdot a$ for each $a\in I_A$.  As $\theta_i(x) \cdot a = \theta_i(x\cdot a)$, we have $\theta_i(y)\to y$ for each $y\in\lin\{ x\cdot a : x\in E, a\in I_A \}$, but such $y$ are dense in $E$, so $\theta_i(y)\to y$ for each $y\in E$.

As $\lambda \colon I_B \to \mc K_A(E)$ satisfies $\lambda(b)(x) = b\cdot x$, given $(b_i)$ an approximate identity in $I_B$, set $\theta_i = \lambda(b_i) \in \mc K_A(E)$ for each $i$, so $b_i \cdot x = \theta_i(x) \to x$ for $x \in E$ by the previous paragraph.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Another, indeed perhaps better, proof of the previous result is to observe that we've already argued that $\lin\{ b\cdot x : b\in I_B, x\in E \}$ is dense in $E$.  A standard Banach algebraic (a three $\epsilon$ argument) then shows that $b_i \cdot x \to x$ for each $x$, and then use $\lambda^{-1}$ to obtain the claim about $\mc K_A(E)$.
\end{remark}

Let $\mc A$ be a $C^*$-category.  As in Remark~\ref{rem:to_modules}, $\mc A(A,B)$ is a Hilbert $C^*$-module over $\mc A(A)$.  However, it is also a Hilbert $\mc A(B)$-$\mc A(A)$-module.  This shows that, for example,
\[ \mc A(B) \supseteq \overline\lin\{ xy^* : x,y\in\mc A(A,B) \} \cong \mc K_{\mc A(A)}(\mc A(A,B)). \]
Hence it is wrong to think of $\mc A(A,B)$ as just a $\mc A(B)$-$\mc A(A)$-module in the algebraic sense: if $\mc A(A,B)$ is ``large'' then also $\mc K_{\mc A(A)}(\mc A(A,B))$ is ``large'', and so $\mc A(B)$ must be as well.  (We avoid writing ``$I_B$'' here, as there is very much a dependance on $A$.)   We also learn how approximate identities in $\mc A(A)$ and $\mc A(B)$ act on $\mc A(A,B)$, and so forth.


\subsection{Correspondances}

Let $A,B$ be $C^*$-algebras.  We recall the definition of a $B$-$A$-correspondance from Example~\ref{eg:tensor_bimods}.

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:bimodmaps_act_tensor}
Let $E$ be a Hilbert $C^*$-module over $B$, and let $F$ and $F'$ be a $B$-$A$-correspondance.  Given $t\in\mc L_A(F, F')$ which is also a $B$-module map, there is a bounded map $1\otimes t \colon E \otimes_B F \to E\otimes_B F'$ saisfying $(1\otimes t)(x\otimes y) = x\otimes t(y)$.  This gives a $*$-homomorphisms ${}_B \mc L_A(F) \to \mc L_A(E\otimes_B F)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Consider $\sum_{i=1}^n x_i \otimes y_i \in E \otimes F$.  By \cite[Lemma~4.2]{Lance_HilbModsBook}, the matrix $((x_i|x_j)) \in M_n(B)$ is positive, so there is $b=(b_{ij}) \in M_n(B)$ with $\sum_{k=1}^n b_{ki}^* b_{kj} = (x_i|x_j)$ for each $i,j$.  Set $z_k = \sum_i b_{ki} \cdot y_j \in F$ for each $z$.  Then
\begin{align*}
\Big\| \sum_i x_i \otimes t(y_i) \Big\|^2
&= \Big\| \sum_{i,j} (t(y_i) | (x_i|x_j) \cdot t(y_j)) \Big\|
= \Big\| \sum_{i,j} (t^*t(y_i) | (x_i|x_j) \cdot y_j) \Big\| \\
&= \Big\| \sum_{i,j,k} (t^*t(y_i) | b_{ki}^* b_{kj} \cdot y_j) \Big\|
= \Big\| \sum_{i,j,k} (b_{ki} \cdot t(y_i) | b_{kj} \cdot t(y_j)) \Big\|
= \Big\| \sum_{k} ( t(z_k) | t(z_k) ) \Big\|,
\end{align*}
using repeatedly that $t$ is a $B$-module map.  By \cite[Proposition~1.2]{Lance_HilbModsBook}, for $s\in\mc L_A(F)$ and $z\in F$ we have $(sz|sz) \leq \|s\|^2 (z|z)$ as an inequality in $A$.  As $t^*t$ is positive in $\mc L_A(F)$, see \cite[Lemma~4.1]{Lance_HilbModsBook}, there is $s\in\mc L_A(F)$ with $s^*s=t^*t$ (see also Example~\ref{eg:HilbMods}).  Thus $(tz|tz) = (z|t^*tz) = (z|s^*sz) = (sz|sz) \leq \|s\|^2 (z|z) = \|t\|^2 (z|z)$ for each $z\in F$, in particular for $z=z_k$ for any $k$.  Summing gives
\[ \sum_k  ( t(z_k) | t(z_k) )  \leq \|t\|^2 \sum_k (z_k|z_k)
\quad\implies\quad
\Big\|\sum_k  ( t(z_k) | t(z_k) )\Big\| \leq \|t\|^2 \Big\|\sum_k (z_k|z_k)\Big\|
= \|t\|^2 \Big\| \sum_i x_i \otimes y_i \Big\|^2. \]
So, firstly, $1\otimes t$ is well-defined (respects the quotienting operation), and secondly $1\otimes t$ is bounded.

Then $1\otimes t$ has adjoint $1\otimes t^*$ as
\[ (x\otimes y|x'\otimes t^*(y')) = (y|(x|x')\cdot t^*(y')) = (y|t^*((x|x')\cdot y'))
= (t(y)|(x|x')\cdot y') = (x\otimes t(y)|x'\otimes y'). \]
So $t\mapsto 1\otimes t$ maps into $\mc L_A(E\otimes_BF)$ and is a $*$-homomorphism.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}\label{lem:bimod_is_centre}
Under the isomorphism between $\mc L_A(A)$ and $M(A)$, the algebra of bimodule maps ${}_A\mc L_A(A)$ is isomorphic to $ZM(A)$ the centre of $M(A)$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $t\in\mc L(A)$ correspond to $x\in M(A)$.  That $t$ is a bimodule map means that $x(ab) = a(xb)$ for $a,b\in A$, equivalently, $(xa)b = (ax)b$ for each $a,b$ which in turn is equivalent to $xa=ax$ for $a\in A$.  So $x\in ZM(A)$ implies this, while conversely, for $y\in M(A)$ we have $(xy)a = x(ya) = (ya)x = y(ax) = y(xa) = (yx)a$ for all $a$, so $xy=yx$, so $x\in ZM(A)$.
\end{proof}
